```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates and Chaerephon arrive late to a rhetorical display by Gorgias at Callicles' house; Socrates declines a full display and requests a dialectical conversation instead.
textual_basis: Callicles offers his home for Socrates to hear Gorgias (447b). Socrates redirects from a display (epideixis) to dialogue (dialegesthai, 447c).
limits: The observation records the initial setting and Socrates' preference for conversation over display, not any claim about Gorgias' willingness or rhetorical quality.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Polus evades Chaerephon's question about what art Gorgias practices by delivering an encomium of rhetoric instead of a definition; Socrates intervenes to re-establish the question-and-answer format directly with Gorgias.
textual_basis: At 448c Polus praises rhetoric as the finest art rather than naming its type. At 448d-448e Socrates describes Polus as more practiced in rhetoric than in dialectic and redirects to Gorgias for a brief answer.
limits: The observation records Polus' non-answer and Socrates' procedural correction, not any claim about Polus' knowledge or character.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 449b-449c Socrates asks Gorgias to set aside long speeches and answer briefly; Gorgias agrees and claims no one can say the same things more briefly.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0004
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 451e-453e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 451e-453e
  start_marker: 451e
  end_marker: 453e
  start_char: 9476
  end_char: 14268
  text_sha256: 0ccf579b05d8c3121fd3b3742d73b5edb502b18d3c1cac4f2e053b7e4072ce05
greek_terms:
  - πειθοῦς δημιουργός
  - ῥητορική
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 452d-453a, Gorgias defines rhetoric's greatest good as the power to persuade by speeches in lawcourts, council, assembly, and any political gathering. Socrates restates this in more formal terms, calling rhetoric the craftsperson of persuasion (at 453a), and Gorgias accepts the reformulation as adequate.
textual_basis: |
  At 452d-e, Gorgias answers Socrates' question "what is the greatest good?" with the power to persuade by speeches in political bodies. At 453a, Socrates says "you seem to me to have indicated nearly what art you consider rhetoric to be" and reformulates: rhetoric is the craftsperson of persuasion, and its whole business culminates in producing persuasion in the souls of listeners. Gorgias replies "you seem to me to define it adequately."
limits: |
  This is the first explicit definition of rhetoric in the dialogue. The observation does not claim it is Gorgias's final or only definition, nor evaluate its correctness.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0005
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 451e-453e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 451e-453e
  start_marker: 451e
  end_marker: 453e
  start_char: 9476
  end_char: 14268
  text_sha256: 0ccf579b05d8c3121fd3b3742d73b5edb502b18d3c1cac4f2e053b7e4072ce05
greek_terms:
  - δημιουργός
  - πειθώ
  - πειθοῦς δημιουργός
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 452e-453a, Socrates frames rhetoric through the craftsman-product analogy: rhetoric is a demiourgos whose product is persuasion, and its entire business culminates in producing persuasion in the souls of listeners. Gorgias also employs this logic at 452e when he claims that with the power of persuasion one will possess the doctor and trainer as slaves and the money-maker will make money for the speaker rather than for himself.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates at 453a: "rhetoric is the craftsperson of persuasion, and its whole activity and sum culminates in this." He further asks whether rhetoric can do anything more than produce persuasion in the souls of listeners. At 452e, Gorgias describes the persuasive speaker as one who masters other practitioners, subordinating their crafts to the speaker's power.
limits: |
  The observation records the presence of the craft analogy framing. It does not claim the analogy is ultimately sustainable or that it is rejected later in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0006
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 451e-453e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 451e-453e
  start_marker: 451e
  end_marker: 453e
  start_char: 9476
  end_char: 14268
  text_sha256: 0ccf579b05d8c3121fd3b3742d73b5edb502b18d3c1cac4f2e053b7e4072ce05
greek_terms:
  - πειθώ
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 453a-453e, Socrates signals a procedural move: he will question Gorgias "for the sake of the argument" rather than stating his own view, uses the Zeuxis analogy to justify asking for specification, and then opens the question of whether rhetoric alone produces persuasion or whether other arts like arithmetic also do so. This exposes the risk that the definition of rhetoric as persuasion-producer is too broad and requires narrowing.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates at 453b-c says he questions for the sake of the argument (tou logou heneka). At 453c-d, he uses the Zeuxis analogy: if someone says Zeuxis paints animals, one should ask which animals and where, since other painters also paint animals. At 453d-e, he asks whether rhetoric alone persuades or other arts do, and Gorgias agrees that anyone who teaches anything persuades, and that arithmetic also persuades and is therefore also a persuasion-producer.
limits: |
  The observation records the setup of an elenctic check on definitional breadth. It does not record its resolution, which falls outside this segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0007
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 454a-456a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 454a-456a
  start_marker: 454a
  end_marker: 456a
  start_char: 14268
  end_char: 18850
  text_sha256: eeb97e994d5a79eb45720a6d4afc858645ad7ce4ec443b18a90b02f01373dbf1
greek_terms:
  - ἐπιστήμην
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates leads Gorgias through an assent chain (454d-455a) that divides persuasion into two kinds: one producing belief without knowledge, the other producing knowledge (episteme). Gorgias agrees that rhetoric produces only the belief-kind, not the teaching-kind, specifically about the just and unjust."
textual_basis: At 454d, Socrates asks whether having learned and having been persuaded are the same; Gorgias says they differ. Socrates then shows that belief (pistis) can be true or false, while knowledge (episteme) cannot (454d). He proposes two kinds of persuasion at 454e, and at 454e-455a Gorgias concedes rhetoric produces belief without knowledge.
limits: This records the doctrinal distinction drawn through elenchus but does not evaluate whether the distinction holds or whether Gorgias could have resisted it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0008
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 454a-456a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 454a-456a
  start_marker: 454a
  end_marker: 456a
  start_char: 14268
  end_char: 18850
  text_sha256: eeb97e994d5a79eb45720a6d4afc858645ad7ce4ec443b18a90b02f01373dbf1
greek_terms:
  - τοῦ ἑξῆς ἕνεκα περαίνεσθαι τὸν λόγον
  - ὑπονοοῦντες προαρπάζειν ἀλλήλων τὰ λεγόμενα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 454c, Socrates pauses the argument to explain his questioning procedure explicitly: he asks questions for the sake of advancing the argument in sequence, not for his own sake, and to prevent the interlocutors from developing the habit of anticipating and seizing on each other's points before they are fully stated."
textual_basis: Socrates states at 454c that he asks questions for the sake of carrying the argument forward in order (tou hexes heneka perainesthai ton logon), not for his own sake, but so that they do not become accustomed to snatching at each other's words by guessing ahead (hina me ethizometha hyponoountes proarpazein allelon ta legomena). Gorgias approves of this approach.
limits: This records an explicit dialogical procedure statement. It does not claim that Socrates always follows this procedure, nor does it assess whether the explanation is sincere or ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0009
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 454a-456a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 454a-456a
  start_marker: 454a
  end_marker: 456a
  start_char: 14268
  end_char: 18850
  text_sha256: eeb97e994d5a79eb45720a6d4afc858645ad7ce4ec443b18a90b02f01373dbf1
greek_terms:
  - ἐκ τῆς Θεμιστοκλέους συμβουλῆς
  - ἐκ τῆς Περικλέους
english_gloss: ""
observation: In 455b-456a, a contest emerges over which domain rhetoric covers in public deliberation. Socrates lists technical domains (medicine, shipbuilding, architecture, generalship) where the relevant craftsman, not the rhetor, should advise (455b-c). Gorgias responds with the historical counterexample that the Athenian dockyards, walls, and harbor were built through the advice of Themistocles and Pericles — rhetors, not craftsmen (455d-e).
textual_basis: "Socrates at 455b-c enumerates crafts (doctors, shipbuilders, architects, generals) whose practitioners should advise on their respective domains. At 455e Gorgias counters by naming Themistocles and Pericles: the dockyards, the Athenian walls, and the construction of the harbors came about through the advice of Themistocles and Pericles, not from the craftsmen (ek tes Themistokleous symboules ... ek tes Perikleous all' ouk ek ton demiourgon)."
limits: This records the domain contest between rhetoric and crafts. It does not assess whether Gorgias's counterexample refutes Socrates's point or whether the historical claims about Themistocles and Pericles are accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0010
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 456b-458d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 456b-458d
  start_marker: 456b
  end_marker: 458d
  start_char: 18850
  end_char: 23824
  text_sha256: 341b35f4bf780d9ff7457ed4b518e5eef13bbcbe03a6586f063458df72b38a8b
greek_terms:
  - ἀγωνίᾳ
  - πυκτεύειν
  - παγκρατιάζειν
  - ἐν ὅπλοις μάχεσθαι
  - μεταστρέψαντες
  - δικαίως
english_gloss: ""
observation: Gorgias defends rhetoric against the charge of misuse by drawing an analogy to competitive athletics (boxing, pankration, fighting in armor). He argues that just as one should not hate the trainers or expel them from the city when a student misuses those skills, one should not blame the teacher or the art of rhetoric when a rhetorician acts unjustly. The trainers imparted the skill for just use against enemies and wrongdoers, in defense not aggression; those who pervert the skill are at fault, not the teachers or the art.
textual_basis: "At 456c-457c Gorgias explicitly maps the source domain (athletic training: boxing, pankration, armed combat) onto the target domain (rhetoric) using parallel argument structure. He uses the same formula for both: the skill is given for just use (dikaios), and if someone misuses it, the teacher and art are not to blame but the user is. He then states at 457a-b that the same argument (ho autos logos) applies to rhetoric."
limits: This observation records the presence of a craft analogy structure, not whether the analogy is sound. It does not assess whether the athletic training analogy fits rhetoric or whether Gorgias had already conceded claims that undermine it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates inserts a procedural preface before refuting Gorgias, warning that arguments turn contentious and saying he fears Gorgias may take refutation as victory-seeking.
textual_basis: At 457c-458b the preface moves from contentious argument patterns to Socrates' fear about how Gorgias may take refutation and to Socrates' willingness to refute and be refuted.
limits: This records the procedural hedge; it does not decide Socrates' sincerity or prove inconsistency in Gorgias.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0012
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 456b-458d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 456b-458d
  start_marker: 456b
  end_marker: 458d
  start_char: 18850
  end_char: 23824
  text_sha256: 341b35f4bf780d9ff7457ed4b518e5eef13bbcbe03a6586f063458df72b38a8b
greek_terms:
  - αἰσχρὸν
  - θορύβου
english_gloss: ""
observation: "After Socrates and Gorgias agree on the conditions for dialectical discussion, Gorgias raises a procedural concern about the audience and length: he had already given many displays before they arrived, and the discussion might run long (458b-c). Chaerephon interrupts to say the audience clearly wants to hear them speak (458c). Callicles then swears by the gods he has never enjoyed speeches so much and they could talk all day as far as he is concerned (458c-d). Socrates defers to Gorgias, who concedes it would be shameful (aischron) not to continue since he himself had offered to answer any question (458d)."
textual_basis: "Three distinct speakers intervene in the procedural question at 458b-d: Gorgias raises audience concerns, Chaerephon counters as an audience representative, and Callicles adds enthusiasm. Gorgias is then boxed in by his own earlier promise that anyone may ask him anything, making continuation the only non-shameful option."
limits: This records the multi-party procedural negotiation, not the dramatic irony of Callicles's enthusiasm given his later role in the dialogue. It does not assess whether the audience's eagerness is authentic or staged.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0013
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 458d-458e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 458d-458e
  start_marker: 458d

  text_sha256: ce825b3ad4bb8bb1cf3367f10d9596bab3c079dba700938bf879dc26b7558a26
greek_terms:
  - θαυμάζω
  - ὀρθῶς
  - ὑπολαμβάνω
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates opens his examination of Gorgias by disclaiming understanding: he says he wonders at what Gorgias has said, and that perhaps Gorgias speaks correctly while Socrates does not correctly understand."
textual_basis: At 458e, Socrates says he wonders at what Gorgias has said and that perhaps Gorgias speaks correctly while Socrates does not correctly understand. He frames the coming examination as arising from confessed uncertainty rather than as a direct challenge.
limits: Does not claim Socrates is insincere or that this is a deliberate rhetorical strategy. Records only that the text presents the examination as arising from Socrates' confessed uncertainty.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0014
source_work: Gorgias

greek_terms:
  - αἰσχρὸν
  - ἐπαγγειλάμενον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Gorgias agrees to continue the discussion because he had previously promised to answer whatever anyone wants to ask, and he says it would be shameful not to comply.
textual_basis: At 458d-458e, Gorgias states it would be shameful for him not to be willing, since he himself announced that anyone could ask whatever they want. His prior public commitment constrains his choice to continue the discussion.
limits: Does not analyze whether the constraint is genuine or merely rhetorical. Notes only the textual mechanism by which the respondent's own prior declaration is used to secure his participation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0015

english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates initiates the definitional phase of the dialogue by asking Gorgias whether he claims to be able to make someone a rhetorician, and then begins to specify rhetoric's scope as being persuasive in a crowd without teaching.
textual_basis: At 458e, Socrates asks whether Gorgias can make someone a rhetorician and then begins characterizing rhetoric's scope with "concerning everything so as to be persuasive in a crowd, not teaching but..." This introduces the definiendum and initiates scope characterization.
limits: Does not claim that a complete definition is given in this segment. Records only the introduction of the definiendum and the beginning of its characterization.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0016
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 459a-459b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 459a-459b
  start_marker: 459a
  end_marker: 459b
  start_char: 24162
  end_char: 24980
  text_sha256: 652fe730dc76296c960bb3b7008990b40c987ea802b91e867f7cd55a7cdd74d6
greek_terms:
  - ἔν γε ὄχλῳ
  - τοῖς μὴ εἰδόσιν
  - οὐκ εἰδὼς
  - τοῦ εἰδότος
  - οὐδὲν δεῖ αὐτὴν εἰδέναι
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates draws from Gorgias the concession that the rhetor's persuasiveness over the doctor holds only "in a crowd" (459a), then immediately redefines "in a crowd" as "among those who do not know" (459a). This transforms a practical qualification about audience setting into an epistemic condition: the rhetor is more persuasive than the knower only before an ignorant audience (459a-459b). By 459b the conclusion is generalized: rhetoric need not know how things really are.
textual_basis: |
  At 459a Gorgias qualifies his earlier claim by adding the crowd restriction. Socrates seizes on this restriction and substitutes "those who do not know" for "crowd" without resistance (459a). The chain proceeds through 459a-459b: the non-knower is more persuasive than the knower among non-knowers. By 459b the conclusion extends to all other crafts: rhetoric does not need to know how things are in themselves.
limits: |
  The observation does not assess whether the substitution of "those who do not know" for "crowd" is logically valid. It does not claim that Gorgias intended the epistemic reading of "crowd" or that he resisted the move. The observation is limited to the textual mechanics of the redefinition within this single elenctic chain.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0017
source_work: Gorgias

greek_terms:
  - αὐτίκα ἐπισκεψόμεθα
  - νῦν δὲ τόδε πρότερον σκεψώμεθα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explicitly defers Gorgias's question about whether the rhetorician is worse off than craftsmen and redirects to a prior question about the rhetorician's knowledge of ethical matters.
textual_basis: At 459c Socrates uses a future verb to defer Gorgias's question to a later point and then uses a present hortatory formula to redirect the inquiry to what he calls a prior question, moving the discussion from the rhetorician's standing relative to other craftsmen to the rhetorician's knowledge of justice, nobility, and goodness.
limits: This observation records only the procedural deferral and redirection within this speech turn. It does not assess whether Socrates later returns to Gorgias's question or whether the deferral is strategic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0018

  - αἰσχρὸν
  - καλὸν
  - ἀγαθὸν
  - κακὸν
  - ὑγιεινὸν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates extends the craft-comparison structure from a technical domain (health) to ethical concepts by asking whether the rhetorician stands in the same relationship to justice, nobility, and goodness as he does to health.
textual_basis: At 459c-d Socrates asks whether the rhetorical man is situated in the same way with respect to justice and injustice, shameful and noble, good and bad as he is with respect to health and the other subjects of the crafts, using a parallel construction that maps ethical pairings onto the same structural position as health in the earlier craft discussion.
limits: This observation records the structural mapping of ethical concepts into the craft-comparison framework. It does not assess whether the analogy holds, nor whether Socrates accepts or rejects the mapping at this point.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0019
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 459c-459d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - πειθὼ
  - εἰδέναι
  - οὐκ εἰδώς
  - οὐκ εἰδόσιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates formulates the claim to be examined in the upcoming elenchus: that the rhetorician, without knowing what is good, bad, noble, shameful, just, or unjust, has contrived persuasion about these matters so as to seem to know among those who do not know."
textual_basis: At 459d Socrates poses the question whether the rhetorical man, not himself knowing what is good or bad or noble or shameful or just or unjust, has contrived persuasion about these things so as to seem to know while not knowing among those who likewise do not know. The formulation sets up a knowledge-claim to be tested.
limits: This observation records the formulation of a claim set up for elenctic examination. It does not assert that the claim is proven, refuted, or endorsed by Socrates within this segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0020
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 460b-460c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 460b-460c
  start_marker: 460b
  end_marker: 460c
  start_char: 26510
  end_char: 27298
  text_sha256: 42d7101c54bf4dd8762ae8ef0a710da1243a4fb0d7e8ae290735da1601c0aa07
greek_terms:
  - ἀνάγκη
  - ἐκ τοῦ λόγου
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates derives that the rhetorician must be just and will never wish to do injustice, and he marks this as compelled by the argument (greek_terms: ananke ek tou logou)."
textual_basis: At 460c Socrates states that the rhetorician is necessarily just ek tou logou, then concludes the rhetorician will never wish to do injustice. The conclusion follows a chain of assents from Gorgias at 460b-460c.
limits: The observation registers an explicit argument-compulsion marker, not a claim about whether the argument is valid or whether Gorgias actually holds the conclusion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0021
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 460b-460c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 460b-460c
  start_marker: 460b
  end_marker: 460c
  start_char: 26510
  end_char: 27298
  text_sha256: 42d7101c54bf4dd8762ae8ef0a710da1243a4fb0d7e8ae290735da1601c0aa07
greek_terms:
  - κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν λόγον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates uses a series of craft analogies (carpentry, music, medicine) to establish that the person who has learned a craft becomes the kind of person that craft-knowledge produces, then extends the analogy to justice: the one who has learned just things is just."
textual_basis: At 460b Socrates secures Gorgias's assent that the learner of carpentry is a carpenter, of music is musical, of medicine is medical, and then states that by the same reasoning (kata ton auton logon) the learner of just things is just.
limits: The observation notes the craft-to-justice analogical move. It does not evaluate whether rhetoric is a craft in the same sense as carpentry, music, or medicine.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0022

greek_terms:
  - δίκαιος
  - δίκαια πράττειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The passage moves through a rapid chain of short-assent questions. Gorgias answers with brief affirmatives to each step, allowing Socrates to build the link from having learned just things to being just, to doing just things, to the rhetorician's necessary justice.
textual_basis: Between 460b and 460c Gorgias gives five assents (pany ge, nai three times, pantos depou) to a series of questions that collectively produce the conclusion that the rhetorician is just and will never wish to do injustice.
limits: This records turn-level assent density, not whether Gorgias understood each step or could later resist them. It does not claim that Gorgias has been refuted.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0023
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 460d-460e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 460d-460e
  start_marker: 460d
  end_marker: 460e
  start_char: 27298
  end_char: 28075
  text_sha256: 9576a06337ff61911f491fcd294647599b5341cc217a632f87511894293d71e1
greek_terms:
  - τῇ ῥητορικῇ ἀδίκως χρῆται
  - τῷ ἀδικοῦντι
  - οὐκ ὀρθῶς χρωμένῳ τῇ ῥητορικῇ
  - ἐρρήθη ταῦτα ἢ οὔ
english_gloss: Socrates restates the earlier analogy between the boxer misusing boxing and the rhetor misusing rhetoric, drawing the conclusion that blame falls on the user, not the teacher.
observation: At 460d, Socrates recalls an earlier agreed proposition — that someone who uses rhetoric unjustly is blamed, not the teacher — and asks Gorgias to confirm it was stated. This recall of a prior concession immediately sets up a contradiction with a newly apparent conclusion at 460e that the rhetorical man would never act unjustly.
textual_basis: Socrates asks explicitly at 460d whether the earlier proposition was stated, and Gorgias confirms. At 460e Socrates then states that this same rhetorical man now appears never to have done injustice. The juxtaposition of a recalled concession with a newly derived claim creates the contradiction.
limits: This observation identifies the structural move of recalling a prior concession to generate a contradiction. It does not assess whether the contradiction is genuine or whether Gorgias's prior concessions were coerced.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0024

  end_char: 28075
  text_sha256: 9576a06337ff61911f491fcd294647599b5341cc217a632f87511894293d71e1
greek_terms:
  - πύκτης τῇ πυκτικῇ
  - παιδοτρίβαις
  - τῷ διδάξαντι
  - τῷ ἀδικοῦντι
english_gloss: Socrates deploys the boxer/boxing-teacher analogy to establish the principle that a bad user of an art is blamed, not the teacher, then applies it to rhetoric.
observation: "At 460d Socrates recapitulates an earlier analogy: just as a boxer who uses boxing unjustly is not blamed, and the boxing teacher is not expelled, so too the rhetor who uses rhetoric unjustly should be blamed, not the teacher. The analogy serves as a recalled premise in a refutation chain."
textual_basis: The analogy is stated at 460d with parallel structures about the boxer using boxing unjustly and the rhetor using rhetoric unjustly, followed by the conclusion that blame falls on the wrongdoer, not the teacher. The craft (boxing/rhetoric) is the source domain, the user/teacher relationship is the target.
limits: This records only the structural recall and deployment of a craft analogy inside an elenctic sequence. It does not evaluate whether the boxing-rhetoric analogy is sound or whether Gorgias endorsed the analogy's original formulation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0025

  []
english_gloss: by the dog
observation: Socrates swears "by the dog" in the middle of a methodological remark about how much discussion is needed to settle the questions raised with Gorgias.
textual_basis: At 461b, Socrates inserts an oath by the dog within a procedural comment that the preceding puzzles require considerable further examination. The oath is not attached to a theological claim but to a remark about the demands of dialectical inquiry.
limits: This record notes the presence and location of the oath. It does not claim anything about Socrates' religious beliefs or about what the oath signifies philosophically.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0026

greek_terms:
  - ἐλέγχεσθαι
english_gloss: being refuted
observation: At 461a, Socrates recalls prefacing the discussion by saying that dialectical exchange is worthwhile only if Gorgias, like Socrates himself, regards being refuted as a gain rather than a loss.
textual_basis: In 461a Socrates recounts his earlier words that if Gorgias considered being refuted a gain as Socrates does, it would be worth conversing, but if not, to let it go. This frames willingness to undergo elenchus as a precondition for the dialogue.
limits: The observation records Socrates' stated attitude toward elenchus as a condition of the discussion. It does not assess whether Gorgias or Polus in fact share that attitude.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0027

greek_terms:
  - ᾐσχύνθη
english_gloss: felt shame
observation: Polus interrupts at 461b and attributes Gorgias's earlier concession to shame rather than to genuine agreement, claiming Gorgias was ashamed not to concede that the rhetor knows justice, the fine, and the good.
textual_basis: At 461b Polus says Gorgias felt shame not to agree that the rhetorical man also knows just things, fine things, and good things, and that from this concession a contradiction followed. Polus treats shame as a cause of dialectical concession rather than reasoned assent.
limits: The record notes Polus's attribution of a non-rational motive to Gorgias's concession. It does not assess whether Polus's diagnosis is correct or whether Socrates exploits shame strategically.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0028
source_work: Gorgias

  text_sha256: 815ff60bcfc38986cd09d7af387764f7d137d00023c52496a6b5ac196df15800
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explicitly requests that Polus restrain his use of long speeches (macrologia) and instead engage in short question-and-answer exchange. Socrates conditions his willingness to revisit any prior agreements on Polus's compliance with this constraint.
textual_basis: "At 461d, Socrates says: 'ten makrologian, o Pole, hen katherxes' ('the long speech, Polus, which you first tried to use, if you would restrain it'). He then makes his willingness to revise any agreed-upon point conditional on this: 'ean moi hen monon phylattes' ('if you guard just this one thing for me')."
limits: This observation records Socrates's procedural demand at this point. It does not assess whether Polus complies in the ensuing exchange or whether the demand is effective.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0029
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 461c-461d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 461c-461d
  start_marker: 461c
  end_marker: 461d

  end_char: 29676
  text_sha256: 815ff60bcfc38986cd09d7af387764f7d137d00023c52496a6b5ac196df15800
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates addresses Polus with mock-deferential language, claiming that younger companions (hetairoi) and sons are acquired deliberately so that when the older generation stumbles, the younger can correct them in both deeds and speeches.
textual_basis: "At 461c, Socrates says: 'exepitedes ktometha hetairous kai hyeis, hina epeidan autoi presbyteroi genomenoi sphallometha, parontes hymeis hoi neoteroi epanorthote hemon ton bion kai en ergois kai en logois' ('we deliberately acquire companions and sons so that when we grow older and stumble, you younger ones being present may correct our life both in deeds and in speeches'). He then invites Polus to correct any misstep that he and Gorgias have made in the argument (461c-d)."
limits: This record notes the rhetorical stance of deference to youth. It does not determine whether Socrates is sincere or the irony is directed at Polus's earlier objection at 461c about the impropriety of leading arguments toward certain kinds of questions.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0030

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates offers to retract any previously agreed-upon point (homologemena) that Polus considers poorly agreed, making the argument open to revision at Polus's discretion.
textual_basis: "At 461d, Socrates says: 'ego ethelo ton homologemenon ei ti soi dokei me kalos homologesthai, anathesthai hoti an sy boule' ('I am willing to retract any of the agreed-upon points, if any seems to you not well agreed, whatever you wish')."
limits: This records the procedural offer to reopen prior admissions. It does not track whether any specific agreement is actually retracted later or whether the offer is taken up.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0031
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 463a-463b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 463a-463b
  start_marker: 463a
  end_marker: 463b
  start_char: 31901
  end_char: 32687
  text_sha256: 2b0ac56be44af8942951015482582a93e5019f3ef3845ae7f63329ca4589a047
greek_terms:
  - κολακείαν
  - ἐμπειρία
  - τριβή
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates denies that rhetoric is a craft (techne) and instead calls it a form of experience (empeiria) and routine (tribe). He classifies rhetoric under the heading of flattery (kolakeia), alongside cookery, cosmetics, and sophistry.
textual_basis: At 463a Socrates says rhetoric is part of no noble thing (morion oudenos ton kalon). At 463a-b he states it is not a technikon practice but belongs to a soul that is stochastike, andreia, and clever by nature at associating with people; he calls its 'head' (kephalaion) kolakeia. At 463b he lists four parts of this practice — rhetoric, cosmetics, sophistry, and cookery — and says cookery seems like a techne but is in fact empeiria kai tribe, not a techne.
limits: This observation records Socrates' classification claim, not whether the classification is correct or whether Gorgias would accept it. It does not address whether Socrates later refines or complicates this classification. The fourfold division is introduced here but not fully elaborated within this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0032
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 463a-463b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 463a-463b
  start_marker: 463a
  end_marker: 463b
  start_char: 31901
  end_char: 32687
  text_sha256: 2b0ac56be44af8942951015482582a93e5019f3ef3845ae7f63329ca4589a047
greek_terms:
  - στοχαστικῆς
  - ἀνδρείας
  - δεινῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates defines the practice underlying rhetoric not by giving a genus and differentia but by listing three psychological traits: it belongs to a soul that is stochastike (conjectural/groping), andreia (bold/courageous), and clever by nature (deine physei) at associating with humans."
textual_basis: At 463a Socrates describes rhetoric's underlying practice (epitedeuma) as technikon men ou (not craft-like), but of a soul that is stochastikes kai andreias kai physei deines prosomilein tois anthropois. This is a characterization by concatenated traits rather than by a definition in terms of a form or essence.
limits: This observation does not claim that Socrates always defines things this way or that trait-list definition is his preferred mode. It only records the form of the characterization at this point. It does not evaluate whether the three traits are jointly sufficient or whether they apply to all subdivisions of kolakeia equally.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0033
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 463a-463b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 463a-463b
  start_marker: 463a
  end_marker: 463b
  start_char: 31901
  end_char: 32687
  text_sha256: 2b0ac56be44af8942951015482582a93e5019f3ef3845ae7f63329ca4589a047
greek_terms:
  - τέτταρα ταῦτα μόρια ἐπὶ τέτταρσιν πράγμασιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates organizes rhetoric into a four-part division (tetrara tauta moria epi tettarsin pragmasin): cookery (opsopoiike), cosmetics (kommotike), sophistry (sophistike), and rhetoric itself, all subsumed under the heading of flattery (kolakeia)."
textual_basis: "At 463b Socrates enumerates: cookery, cosmetics, sophistry, and rhetoric as four parts (tetrara tauta moria) set over four objects (epi tettarsin pragmasin), all under the practice he called kolakeia. Cookery is singled out as an instance that seems to be a techne but is in fact empeiria kai tribe."
limits: This observation notes the formal structure of the fourfold division as announced. It does not specify which pragmata each morion corresponds to, since that mapping is not given within this span. It does not address whether this scheme is original to Socrates or conventional.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0034
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 462b-462c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 462b-462c
  start_marker: 462b
  end_marker: 462c
  start_char: 30390
  end_char: 31161
  text_sha256: 24f0263d8b5de71565b355f60f3b0af2f3b022ad1b18df99ecc07693634f0aa9
greek_terms:
  - οὐδεμία ἔμοιγε δοκεῖ
  - τέχνην
english_gloss: No craft at all, it seems to me
observation: Socrates denies that rhetoric is a techne at all, calling it an empeiria (knack/experience) rather than a craft.
textual_basis: At 462b Socrates states that rhetoric seems to him to be no craft (oudemia techne). In 462c he explicitly identifies it as an empeiria, directly demoting it from the craft status that Gorgias and Polus had assumed for it. He also ties this empeiria to the production of a certain gratification and pleasure (charitos tinos kai hedones apergasias).
limits: This observation records the denial of craft status and the alternative classification as empeiria. It does not evaluate whether Socrates is correct or whether the later elaboration into flattery (kolakeia) is present in this segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0035
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 462b-462c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 462b-462c
  start_marker: 462b
  end_marker: 462c
  start_char: 30390
  end_char: 31161
  text_sha256: 24f0263d8b5de71565b355f60f3b0af2f3b022ad1b18df99ecc07693634f0aa9
greek_terms:
  - ἐρώτα ἢ ἀποκρίνου
  - ἀπόκριναι
english_gloss: either ask or answer / answer me
observation: Socrates transfers the role of interrogator to Polus, offering him the choice to ask or answer, and Polus accepts the questioning role.
textual_basis: 'At 462b Socrates tells Polus to do whichever he wants: either ask or answer (erota e apokrinou). Polus immediately accepts by replying "I will do that" and launches into questioning Socrates (kai moi apokrinai, o Sokrates). This marks an explicit role reversal from the earlier sequence where Socrates questioned Gorgias.'
limits: This records the staged transfer of interrogator role. It does not address whether Polus's questioning follows the same dialectical norms as Socrates's earlier elenchus of Gorgias.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0036
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 462b-462c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 462b-462c
  start_marker: 462b
  end_marker: 462c
  start_char: 30390
  end_char: 31161
  text_sha256: 24f0263d8b5de71565b355f60f3b0af2f3b022ad1b18df99ecc07693634f0aa9
greek_terms:
  - ἐμπειρίαν
  - χάριτός τινος καὶ ἡδονῆς ἀπεργασίας
english_gloss: a certain knack / production of gratification and pleasure
observation: After denying that rhetoric is a techne, Socrates redefines it positively as an empeiria concerned with producing a certain gratification and pleasure (charitos tinos kai hedones apergasias).
textual_basis: "At 462c, when Polus asks what rhetoric is if not a techne, Socrates answers with a positive redefinition: an empeiria, and when pressed further specifies the domain as the production of gratification and pleasure. This replaces the earlier attempt at definition through craft analogy with a new metaphorical framing drawn from experience rather than technical knowledge."
limits: This observation records the positive redefinition as empeiria within 462c. The further elaboration into flattery (kolakeia) and subdivision into parts occurs later in the dialogue and is outside the scope of this record.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0037
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 463c-463d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 463c-463d
  start_marker: 463c
  end_marker: 463d
  start_char: 32687
  end_char: 33453
  text_sha256: fd409d05f6368da30884bdecb6f506d8605c2353158ec719dc18ef355253bfdb
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates insists that the question of whether rhetoric is noble or shameful (kalon or aischron) cannot be answered before the question of what rhetoric is (hoti estin) is settled. He explicitly subordinates the evaluative question to the definitional one.
textual_basis: At 463c Socrates says he will not answer whether he holds rhetoric to be noble or shameful before he first answers what it is (prin an proton apokrinomai hoti estin). When Polus repeats the noble-or-shameful question at 463d, Socrates responds to it only after stating the definition that rhetoric is an image of a part of politics (politikes moriou eidolon), and then comments that he answers as though to one who already knows what he means (hos ede eidoti ha ego lego).
limits: This observation records the explicit procedural priority claimed for definition over evaluation. It does not assess whether Socrates actually achieves this priority in the ensuing argument, nor does it cover the rhetorical force of his eventual evaluative answer.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0038
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 463c-463d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  start_marker: 463c

  text_sha256: fd409d05f6368da30884bdecb6f506d8605c2353158ec719dc18ef355253bfdb
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates proposes a definition of rhetoric: rhetoric is an image (eidolon) of a part (moriou) of politics (politikes)."
textual_basis: "At 463d Socrates states: estin gar he rhetorike kata ton emon logon politikes moriou eidolon (for rhetoric, according to my account, is an image of a part of politics)."
limits: The observation records the definition as stated. It does not analyze the metaphor of image-original. The claim is stated kata ton emon logon (according to my account), marking it as Socrates' own formulation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0039
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 463c-463d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 463c-463d
  start_marker: 463c
  end_marker: 463d
  start_char: 32687
  end_char: 33453
  text_sha256: fd409d05f6368da30884bdecb6f506d8605c2353158ec719dc18ef355253bfdb
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Polus repeatedly presses the evaluative question (whether rhetoric is noble or shameful) after Socrates explicitly defers it pending a definition. Socrates notes that Polus has not yet learned what part of flattery Socrates says rhetoric is, and redirects him to the correct question.
textual_basis: At 463c Socrates says Polus has not yet learned (oupo pepustai) what part of flattery he says rhetoric is and that Polus does not notice that he himself has not yet answered (auton lelathe oupo apokekrimenos). Socrates then instructs Polus to ask 'what kind of part of flattery I say rhetoric is' (erota hopoion morion tes kolakeias phemi einai ten rhetoriken). Polus complies (eroto de, kai apokrinai hopoion morion) but immediately returns to the noble-or-shameful question after Socrates gives the definition at 463d.
limits: "The observation records the procedural asymmetry: Socrates tries to control the question order while Polus persists in the evaluative frame. It does not assess whether Polus's persistence is reasonable or whether Socrates's deferral is evasive."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0040
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 464d-465a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 464d-465a
  start_marker: 464d
  end_marker: 465a
  start_char: 35029
  end_char: 35916
  text_sha256: 62981ecd8ed0ef3ce527f8a50c2369b579d106cfb6857b4ec07744f22d10520c
greek_terms:
  - κολακεία
  - ἐμπειρία
  - τέχνη
  - ἄλογον πρᾶγμα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates names the disguised practice flattery (kolakeia), calls it shameful, and denies it is a craft (techne). He classifies it as an empeiria (knack or routine experience) because it lacks a rational account (logos) of what it applies and why, and therefore cannot give the cause (aitia) of each thing. He adds a general criterion: he does not call anything a craft that is an alogon pragma (irrational practice)."
textual_basis: At 464e-465a, Socrates says he calls it kolakeia and that such a thing is shameful because it aims at pleasure without the best. He declares it is not techne but empeiria, since it has no logos about the nature of what it applies, so it cannot give the cause of each thing. He then states that he does not call anything a techne that is an alogon pragma.
limits: This observation records Socrates's explicit classification of the disguised practice. It does not analyze whether the criterion applies consistently to rhetoric or other practices discussed elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0041
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 464d-465a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 464d-465a
  start_marker: 464d
  end_marker: 465a
  start_char: 35029
  end_char: 35916
  text_sha256: 62981ecd8ed0ef3ce527f8a50c2369b579d106cfb6857b4ec07744f22d10520c
greek_terms:
  - κολακεία
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates maps the relation between medicine (iatrike) and cookery (opsopoiike) as a paradigm for the relation between a genuine craft and its flattering counterfeit. Cookery has slipped in under medicine, pretends to know the best foods for the body, and would defeat a doctor in a contest before children or foolish men, just as the doctor would starve to death because the audience cannot recognize genuine expertise.
textual_basis: At 464d-464e, Socrates describes cookery as having slipped in under medicine (hypo men oun ten iatriken he opsopoiike hypodeduken) and as pretending to know the best foods for the body. He then imagines a contest before children or mindless adults in which the cook defeats the doctor, and the doctor would die of hunger.
limits: This observation records the medicine-cookery paradigm as a craft-analogy for genuine vs. counterfeit expertise. It does not address whether Socrates later applies the same structure to other pairs (e.g., gymnastics vs. cosmetics, legislation vs. sophistry).
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0042
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 464d-465a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 464d-465a
  start_marker: 464d
  end_marker: 465a
  start_char: 35029
  end_char: 35916
  text_sha256: 62981ecd8ed0ef3ce527f8a50c2369b579d106cfb6857b4ec07744f22d10520c
greek_terms:
  - λόγον
  - αἰτίαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates proposes a procedural definition of craft (techne): a craft must possess a logos — a rational account — and must be able to give the cause (aitia) of each thing it does. Anything lacking a logos is not a craft but an irrational practice (alogon pragma)."
textual_basis: At 465a, Socrates states that empeiria is not techne because it has no logos about the nature of what it applies, so it cannot give the cause of each thing. He then adds the general criterion that he does not call anything a techne that is an alogon pragma.
limits: This observation records Socrates's definitional criterion for what counts as a techne. It does not assess whether this criterion is applied consistently later in the dialogue or whether it successfully distinguishes all genuine crafts from counterfeits.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0043
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 464b-464c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 464b-464c
  start_marker: 464b
  end_marker: 464c
  start_char: 34193
  end_char: 35029
  text_sha256: 9ede91029ccebb5cb19a9c7511bf1f78842a781bcba7fa75237b8154fe8748f7
greek_terms:
  - τέχνας
  - πολιτικὴν
  - γυμναστικήν
  - ἰατρικήν
  - νομοθετικήν
  - δικαιοσύνην
  - δυοῖν ὄντοιν τοῖν πραγμάτοιν δύο λέγω τέχνας
  - ἀντίστροφον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates divides care into two crafts corresponding to two domains: the political craft for the soul and a single craft for the body. He then partitions the body-care into two sub-parts, gymnastic and medicine, and maps the political craft's two sub-parts antiphonally onto them: legislation corresponds to gymnastic, and justice corresponds to medicine."
textual_basis: "At 464b Socrates explicitly names the two crafts and maps them to soul and body. At 464b-464c he derives four sub-parts through the cross-mapping: gymnastic and medicine for the body and legislation and justice for the soul, with the antiphon term signalling the chiasmus."
limits: This observation records the structural mapping as stated at 464b-464c. It does not evaluate whether the analogy holds, nor whether later passages revise it. It does not claim that the four-fold classification is Platonic doctrine.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0044
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 464b-464c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 464b-464c
  start_marker: 464b
  end_marker: 464c
  start_char: 34193
  end_char: 35029
  text_sha256: 9ede91029ccebb5cb19a9c7511bf1f78842a781bcba7fa75237b8154fe8748f7
greek_terms:
  - κολακευτικὴ
  - γνοῦσα
  - στοχασαμένη
  - τέτραχα
  - διανείμασα
  - ὑποδῦσα
  - ὑπὸ ἕκαστον τῶν μορίων
  - προσποιεῖται
  - πρὸς τὸ βέλτιστον θεραπευουσῶν
  - οὐ γνοῦσα λέγω ἀλλὰ στοχασαμένη
  - αἰσθομένη
english_gloss: ""
observation: After establishing the four genuine crafts (gymnastic, medicine, legislation, justice), Socrates introduces flattery as a fifth entity that insinuates itself beneath each of the four. Flattery acts not by knowledge but by guessing; it quarters itself, slips underneath each part, and pretends to be the genuine craft.
textual_basis: "At 464b-464c the contrast is explicit: the four always aim at the best while flattery operates by guess rather than knowledge. The verbs for perceiving, guessing, and pretending establish flattery as a counterfeit that mimics the structure of genuine crafts."
limits: This observation records the introduction of flattery as a counterfeit craft at 464b-464c. It does not cover the later identification of specific flattery arts elsewhere, nor does it claim that the passage defines all instances of flattery in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0045
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 465b-465c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 465b-465c

  end_char: 36712
  text_sha256: 8778ea7fea609be41834bfac9333ed474e8723bab9b0f1aa0f292a04f4d6ed74
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 465b-465c Socrates explicitly compares his argumentative procedure to that of geometers, using a proportion format (A is to B as C is to D) to map four practices: kommotike to gymnastike as sophistike to nomothetike, and opsopoiike to iatrike as rhetorike to dikaiosyne."
textual_basis: Socrates says 'I want to tell you just as the geometers' and then delivers two four-term proportional statements using the formula 'what X is to Y, that is Z to W'.
limits: The observation records the form of the analogy, not whether the proportion is accurate or whether Socrates endorses the geometers' method in general.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0046
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 465b-465c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 465b-465c
  start_marker: 465b
  end_marker: 465c
  start_char: 35916
  end_char: 36712
  text_sha256: 8778ea7fea609be41834bfac9333ed474e8723bab9b0f1aa0f292a04f4d6ed74
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 465b kommotike (self-adornment) is described with a string of pejorative predicates: it is maleficent, deceptive, ignoble, and unfree, and it deceives through figures, colors, smoothness, and garments so that people pursue an alien beauty and neglect the proper beauty that comes through gymnastike."
textual_basis: "Socrates calls kommotike maleficent, deceptive, ignoble, and unfree and describes its deceptive means (figures, colors, smoothness, garments), then states the outcome: people pursue alien beauty and neglect the proper beauty from gymnastike."
limits: The observation records the pejorative characterization of kommotike as a kolakeia within the body-soul analogy framework Socrates develops in the surrounding passage; it does not address whether the same predicates apply to the other three flattery-craft pairs.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0047
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 465b-465c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 465b-465c
  start_marker: 465b

  end_char: 36712
  text_sha256: 8778ea7fea609be41834bfac9333ed474e8723bab9b0f1aa0f292a04f4d6ed74
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 465c Socrates claims that although the four practices differ by nature, sophists and rhetors are confused with each other and with their corresponding crafts because they are close in nature and occupy the same domain, so that neither the practitioners themselves nor other people know what to make of them.
textual_basis: "Socrates states that the practices differ by nature, but because they are near each other they are mixed together in the same place and around the same things, and the result is confusion: neither practitioners themselves nor other human beings have any use of them."
limits: The observation records the claim about confusion from proximity; it does not evaluate whether Socrates' fourfold classification successfully distinguishes the practices that he says are confused.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0048

greek_terms:
  - εἴδωλον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Gorgias asks Socrates to explain how he calls rhetoric an image of a part of statesmanship, at 463e. Socrates does not immediately explain the image claim but launches a body/soul distinction as a framework for the explanation.
textual_basis: At 463e Gorgias asks how Socrates says rhetoric is an image (eidolon) of a part of statesmanship. Socrates replies that he will try to explain what rhetoric appears to him to be (463e-464a) and immediately introduces a body/soul dichotomy as the explanatory schema.
limits: Only the setup of the image claim and the body/soul framework is in this span; the full eidolon argument unfolds after 464a.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0049
source_work: Gorgias

greek_terms:
  - εὐεξίαν
  - δοκοῦσαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates introduces a distinction between genuine good condition and merely apparent good condition at 464a, framed as a contrast between seeming good condition but not being so. He applies the distinction to both body and soul.
textual_basis: "At 464a Socrates asks whether there is a condition that seems like good condition but is not. He gives a bodily example: many appear to be in good bodily condition when they are not, detected only by a doctor or trainer. He then states the same phenomenon exists in both body and soul."
limits: The observation records the introduction of the appearance/reality distinction for good condition. The full argument applying this to rhetoric and its counterparts continues after 464a.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0050

greek_terms:
  - ἰατρός
  - γυμναστικῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates invokes the doctor and the trainer as the experts who alone can reliably detect when apparent bodily good condition is not genuine, at 464a.
textual_basis: At 464a Socrates says that many appear to be in good bodily condition, but only a doctor or one of the trainers would readily perceive that they are not. He names the doctor (iatros) and the trainer (gymnastikos) as the sole competent judges.
limits: Only the mention of these two crafts as the sole competent judges of genuine bodily condition is in this span. The analogy to psychic crafts is developed after 464a.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0051
type: observation
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 462d-462e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 462d-462e
  start_marker: 462d
  end_marker: 462e
  start_char: 31161
  end_char: 31901
  text_sha256: f2e8902e4c4bdd2d78fe10551f6c75d32d1877bab8fd3503ffdf934b00bba983
greek_terms:
  - ὀψοποιία
  - τέχνη
  - ἐμπειρία τις
  - χάριτος καὶ ἡδονῆς
  - ἐπιτηδεύσεως μόριον
  - διακωμῳδεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates uses cookery as the immediate control case for rhetoric: he denies that cookery is a craft, calls it an experience for producing gratification and pleasure, and places cookery and rhetoric within the same broader pursuit without making them identical.
textual_basis: |
  At 462d Polus asks about the experience claim and Socrates redirects him to cookery. Socrates denies that cookery is a craft and calls it an experience concerned with gratification and pleasure. At 462e, when Polus asks whether cookery and rhetoric are the same, Socrates says cookery is a part of the same pursuit.
limits: |
  This records the local cookery control case and part-whole relation. It does not supply the later full account of why rhetoric lacks craft status, and Socrates' hesitation about Gorgias is only a reception marker here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0052

  - ὀψοποιίας
  - ἐν ψυχῇ
  - ἐν σώματι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explicitly defines rhetoric as the counterpart of cookery in the soul, parallel to cookery's relation to the body.
textual_basis: "At 465d-465e Socrates states his thesis about rhetoric, calling it antistrophon of cookery in the soul just as cookery is in the body. The term antistrophon marks a structural correspondence between two domains: rhetoric relates to soul as cookery relates to body."
limits: This observation records the explicit analogical mapping. It does not assess whether the analogy holds or whether Socrates elsewhere revises this account of rhetoric.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0053

greek_terms:
  - ἄτοπον
  - μακροὺς λόγους
  - συγγνώμην
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates acknowledges he has violated his own procedural rule by delivering a long speech after forbidding Polus from making long speeches, and asks Polus for forgiveness.
textual_basis: At 465d-465e Socrates says he may have done something strange (atopon) because he forbade Polus from making long speeches but himself has drawn out a lengthy speech. He then asks that Polus grant him forgiveness (syngnomen), explaining that when he spoke briefly Polus did not understand and needed a fuller explanation.
limits: This observation notes the speaker's explicit procedural self-correction within the dialogue. It does not evaluate whether the justification is sincere or whether Socrates elsewhere maintains the short-speech rule consistently.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: This records the local correction of a definition relation, not a new full definition of rhetoric.
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates chides Polus for not remembering what was just said, addressing him with the mock-deferential observation that Polus is so young: 'Don't you remember, at your age, Polus? What will you do later?'"
textual_basis: "At 466a Socrates says: 'Don't you remember, at your age, Polus? What will you do later?' (all' ou mnemoneueis telikoutos on, o Pole? ti tacha draseis?). The phrase ironizes Polus's failure to recall by invoking his youth."
limits: Does not establish Socrates's broader attitude toward Polus; limited to this single ironic address.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0057
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 461e-462a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 461e-462a
  start_marker: 461e
  end_marker: 462a
  start_char: 29676
  end_char: 30390
  text_sha256: 8c68110cf3ee006a2309904a979a8f3de94eda88af357362fe6350e27a94f68c
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates proposes a procedural turn-taking format (asking and being asked in turn, as with Gorgias) as a condition for continuing the discussion with Polus, and recasts his own departure as the symmetric counterpart to Polus's complaint about being unable to speak at length.
textual_basis: |
  At 461e-462a, Socrates responds to Polus's objection by proposing a reciprocal procedure: "as I just now said, retract whatever you think best, and take your turn asking and being asked, just as Gorgias and I did — refute and be refuted" (with the symmetrical condition that if Polus refuses short answers, Socrates claims the right to leave). Polus assents and agrees to be questioned on any topic as one who knows.
limits: |
  This observation records the procedural bargain that structures the ensuing exchange. It does not assess whether Socrates actually follows this symmetrical format or whether Polus's assent to answer anything is sustained.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0058
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 461e-462a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 461e-462a
  start_marker: 461e
  end_marker: 462a
  start_char: 29676
  end_char: 30390
  text_sha256: 8c68110cf3ee006a2309904a979a8f3de94eda88af357362fe6350e27a94f68c
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates explicitly links continued participation to accepting a procedural rule: if Polus speaks at length and refuses to answer the question, Socrates claims the right to leave and stop listening. The procedure is presented as a reciprocal obligation.
textual_basis: |
  At 461e-462a, Socrates contrasts Polus's complaint about being unable to speak at length in Athens with his own right to leave if Polus refuses to answer questions: "if you cared about the argument that has been made and want to set it right… take your turn asking and being asked, as Gorgias and I did, and refute and be refuted." The threat of departure makes continued dialogue conditional on adopted procedure.
limits: |
  This observation records the conditional framing of the discussion. It does not analyze whether this threat is rhetorical or serious, nor whether the procedure is actually honored in the subsequent exchange.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: Socrates first asks whether Polus is stating his own opinion or questioning him at 466c. observation_id: obs_gorgias_0062
```

```yaml
  []
english_gloss: ""
textual_basis: At 467b Polus, after pressing Socrates through a rapid series of short replies, exclaims that Socrates' statements are 'wretched and monstrous' (schetlia ... kai hyperphuē), and Socrates must admonish him not to use abusive language (mē kakēgorei). This marks a breakdown in Polus's composure as questioner.
observation: Polus loses his composure as questioner and resorts to an exclamation of outrage at 467b, which Socrates checks by rebuking him for abusive speech and offering to switch roles or resume orderly questioning.
limits: This does not prove Polus has been refuted or that Socrates' position is correct; it only records the dramatic turn from interrogation to indignation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0063
source_work: Gorgias

  text_sha256: fa88a3f7a21db6892fc0be71ddd1d8f75d39c451fcc5b33356b522e0a2e55638
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 467b-c Socrates offers Polus a choice: either continue questioning Socrates to demonstrate a falsehood, or answer questions himself. The explicit procedural offer (all' ei men echeis eme erōtan, epideixon hoti pseudomai, ei de mē, autos apokrinou) marks a formal turn-taking decision point."
textual_basis: "Socrates explicitly lays out two procedural alternatives at 467b-c: (1) if Polus can question him, show that he speaks falsely, or (2) if not, answer himself. Polus chooses the latter at 467c (all' ethelō apokrinesthai)."
limits: This records the presence of an explicit role-switch offer; it does not address whether Socrates' offer is in good faith or whether this pattern recurs elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0064
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 467b-467c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  text_sha256: fa88a3f7a21db6892fc0be71ddd1d8f75d39c451fcc5b33356b522e0a2e55638
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 467c Socrates introduces the example of patients drinking bitter medicine (pharmaka) administered by doctors. The example distinguishes between what one does (drinking and suffering pain) and the end for which one does it (being healthy, to hugiainein), used to drive a wedge between 'doing what seems best' and 'doing what one wants.'
textual_basis: At 467c Socrates asks Polus whether those who drink medicine from doctors want what they do (drinking the drug and suffering pain, pinein to pharmakon kai algein) or that for the sake of which they drink it (being healthy, to hugiainein, hou heneka pinousin). The craft of medicine provides the source domain for the analogy.
limits: This records the introduction of the medical craft analogy as a tool for distinguishing means from ends; it does not assess whether the analogy succeeds or whether it reapplies the craft-analogy pattern used earlier in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: Does not establish what deity was intended; does not prove any particular dramatic effect.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0066
source_work: Gorgias

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  text_sha256: 0861b2038052aef8522ced065e1258f49dbc02c1e46130f77b7685204f98442b
greek_terms:
  - ποιεῖν ὧν βούλονται
  - ποιεῖν ἄνευ νοῦ
  - ἃ δοκεῖ
english_gloss: doing what they want; doing without understanding; what seems best
observation: Socrates presses Polus into conceding a distinction between doing what one wants and doing what seems best to one, then uses this to argue that acting without understanding is bad, not a great power.
textual_basis: The distinction is introduced at the start of 466e, where Socrates contrasts doing what they want with doing whatever seems best to them, and drives the elenctic exchange through 467a, where Socrates secures Polus's agreement that doing what seems best without understanding is bad.
limits: Does not establish a full theory of wanting versus seeming; only shows the distinction is leveraged for a refutation move in this exchange.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - τύραννοι
  - μέγα δύνασθαι
english_gloss: orators; tyrants; great power
observation: Socrates pairs orators and tyrants together as the two groups whose supposed great power is under examination, treating them as a single class for purposes of the argument about power, understanding, and doing what seems best.
textual_basis: "At 467a, Socrates twice links orators and tyrants in parallel: first in the challenge to Polus to prove they possess understanding and that rhetoric is an art, and again in the concluding question asking how either group could have great power if Socrates is not refuted."
limits: Does not analyze the political relationship between orators and tyrants; only notes their paired treatment in the argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0068
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 469b-469c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 469b-469c
  start_marker: 469b
  end_marker: 469c
  start_char: 44039
  end_char: 44890
  text_sha256: 3777234d26863c7d2e64add4f006f67d57bd9be0befc9d042185c58bd662d9d4
greek_terms:
  - μέγιστον τῶν κακῶν
  - τὸ ἀδικεῖν
  - ἀδικεῖσθαι
  - ἥκιστά γε
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates asserts at 469b that committing injustice is the greatest of evils, greater than suffering injustice. When Polus asks whether suffering injustice is greater, Socrates firmly denies it (469b). Polus then asks whether Socrates would prefer to suffer injustice rather than commit it (469b-469c).
textual_basis: |
  At 469b, Socrates states that committing injustice happens to be the greatest of evils. Polus asks whether suffering injustice is not greater, and Socrates replies with an emphatic denial. This reverses the conventional view that Polus presupposes.
limits: |
  Records Socrates' assertion and Polus's reaction. Does not evaluate whether the claim is adequately defended in this passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0069
source_work: Gorgias

  end_char: 44890
  text_sha256: 3777234d26863c7d2e64add4f006f67d57bd9be0befc9d042185c58bd662d9d4
greek_terms:
  - βουλοίμην μὲν ἂν ἔγωγε οὐδέτερα
  - ἑλοίμην ἂν μᾶλλον ἀδικεῖσθαι ἢ ἀδικεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 469c, Socrates frames the choice between committing and suffering injustice as a forced alternative. He states he would prefer neither, but if it were necessary to choose one, he would choose to suffer injustice rather than commit it.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates says he would want neither, but if it were necessary either to commit injustice or to suffer it, he would choose to suffer it rather than commit it (469c). This presents a dichotomous forced choice after his claim at 469b that committing injustice is the greatest evil.
limits: |
  Records the forced-alternative structure. Does not assess whether the dichotomy is exhaustive or whether the conditional is fairly framed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0070
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 469b-469c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 469b-469c
  start_marker: 469b
  end_marker: 469c
  start_char: 44039
  end_char: 44890
  text_sha256: 3777234d26863c7d2e64add4f006f67d57bd9be0befc9d042185c58bd662d9d4
greek_terms:
  - σὺ ἄρα τυραννεῖν οὐκ ἂν δέξαιο
  - ἐξεῖναι ἐν τῇ πόλει, ὃ ἂν δοκῇ αὐτῷ, ποιεῖν τοῦτο
  - ἀποκτεινύντι καὶ ἐκβάλλοντι
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 469c, Polus immediately escalates Socrates' abstract ethical principle to the concrete political extreme of tyranny. After Socrates says he would choose to suffer injustice over committing it, Polus asks whether Socrates would therefore refuse to be a tyrant, and then defines tyranny as the power to do whatever one wishes in the city.
textual_basis: |
  Following Socrates' statement that he would choose to suffer injustice if forced (469c), Polus asks whether Socrates would not accept being a tyrant. When Socrates asks for clarification, Polus defines tyranny as being able to do in the city whatever seems good to oneself — killing, exiling, and acting entirely according to one's own judgment. This tests the ethical principle by applying it to an extreme political scenario.
limits: |
  Records the turn-structure escalation from abstract principle to extreme political case. Does not evaluate whether the escalation is logically valid or whether Socrates' response is adequate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0071
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 470a-470b
source_ref:

  text_sha256: 341e37932ad5cc56e8e2864caca1667ffacc0c9cd4269b20c434d20c523f576f
greek_terms:
  - μέγα δύνασθαι
  - ὠφελίμως πράττειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates proposes a revised definition of great power (mega dunasthai): it is good only when acting according to one's judgment is accompanied by acting beneficially; if not, it is a bad thing and a small power."
textual_basis: At 470a Socrates states that great power appears to be good when beneficial action follows doing what seems best, and bad when it does not. This reformulates the definiendum that has been under discussion since Polus's intervention.
limits: This observation records the reformulation; it does not assess whether the definition succeeds or whether Polus accepts it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0072
source_work: Gorgias

greek_terms:
  - ὅρον
  - ὁρίζῃ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asks Polus to specify a boundary or criterion for when it is better to kill, banish, and confiscate property. Polus refuses and demands that Socrates answer the same question instead.
textual_basis: At 470b Socrates asks what boundary Polus defines. Polus responds by telling Socrates to answer that very question himself, reversing the interrogative direction.
limits: This records the procedural move and Polus's refusal; it does not evaluate whether the boundary question is answerable.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0073
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 470a-470b

greek_terms:
  - ὁμολογεῖται
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates secures agreement from Polus that it is sometimes better and sometimes not better to do the things previously discussed (killing, banishing, confiscating property), and explicitly marks this as agreed by both parties before proceeding to the next question.
textual_basis: At 470b Socrates states that this point is agreed by both him and Polus, to which Polus assents. This consensus is then used as a shared premise for the question that follows.
limits: This observation notes the procedure of securing shared agreement as a premise; it does not assess whether the agreement is genuine or whether the premise is subsequently used correctly.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0074
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 470c-470d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  end_marker: 470d

  end_char: 47352
  text_sha256: 06153d8623b4b76837735139ff300ac5b45a05498dc341c43d14aa0ff6577784
greek_terms:
  - εὐδαίμων
  - ἄθλιος
  - ἀδικοῦντες
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Polus announces an empirical refutation strategy: he will use recent events (echthes kai proen gegonota) rather than old ones to prove that many who act unjustly are happy (eudaimones). He names Archelaus, son of Perdiccas, ruler of Macedonia, as his first example and asks Socrates whether this man seems happy or wretched."
textual_basis: At 470c-d, Polus rejects relying on old matters and asserts that recent events suffice as evidence. He then introduces Archelaus by name and title and poses a forced-alternative question (eudaimon ... e athlios).
limits: Only the start of Polus's refutation attempt; the passage does not yet unfold the argument. The observation registers the procedural move, not whether the proof succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0075
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 468e-469a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 468e-469a
  start_marker: 468e
  end_marker: 469a
  start_char: 43164
  end_char: 44039
  text_sha256: 8290c86684fdad0cd80acfe5dc688137b9f63fc7ede9cd797ea1be643e649212
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Polus shifts from answering Socrates' questions to challenging Socrates personally: he asks whether Socrates would accept the freedom to do as he pleases in the city, and whether Socrates envies those who kill, seize property, or imprison at will (468e-469a). This moves the exchange from a cooperative elenctic inquiry into a direct challenge to Socrates' consistency and character."
textual_basis: "At 468e, after Socrates extracts Polus' agreement that doing what one thinks best without understanding is not great power, Polus does not continue the assent chain. Instead he pivots: 'would you not accept it being possible for you to do what seems best to you in the city... nor do you envy when you see someone killing...' (468e). The turn is marked by Polus' second-person address and the shift from abstract power to Socrates' personal desires."
limits: This records a procedural turn, not a breakdown. Polus does not yet refuse to answer, and the exchange continues with Socrates' distinction between just and unjust action. The observation does not claim Polus has abandoned the argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0076
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 468e-469a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 468e-469a
  start_marker: 468e
  end_marker: 469a
  start_char: 43164
  end_char: 44039
  text_sha256: 8290c86684fdad0cd80acfe5dc688137b9f63fc7ede9cd797ea1be643e649212
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates responds to Polus' suggestion that unjust actions are enviable with the exclamation 'Speak piously, Polus!' (euphemei, o Pole) at 469a. This is a religious-piety formula used to halt impious speech, deployed ironically to reject Polus' celebration of unchecked power.
textual_basis: "At 469a, when Polus asks whether killing justly or unjustly is enviable in either case, Socrates says: euphemei, o Pole. The verb euphemein means to speak words of good omen or to maintain religious silence before sacred acts. Socrates applies it to Polus' praise of unjust power, treating that praise as a kind of blasphemy."
limits: This observation identifies an irony marker in Socrates' diction. It does not claim Socrates is insincere or that the piety language is purely rhetorical. The term may carry genuine moral revulsion alongside its ironic framing.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml

  - ζηλωτόν

observation: Socrates inserts a just-or-unjust distinction into Polus' envy question, separating just killing as not enviable from unjust killing as wretched and pitiable.
textual_basis: At 468e-469b Socrates asks whether the action is just or unjust and then rejects envy for the unenviable and wretched cases.
limits: This records the introduced evaluative split, not the later proof that Polus must accept it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0078
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 468c-468d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
```
