```yaml
  start_marker: 468c
  end_marker: 468d
  start_char: 42372
  end_char: 43164
  text_sha256: e2d1acdb62c22fa74918c6bce34291f24fe16278e073e8b74176b17d48690dbf
greek_terms:
  - ἃ δοκεῖ αὐτῷ
  - ἃ βούλεται
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates draws an elenctic distinction between what an agent does because it seems good to him and what the agent truly wants, and uses this distinction to argue that a tyrant or rhetor who acts harmfully does not in fact do what he wants.
textual_basis: "At 468c Socrates establishes that we want good things, not bad or neutral things, and that we perform actions like killing or banishing only if they are beneficial. At 468d he applies this to the case of a tyrant or rhetor who kills, banishes, or confiscates thinking it better for himself but it turns out worse: such a person does what seems good to him but, as Polus concedes, does not do what he wants, since the acts are in fact harmful."
limits: Records the distinction as it appears in the elenchus; does not assess the argument's validity or whether Polus's concession is justified.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0079
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 469d-469e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 469d-469e
  start_marker: 469d
  end_marker: 469e
  start_char: 44890
  end_char: 45709
  text_sha256: 804bb9cf280ef32d9b53ee63f2723d8327fafafdf95286620ae2908ecd41af1d
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates constructs a hypothetical scenario in which he claims tyrannical power simply by holding a dagger under his cloak in the crowded marketplace. He then has Polus immediately refute the claim by pointing out that on this reasoning anyone could wield great power by burning houses or dockyards in the same fashion. The refutation operates by showing that a criterion (doing whatever seems best to one) leads to an absurdly overbroad conclusion.
textual_basis: |
  At 469d-469e Socrates imagines saying to Polus that he has gained a wondrous tyrannical power because, with a dagger, he can kill or wound anyone at will (469d). He then predicts Polus would respond that by that reasoning anyone could burn houses or the Athenian dockyards and triremes, so doing what seems best to one is not what having great power means (469e). Polus confirms. The structure is a counterexample: a proposed definition of power yields unacceptable results when generalized.
limits: |
  The counterexample is offered by Socrates himself on Polus's behalf; it does not yet test a definition that Polus originally proposed. The passage does not resolve the positive definition of power but only eliminates one candidate criterion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0080
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 469d-469e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 469d-469e
  start_marker: 469d
  end_marker: 469e
  start_char: 44890
  end_char: 45709
  text_sha256: 804bb9cf280ef32d9b53ee63f2723d8327fafafdf95286620ae2908ecd41af1d
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates introduces a vivid hypothetical scenario in which he imagines himself in the crowded marketplace with a concealed dagger claiming tyrannical power, then projects how Polus would respond. The scenario is a turn-engineering device that lets Socrates supply both sides of the exchange before soliciting Polus's actual reply.
textual_basis: |
  At 469d Socrates says, in direct speech, "If I, taking a dagger under my arm in the crowded marketplace, were to say to you, Polus…" and proceeds to invent both his own boast and the reply he predicts Polus would give (469d-469e). Only after this projected exchange does Socrates ask Polus the real question: "Or do you think?" (469e). Polus then gives his actual answer.
limits: |
  The scenario is a rhetorical-philosophical device, not a report of a past event. It does not by itself resolve whether the definition under consideration (power as doing what seems best) is adequate; it only frames the refutation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0081
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 469d-469e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 469d-469e
  start_marker: 469d
  end_marker: 469e
  start_char: 44890
  end_char: 45709
  text_sha256: 804bb9cf280ef32d9b53ee63f2723d8327fafafdf95286620ae2908ecd41af1d
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 469e Socrates formulates a candidate definition of great power and immediately has Polus reject it: doing whatever seems best to one (to poiein ha dokei auto(i)) is not identical with having great power (to mega dunasthai). This is a definition-proposal step in a larger chain examining what power or the capacity to act is.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates concludes the hypothetical exchange at 469e with the formulation "but this is not what having great power is — doing whatever seems best to one" (all' ouk ara tout' estin to mega dunasthai, to poiein ha dokei auto(i)). He then asks Polus whether he thinks so, and Polus answers "not in this way at least" (ou deta houto ge, 469e).
limits: |
  The passage rejects one definition but does not yet propose a replacement. The rejection occurs through a thought experiment, not through observation of real political practice. The definition under examination is framed by Socrates, not volunteered by Polus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0082
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 470e-471a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - παιδείας
  - δικαιοσύνης
  - εὐδαιμονία
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates makes happiness (eudaimonia) conditional on education (paideia) and justice (dikaiosynē), not on external markers such as kingship. When Polus asks whether Socrates knows the Great King is happy, Socrates answers that he does not know how the King stands regarding paideia and dikaiosynē (470e).
textual_basis: At 470e, Socrates responds to Polus' challenge about the Great King by saying he does not know the King's standing regarding paideia and dikaiosynē, and when Polus asks whether all happiness resides in this, Socrates affirms it.
limits: This records the criterion as stated in this passage. It does not assess whether Socrates defends or revises this criterion elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0083
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 470e-471a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 470e-471a
  start_marker: 470e
  end_marker: 471a
  start_char: 47352
  end_char: 48226
  text_sha256: 28609dec54edc12b02d941683df11039a362459ae5960b4e39954e50a4214b29
greek_terms:
  - καλὸν καὶ ἀγαθὸν
  - εὐδαίμονα
  - ἄδικον καὶ πονηρὸν
  - ἄθλιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'Socrates states a strict antithesis: the noble and good (kalon kai agathon) man and woman are happy (eudaimona), and the unjust and wicked (adikon kai ponēron) are wretched (athlon). The inclusion of "woman" (gunaika) alongside "man" extends the claim to both genders at 470e.'
textual_basis: At 470e, Socrates declares that the noble and good man and woman are happy, and the unjust and wicked are wretched, forming a clean antithetical pair with no middle term.
limits: This records the antithesis as stated. It does not assess whether the antithesis is defended, whether there are implicit exceptions, or whether Socrates treats happiness and wretchedness as exhaustive categories.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  text_sha256: 28609dec54edc12b02d941683df11039a362459ae5960b4e39954e50a4214b29
greek_terms:
  - εὐδαίμων
  - ἄθλιος
  - ἠδίκηκεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Polus attempts to refute Socrates' happiness criterion by applying it to the concrete case of Archelaus, generating the paradoxical consequence that Archelaus would have been happy as a slave and is now wretched because of his crimes (471a).
textual_basis: At 471a, Polus argues that Archelaus, born of a slave woman and with no rightful claim to rule, would have been happy (eudaimōn) according to Socrates' account if he had remained a slave doing what is just, but has become wretched (athlios) because he has committed the greatest injustices.
limits: This records Polus' reductio move. It does not assess whether the reductio succeeds, whether Polus accurately represents Socrates' position, or how Socrates responds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0085

  end_char: 42372
  text_sha256: 1ba89a24954cd56a32c550a294533ef1a30fe00589bf142e50f9f61ec67ddfe1
greek_terms:
  - τὰ μήτε ἀγαθὰ μήτε κακά
  - μεταξὺ
english_gloss: things neither good nor bad, intermediates
observation: Socrates establishes a threefold partition of things into goods, bads, and a neutral intermediate class (things neither good nor bad), using sitting, walking, running, sailing, stones, and wood as examples of the neutral class. Polus agrees to this taxonomy at 468a.
textual_basis: At 468a, Socrates asks Polus whether the listed items are the things neither good nor bad, and Polus confirms. Socrates then uses the term intermediates to refer to this class, and Polus continues to affirm the partition through 468b.
limits: This observation records only the establishment of the threefold partition. It does not address whether the classification is exhaustive or whether the partition holds up under later argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0086

  start_marker: 468a
  end_marker: 468b
  start_char: 41546
  end_char: 42372
  text_sha256: 1ba89a24954cd56a32c550a294533ef1a30fe00589bf142e50f9f61ec67ddfe1
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: for the sake of the good
observation: "Socrates constructs an instrumental transitivity chain: actions in the intermediate class (walking, standing, killing, banishing, seizing property) are done for the sake of the good. Polus assents at each step, and Socrates concludes at 468b that all these things are done by those who do them for the sake of the good."
textual_basis: "At 468a-b, Socrates asks whether intermediates are done for the sake of goods or vice versa. Polus assents that intermediates are for the sake of goods at 468b. Socrates then extends the logic stepwise: we walk when we walk because we think it better, we stand for the same reason, and likewise we kill, banish, and seize property because we think it better to do so than not. Each step secures Polus's assent. The conclusion generalizes to all these things at 468b."
limits: This observation records only the structure of the transitivity argument. It does not evaluate whether the extension from neutral actions to normally harmful actions such as killing and banishing is warranted or whether the assent is unforced.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0088

  end_char: 41546
  text_sha256: 6143cd6bf397547bc66ed906904f2cdca22e1883120785a444b32942b0cb2741
greek_terms:
  - βούλεται
  - πράττει
  - ἕνεκα
english_gloss: "The want/distinction: what people want is not the action they perform but that for the sake of which they perform it."
observation: "In 467d-467e Socrates secures Polus's assent to a general principle: whenever someone does something for the sake of something, they do not want the action they perform, but rather that for the sake of which they perform it. Sailors, for example, do not want sailing with its dangers; they want wealth, for the sake of which they sail."
textual_basis: "Socrates generalizes from an earlier case of medicine (taking unpleasant remedies for the sake of health) to sailing and then to all things at 467e. Polus assents to the universal formulation. The explicit thesis is: if someone does X for the sake of Y, they do not want X but Y."
limits: This observation records the want/action distinction as an elenctic premise secured from Polus. It does not evaluate whether the distinction is philosophically sound, does not address potential counterexamples, and does not claim that the distinction is original to this passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - οὔτε ἀγαθὸν οὔτε κακόν
english_gloss: "Socrates introduces a threefold distinction: good, bad, and an intermediate that is neither good nor bad."
observation: "At 467e Socrates introduces a tripartite classification of all existing things: everything is either good, bad, or between these — neither good nor bad. He then proceeds to list wisdom, health, and wealth as goods, and their opposites as bads."
textual_basis: "Socrates addresses Polus with a question about whether anything among the things that are is not either good or bad or between these, neither good nor bad (467e). Polus responds with strong assent. Socrates then secures agreement on examples: wisdom, health, wealth as goods, their contraries as bads."
limits: This observation records the threefold value partition as a premise in the argument. It does not assess whether the intermediate category can be coherently maintained, does not trace its later use in the dialogue beyond the segment, and does not claim this partition is unique to the Gorgias.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: Things in the intermediate category sometimes participate in the good.
observation: At the very end of 467e Socrates begins to characterize the intermediate category — things that are neither good nor bad — by saying that they sometimes participate in the good. This uses participation vocabulary to describe the relationship between intermediate things and the good.
textual_basis: "The excerpt ends with Socrates beginning to describe the neither-good-nor-bad things: 'those which are neither good nor bad, do you mean such things as sometimes participate in the good...' (467e). The sentence is incomplete in this segment but the participation language is clearly introduced as a characterization of the intermediate class."
limits: This observation records the introduction of participation vocabulary applied to the threefold value partition. It does not claim that the participation term here carries the full technical sense it has in dialogues like the Phaedo, does not assess whether the intermediate class can both participate in good and bad, and notes the sentence continues beyond 467e.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 471b-471c Polus describes the successive murders committed by Archelaus in Macedonia and concludes that he did not realize he had become most wretched.
```

```yaml
english_gloss: happy
observation: Polus presses Socrates' unjust-happiness thesis with the Archelaus example by pairing the child-heir murder with the claim that Athenians would prefer Archelaus' life.
textual_basis: At 471c Polus says Archelaus did not choose to become happy justly by raising the legitimate child heir and returning the rule, then contrasts most-wretched and happiest evaluations of Archelaus. At 471d he adds that no Athenian, beginning with Socrates, would prefer to be another Macedonian rather than Archelaus.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0094
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 472a-472b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 472a-472b
  start_marker: 472a
  end_marker: 472b
  start_char: 49904
  end_char: 50752
  text_sha256: dbbee34fe2dff5de08cad3632aff5684514a3e89c6fab1997a647b5c286aac16
greek_terms:
  - ψευδομάρτυρας
  - καταψευδομαρτυρηθείη
english_gloss: false witnesses, to be falsely testified against
observation: Socrates contrasts the many witnesses Polus could produce against him with his own demand for a single witness who agrees with what he says, treating unanimous external testimony as irrelevant to truth.
textual_basis: At 472a-b, Socrates says even if Polus produces nearly all Athenians and foreigners as witnesses — naming Nicias son of Niceratus, Aristocrates son of Scellius, and the whole house of Pericles — he does not agree (oukh homologo), because Polus is not compelling him (anankazeis) but rather trying to expel him from reality and truth (ekballein me ek tes ousias kai tou alethous) with false witnesses (pseudomartyras). He insists the only meaningful conclusion requires Socrates himself to produce a single witness (hena onta martyra) who agrees.
limits: This passage establishes Socrates' methodological preference for one-on-one agreement over the weight of numbers, but does not itself argue for any specific claim about rhetoric or justice.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates names specific prominent Athenians — Nicias son of Niceratus, Aristocrates son of Scellius, and the whole house of Pericles — as potential witnesses Polus could call to testify against him, anchoring the argument in identifiable public figures.
textual_basis: "At 472a-b, Socrates lists by name: Nicias son of Niceratus with his brothers (whose tripods stand at the Dionysion), Aristocrates son of Scellius (whose votive offering is at the Pythion), and the whole household of Pericles or any other family Polus might choose from those present. These names are offered sarcastically as witnesses Polus could produce, embedding the abstract procedural point in recognizable Athenian prosopography."
limits: The passage names these figures as rhetorical foil, not as characters present in the dialogue. The observation records the prosopographic reference without claiming anything about the historical relationships among these figures.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: mode of refutation
observation: "Socrates distinguishes two modes of refutation (tropos elenchou): the one Polus and many others believe in, and another which Socrates himself believes in, and proposes to compare them. (472c)"
textual_basis: Socrates says 'there is this certain mode of refutation, as you and many others think; and there is also another, which I in turn think' and proposes to set them side by side to examine whether they differ. This is an explicit procedural intervention that redefines the terms of the argumentative contest before proceeding.
limits: The passage does not yet describe the content of either mode of refutation, only that they exist and that Socrates proposes comparing them.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0098

greek_terms:
  - εὐδαίμων
  - μακάριον
english_gloss: happy, blessed
observation: "Socrates frames the central dispute as concerning who is happy (eudaimon) and who is not, and characterizes knowledge of this as most fine to know and most shameful not to know. He then states the opposed positions: Polus holds that an unjust man can be blessed (makarion), citing Archelaus as example, while Socrates holds this impossible. (472c-d)"
textual_basis: Socrates says the summary (kephalaion) of their dispute is knowing or being ignorant of who is happy and who is not. He then explicitly formulates Polus's position ('you hold it possible for an unjust man to be blessed') and his own opposite position ('I say it is impossible'), and labels this as 'one point of dispute' (hen men touti amphisbetoumen).
limits: This observation records the marking of eudaimonia as the definiendum and the framing of opposed positions. It does not resolve the dispute.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0100
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 471d-471e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 471d-471e
  start_marker: 471d
  end_marker: 471e
  start_char: 49068
  end_char: 49904
  text_sha256: 5a8127ed3c162d2f452816cdaed1e238b501113eabe7ed95bbd001d3d1a264a9
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates explains his dialectical procedure by contrasting it with rhetorical refutation in the lawcourts. He says that Polus attempts to refute him rhetorically, like those in the lawcourts who think they refute someone by producing many reputable witnesses, whereas the one who speaks the opposite produces only one or none (471e). Socrates links his earlier praise of Polus's rhetorical education to this diagnosis: Polus has neglected dialectic (471d)."
textual_basis: At 471d Socrates recalls having praised Polus for being well educated in rhetoric but having neglected the practice of dialectic. At 471e he explicitly states that Polus is trying to refute him rhetorically, and he explains the courtroom model of refutation by many witnesses, dismissing its worth.
limits: This observation records the explicit procedural contrast drawn by Socrates; it does not assess whether the contrast is fair to Polus or whether Socrates's dialectical practice consistently follows this distinction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates describes the rhetorical model of refutation practiced in the lawcourts as relying on the number and reputation of witnesses rather than on truth. He says that this kind of refutation is worth nothing (471e). He immediately applies this critique to Polus's own argumentative strategy.
textual_basis: At 471e Socrates states that in the lawcourts each side thinks it refutes the other when it produces many reputable witnesses while the opponent produces one or none, and he concludes that this kind of refutation is worth nothing.
limits: The observation records Socrates's explicit claim about rhetorical refutation; it does not evaluate whether Polus's actual argument fits this description or whether the critique is itself a rhetorical move.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἀδύνατον
  - ἀληθὲς
  - ἐλέγχεται
english_gloss: Socrates claims that the truth (to alethes) can never be refuted (elenchetai), making it impossible (adynaton) for Polus to refute him.
observation: Socrates asserts that the truth can never be refuted (to gar alethes oudepote elenchetai), and on this basis claims it is impossible for Polus to refute him, not merely difficult.
textual_basis: At 473b, after Polus says refuting Socrates' claim is harder than the previous one, Socrates replies 'not so, Polus, but impossible; for the truth is never refuted' (ou deta, o Pole, all' adynaton; to gar alethes oudepote elenchetai). Socrates grounds the impossibility of refutation in a general claim about the nature of truth, not in the strength of his own argument.
limits: This observation notes the explicit claim about truth and refutation at 473b; it does not assess whether Socrates' surrounding argument is sound or whether the claim is consistent with other passages where Socrates is refuted.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - στρεβλῶται
  - ἐκτέμνηται
  - ἐκκάηται
  - λώβας
  - ἀνασταυρωθῇ
  - καταπιττωθῇ
english_gloss: "Polus lists extreme tortures: racking, mutilation, eye-burning, and final crucifixion or pitch-coating."
observation: "Polus challenges Socrates by constructing a graphic hypothetical: a would-be tyrant is caught, racked, castrated, has his eyes burned out, suffers many other mutilations, watches his children and wife suffer the same, and is finally crucified or coated in pitch. Polus asks whether this man is happier than one who escapes and rules as a tyrant. The example functions as a reductio ad absurdum of Socrates' position that the unpunished wrongdoer is more wretched than the punished one."
textual_basis: At 473c, Polus says 'how do you mean? If a man committing injustice is caught plotting tyranny, and caught, he is racked and castrated and his eyes burned out, and suffering many other great and varied mutilations himself and seeing his own children and wife suffer them, he is finally crucified or coated in pitch...' This enumeration of punishments escalates in brutality and moves from the perpetrator's own body to his family before the final execution.
limits: This observation records the structure of Polus's hypothetical and its rhetorical function. It does not evaluate whether Polus fairly characterizes Socrates' position or whether Socrates' reply later in the dialogue adequately addresses the example.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0105
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 473b-473c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 473b-473c
  start_marker: 473b
  end_marker: 473c
  start_char: 52406
  end_char: 53232
  text_sha256: b7a66cb341798bbea66678c21ea436daf2ba107ba6f7c85dc728efabf29c3ea8
greek_terms:
  - εὐδαιμονέστερος
  - εὐδαιμονιζόμενος
  - ζηλωτὸς
english_gloss: Polus frames the debate around happiness (eudaimonesteros) and the tyrant's enviable status (zelotos) and being congratulated on happiness (eudaimonizomenos).
observation: "Polus reframes the dispute explicitly in terms of happiness: he asks whether the tortured man will be happier (eudaimonesteros) than the successful tyrant who lives envied (zelotos) and congratulated on his happiness (eudaimonizomenos). This makes happiness, not just justice, the criterion the interlocutors are contesting."
textual_basis: At 473b, Socrates restates his position that the unpunished wrongdoers are most wretched (athliotatous) and those punished less so. At 473c, Polus counters with a question framed around happiness (eudaimonesteros estai), and the tyrant is described as envied (zelotos) and having his happiness proclaimed by others (eudaimonizomenos), setting up happiness as the shared term of disagreement.
limits: This observation identifies the framing of the dispute around happiness. It does not track whether eudaimonia is defined anywhere in the dialogue or whether Polus and Socrates mean the same thing by it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0106
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 472e-473a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 472e-473a
  start_marker: 472e
  end_marker: 473a
  start_char: 51677
  end_char: 52406
  text_sha256: f29e66816d60e9947ab4ce0427e3570aecee3913e044150a8c18511d9bfc3f82
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: strange, out-of-place
observation: "Polus characterizes Socrates' speech as 'strange' (atopa), using a pejorative label rather than engaging with the argument's content. This is a textbook respondent-collapse turn: instead of assenting or offering counterargument, Polus dismisses the whole Socratic position as bizarre."
textual_basis: At 473a Polus interrupts Socrates' argumentative summary (in 472e) with an exclamation calling Socrates' attempted speech 'strange' (atopa), refusing to assent. The label marks the Socratic position as out of bounds rather than refuted.
limits: This single turn is a dramatic collapse marker, not evidence that Polus permanently exits the argument; he continues engaging after this line.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0107
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 474e-475a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 474e-475a
  start_marker: 474e
  end_marker: 475a
  start_char: 55745
  end_char: 56525
  text_sha256: a4c1d9dad0ac8406954903d9c4b9995dbd3009b43ef5804a9c63d1d86bc661fa
greek_terms:
  - καλόν
  - ἡδονή
  - ὠφελία
  - ἀμφότερα
english_gloss: definition of the fine (kalon) by pleasure and benefit
observation: "Socrates proposes and Polus affirms a definition of to kalon (the fine/beautiful) by two criteria: pleasure (hedone) and benefit (opheleia), either singly or both together. This generalizes from visible shapes and colors through sounds, music, laws, pursuits, and finally to the beauty of learning (mathemata)."
textual_basis: "At 474e, after Polus cannot say whether suffering injustice is uglier than doing it, Socrates pivots to classification: shapes, colors, voices, music, laws, and pursuits are called kala either through pleasure, benefit, or both. At 475a Polus confirms the definition for mathemata and explicitly praises Socrates for defining to kalon by hedone and agathon (equated here with opheleia)."
limits: This observation records the explicit definition offered and agreed to. It does not assess whether Socrates endorses the definition, whether it is meant ironically, or whether it survives later scrutiny in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0108
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 474e-475a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 474e-475a
  start_marker: 474e
  end_marker: 475a
  start_char: 55745
  end_char: 56525
  text_sha256: a4c1d9dad0ac8406954903d9c4b9995dbd3009b43ef5804a9c63d1d86bc661fa
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: definition of the shameful by the opposites of pleasure and benefit
observation: "Immediately after defining to kalon by pleasure and benefit, Socrates derives the definition of to aischron (the shameful/ugly) by opposition: it is defined by pain (lupe) and badness/evil (kakon). Polus assents to this as necessary (ananke)."
textual_basis: "At 475a, Socrates asks: 'Is not the shameful (to aischron) by the opposite, by pain (lupe) and bad (kako)?' Polus replies 'Necessarily' (ananke). This completes the paired definition structure introduced at 474e-475a."
limits: This observation notes the structural move of defining a term by exact opposition to its paired positive. It does not evaluate whether this definition will be critiqued later or whether it commits Socrates to hedonism.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0109
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 474e-475a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 474e-475a
  start_marker: 474e
  end_marker: 475a
  start_char: 55745
  end_char: 56525
  text_sha256: a4c1d9dad0ac8406954903d9c4b9995dbd3009b43ef5804a9c63d1d86bc661fa
greek_terms:
  - καλῶς γε νῦν ὁρίζῃ
  - ἡδονῇ τε καὶ ἀγαθῷ
english_gloss: Polus praises Socrates' definition
observation: Polus explicitly praises Socrates for defining to kalon by hedone and agathon, saying 'and you define it well now, Socrates, defining the fine by pleasure and good.' This praise occurs in the context of a definition Socrates has just extracted from Polus through rapid questioning, and the term agathon appears where earlier the criterion was opheleia (benefit).
textual_basis: "At 475a, Polus says: 'Quite so; and you define it well now, Socrates, defining to kalon by hedone and agathon.' The substitution of agathon for the earlier opheleia is textual data, and the praise formula itself is notable within the adversative dynamic of the dialogue."
limits: This observation records the explicit praise and the terminological shift. It does not claim that Polus is being ironic, that Socrates' definition is intentionally flawed, or that Socrates means something hidden. The praise may or may not be ironic; the record only flags the marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0110
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 473d-473e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 473d-473e
  start_marker: 473d
  end_marker: 473e
  start_char: 53232
  end_char: 54027
  text_sha256: 62dc83225f334ec6c64bda0482f0f18954a125e4c01c16988b5aaa816b4f9ff5
greek_terms:
  - μορμολύττῃ
  - καταγελᾶν
  - ἐλέγχειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates characterizes Polus's laughter at his argument as a distinct type of refutation — one that merely laughs rather than refutes. He labels this as a new species (eidos) of elenchus.
textual_basis: At 473e, when Polus laughs, Socrates says 'another form of refutation this is, whenever someone says something, to laugh, but not to refute' (allo au touto eidos elenchou estin, epeidan tis ti eipe, katagelan, elenchein de me). Socrates explicitly names mocking laughter as a pseudo-elenctic move.
limits: This observation records Socrates's meta-argumentative classification of laughter as a pseudo-elenctic species. It does not assess whether Polus's laughter is justified or what the argumentative content of the exchange is.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0111
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 473d-473e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  end_char: 54027
  text_sha256: 62dc83225f334ec6c64bda0482f0f18954a125e4c01c16988b5aaa816b4f9ff5
greek_terms:
  - εὐδαιμονέστερος
  - ἀθλιώτερος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates presents a comparative forced alternative: of two wretched men, neither is happier (eudaimonesteros), but the one who escapes and becomes tyrant is more wretched (athlioteros) than the one who pays the penalty."
textual_basis: "At 473d-e Socrates states: 'of two wretched men, neither would be happier — but the one who escapes and rules as tyrant is more wretched' (dyoin gar athlioin eudaimonesteros men ouk an eie — athlioteros mentoi ho diapheugon kai tyrannesusas). The comparative is framed as a forced ranking of two wretched states."
limits: This records the structure of the forced alternative. It does not evaluate whether the premise that both are wretched has been established in the preceding argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0112
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 473d-473e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  text_sha256: 62dc83225f334ec6c64bda0482f0f18954a125e4c01c16988b5aaa816b4f9ff5
greek_terms:
  - ἐροῦ
  - τουτωνί
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Polus counters Socrates's claim by appealing to common opinion: he maintains Socrates is refuted when he says things no human being would affirm, and invites Socrates to ask any of those present."
textual_basis: "At 473e Polus asks: 'Don't you think you've been refuted, Socrates, whenever you say such things as no human being would affirm? Just ask one of these people here' (ouk oiei exelelenchthai ... hotan toiauta leges ha oudeis an pheseien anthropon; epei erou tina toutoni). The refutation standard is explicitly placed in the mouths of bystanders rather than in logical demonstration."
limits: This records the appeal-to-audience refutation move. It does not evaluate whether Polus is correct in his characterization of Socrates's claim or whether audience consensus is a valid criterion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0113
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 475b-475c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  end_char: 57375
  text_sha256: 8b31545e38400762ffca0bba17b9feaecd66341c2788c03e18a4a51055b1ff30
greek_terms:
  []
observation: "Socrates frames the difference between doing injustice and suffering injustice as a forced trilemma: the more shameful thing must exceed the other either in pain, in badness, or in both. Polus is made to accept this exhaustive partition at 475b-c."
textual_basis: At 475b Socrates states that the more shameful thing must surpass by pain, badness, or both (by pain, by badness, or by both). After eliminating pain and the combination, Socrates concludes only the badness option remains at 475c.
limits: This observation notes the formal structure of the forced alternative, not whether the trilemma is exhaustive or whether Polus should have accepted the initial premise.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0114

  text_sha256: 8b31545e38400762ffca0bba17b9feaecd66341c2788c03e18a4a51055b1ff30
greek_terms:
  []
observation: Socrates leads Polus through a rapid assent chain of nine consecutive short replies at 475b-c, eliminating pain and the combination of pain and badness as explanations for why doing injustice is more shameful than suffering it, leaving only badness.
textual_basis: After the initial framing at 475b, the exchange at 475c shows Polus giving only minimal assent tokens as Socrates eliminates the pain option, the both option, and the remainder option, concluding with a strong affirmation.
limits: This observation registers the density and brevity of Polus's assent tokens, not whether he has any overlooked opportunity to resist Socrates's reasoning.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0115
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 474c-474d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 474c-474d
  start_marker: 474c
  end_marker: 474d
  start_char: 54876
  end_char: 55745
  text_sha256: 3069c94d9db8d18233a9f801f48e458173fbc9e6b534385ce51e000299eb532e
greek_terms:
  - καλόν
  - αἴσχιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  When Socrates infers that doing injustice must also be worse (kakion) if it is more shameful (aischion), Polus rejects the inference (hekista ge, 474c). Socrates then diagnoses the blockage explicitly: Polus does not treat the beautiful (kalon) and the good (agathon) as the same, nor the bad (kakon) and the shameful (aischron) as the same (474c-474d). Polus confirms this separation.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates states at 474c-474d: "You do not hold, it seems, that the beautiful and the good are the same, nor the bad and the shameful." Polus replies "Certainly not" (ou deta). This explicit diagnosis makes the predicate architecture of Polus's position visible: the kalon/aischron axis is not reducible to the agathon/kakon axis in his view.
limits: |
  This observation records that Polus separates these value predicates; it does not assess whether Socrates himself identifies them or whether the surrounding argument depends on identifying them. It also does not claim that Polus has a worked-out theory of value.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0116
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 474c-474d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 474c-474d
  start_marker: 474c
  end_marker: 474d
  start_char: 54876
  end_char: 55745
  text_sha256: 3069c94d9db8d18233a9f801f48e458173fbc9e6b534385ce51e000299eb532e
greek_terms:
  - καλὰ
  - χρείαν
  - ἡδονήν
  - χρήσιμον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 474d, immediately after diagnosing Polus's separation of the beautiful from the good, Socrates proposes a two-criterion account of what makes things beautiful (kala): either usefulness (kata ten chreian, with respect to whatever each thing is useful for) or pleasure (kata hedonen tina, if in being viewed it makes viewers rejoice). He presents this as a candidate exhaustive account, asking Polus whether he has anything beyond these two to say about bodily beauty.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates explicitly offers two criteria at 474d: "don't you say they're beautiful either according to their usefulness — with respect to whatever each is useful for — or according to some pleasure, if in being viewed it makes the viewers rejoice? Do you have anything beyond these to say about bodily beauty?" The question is presented as a proposed definition with a challenge to identify a third criterion.
limits: |
  This observation records that two criteria are proposed for beauty; it does not assess whether Polus accepts them, whether they are Socrates's own view, or whether the criteria are later revised. The passage addresses bodily beauty first and indicates a larger class of beautiful things (bodies, colors, shapes, sounds, practices) but the criteria are elicited only for the bodily case in this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0117
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 474c-474d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 474c-474d
  start_marker: 474c
  end_marker: 474d
  start_char: 54876
  end_char: 55745
  text_sha256: 3069c94d9db8d18233a9f801f48e458173fbc9e6b534385ce51e000299eb532e
greek_terms:
  - πότερον
  - ἢ
  - ἀποκρίνου
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 474c Socrates deploys a compressed series of dichotomous questions (poteron... e...): first he asks whether doing injustice or suffering injustice is worse, and after Polus answers he immediately asks whether doing injustice or suffering injustice is more shameful. Each question extracts a one-phrase answer, and Socrates then tries to combine the two answers into an inference ("so also worse, if more shameful"). When Polus resists, the chain pivots to a diagnostic question about value-predicate identity rather than continuing the dichotomy format.
textual_basis: |
  The text at 474c shows two consecutive poteron... e questions with short answers, followed by Socrates's attempted inference (oukoun kai kakion, eiper aischion) and Polus's rejection (hekista ge). The format shifts from dichotomous choice to a diagnostic statement about Polus's views at 474d, marking a transition from the compressed question phase to a more expository one.
limits: |
  This observation records the compressed dichotomous question format at this juncture; it does not claim this is Socrates's only or typical questioning style, nor does it assess whether the inference Socrates attempts is valid. The observation is local to 474c.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0118

greek_terms:
  - ἕνα μὲν παρασχέσθαι μάρτυρα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates rejects a vote of those present as a valid test and insists on a single witness — the interlocutor himself — as sufficient for refutation.
textual_basis: At 474a Socrates says he knows how to produce one witness, the person he is conversing with, and dismisses the many. At 474b he refuses to put it to a vote of those present.
limits: This observation records the procedural claim; it does not evaluate whether Socrates actually secures Polus's agreement in what follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0119
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 474a-474b

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - τὸ ἀδικεῖν τοῦ ἀδικεῖσθαι κάκιον
  - τὸ μὴ διδόναι δίκην τοῦ διδόναι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asserts that all people — himself, Polus, and everyone else — believe that doing injustice is worse than suffering it, and not paying the penalty is worse than paying it. Polus denies this for himself and everyone.
textual_basis: At 474b Socrates states that he thinks he, Polus, and all other people consider doing injustice worse than suffering it, and not paying the penalty worse than paying it. Polus responds in direct denial at 474b.
limits: This records Socrates's claim and Polus's denial; it does not assess whether the claim is true or whether Socrates later proves it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ἐν τῷ μέρει
  - πείρασαι τοῦ ἐλέγχου
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explicitly requests that Polus hand over the refutation to him in turn so that he may test Polus by the kind of cross-examination Socrates considers proper, instead of appealing to the votes of bystanders.
textual_basis: At 474a Socrates asks Polus to hand over to him in turn and test the refutation of the sort Socrates thinks it should be.
limits: This records the procedural handover request; it does not evaluate whether Polus agrees or whether the refutation succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0121
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 475d-475e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 475d-475e
  start_marker: 475d
  end_marker: 475e
  start_char: 57375
  end_char: 58201
  text_sha256: 3b3173c89e51593b4f75e34932d6d7e02f065414012dcafc99b1e1303fcc7185
greek_terms:
  - κάκιον
  - αἴσχιον
  - ἀδικεῖν
  - ἀδικεῖσθαι
  - μᾶλλον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates rephrases the previously conceded point — that committing injustice is more shameful than suffering it — by introducing the evaluative term 'worse,' and then asks Polus whether he would accept the combination of these two predicates (more shameful and worse) in preference to the lesser evil.
textual_basis: At 475d Socrates recalls the earlier agreement that committing injustice is more shameful than suffering it, then notes that now it has appeared worse. He frames the question as a forced choice between the compound 'worse-and-shameful' and the lesser alternative. Polus refuses at 475e, and Socrates generalizes that no one would choose to commit injustice over suffering it. The reframing moves from a shame-based predicate to a harm-based predicate, which Socrates treats as logically equivalent for the purpose of the choice.
limits: This record does not claim that Socrates proved the equivalence of the two evaluative terms; it only notes that Socrates rephrases the earlier concession by stacking the two predicates together. The passage does not address whether the interlocutors had previously distinguished these concepts.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0122

  - ἰατρῷ
  - παρέχων

  - παραβαλλόμενος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates tells Polus to offer himself to the argument like to a doctor, then contrasts the refutations as mutually incompatible when set side by side.
textual_basis: At 475d-476a Socrates urges Polus to answer bravely by offering himself to the argument as to a doctor, and then describes the refutation comparison.
limits: This records the local medical analogy and comparison of refutations, not a full theory of elenchus or a named rival doctrine.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0123
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 475d-475e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 475d-475e
  start_marker: 475d
  end_marker: 475e
  start_char: 57375
  end_char: 58201
  text_sha256: 3b3173c89e51593b4f75e34932d6d7e02f065414012dcafc99b1e1303fcc7185
greek_terms:
  - ἐξαρκεῖς
  - εἷς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates contrasts the mass of ordinary opinion with the single interlocutor who suffices for the argument: he tells Polus that while everyone else agrees with him against Socrates, Socrates finds Polus alone sufficient as a witness for the conclusion. This inverts the standard rhetorical appeal to the many, asserting that one credible assent outweighs a universal dissenting majority."
textual_basis: "At 475e Socrates says that all the others agree with Polus except Socrates himself, but for Socrates, Polus alone is sufficient. This sets up an explicit contrast between the many who agree with Polus and the single respondent who suffices for Socrates. The formulation is a procedural claim about argumentative sufficiency: one interlocutor's genuine assent is treated as enough to establish the conclusion, regardless of how many others would disagree."
limits: This record observes the contrast between the many and the one at 475e. It does not claim that Socrates is making an epistemological argument about the value of expert versus majority opinion; the passage is about argumentative closure, not about epistemic authority. The observation does not address whether this is a consistent Socratic principle or a rhetorical move.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0124
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 477b-477c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 477b-477c
  start_marker: 477b
  end_marker: 477c
  start_char: 60502
  end_char: 61379
  text_sha256: d0abf4117be82dfb5cb5eca5e15cf98d28354d8462fa63f8183103999e735c22
greek_terms:
  - κακία
  - πενία
  - νόσον
  - ἀδικία
  - πονηρία
  - χρημάτων
  - σώματος
  - ψυχῆς
english_gloss: badness/vice, poverty, disease, injustice, wickedness, possessions, body, soul
observation: "Socrates organizes badness into a tripartite scheme: poverty corresponds to possessions, disease to the body, and injustice to the soul (477b-c)."
textual_basis: "At 477b Socrates asks about badness in the arrangement of possessions, then body, then soul. At 477c he names three corresponding vices: poverty, disease, and injustice, explicitly stating these are three vices of three things."
limits: This observation records the tripartite classification structure; it does not evaluate whether the analogy between domains holds or whether the three categories are exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0125

  text_sha256: d0abf4117be82dfb5cb5eca5e15cf98d28354d8462fa63f8183103999e735c22
greek_terms:
  - αἴσχιστον
  - λύπην
  - βλάβην
english_gloss: most shameful, worst, pain, harm
observation: Socrates infers that injustice is the worst because it is the most shameful, using a criterion that the most shameful thing is what causes the greatest pain, harm, or both (477c).
textual_basis: "At 477c Socrates has Polus agree that injustice is most shameful, then asks whether it is therefore worst. When Polus asks how, Socrates invokes a principle established earlier: what is most shameful always causes the greatest pain or the greatest harm or both."
limits: This observation records the inference structure; it does not assess whether the premise (most shameful equals greatest pain or harm) is adequately defended or whether the inference is valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0126
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 477b-477c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - πῶς, ὦ Σώκρατες, λέγεις;
english_gloss: How do you mean, Socrates?
observation: Polus, after rapidly assenting to a series of Socratic questions, breaks the assent pattern at the critical inference that the most shameful is the worst, asking for clarification at 477c.
textual_basis: Throughout 477b-c Polus gives short assenting answers. At 477c, when Socrates concludes that if injustice is most shameful it is also worst, Polus interrupts the assent chain with a request for clarification.
limits: This observation records the turn-level break; it does not claim Polus resists the conclusion, only that he pauses the assent flow at this juncture.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0127

greek_terms:
  - δίκαια
  - καλά
  - ἀγαθά
  - ἡδέα
  - ὠφέλιμα
  - βελτίων τὴν ψυχὴν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates leads Polus through a rapid four-premise assent chain from the justness of punishment through its fineness to its goodness, culminating in the claim that the punished person is benefited by becoming better in soul.
textual_basis: Between 476e and 477a Polus assents to a series of linked propositions (punishment is just, just things are fine, fine things are good because pleasant or beneficial, therefore the punished experiences good things and is benefited), with his responses progressing from 'phainetai' to 'ananke' to 'eikos ge'.
limits: This observation records the assent structure, not whether the argument is sound, and does not assess Polus's degree of conviction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: Socrates identifies the benefit of just punishment as the soul becoming better and being released from vice of soul.
textual_basis: At 477a Socrates asks whether the punished person is benefited, then specifies that benefit as becoming better in soul if punished justly and being released from vice of soul.
limits: This record notes the explicit tie between punishment and psychic improvement. It does not repeat the preceding assent chain about fine and good things, and it does not evaluate whether the argument works.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 476c-476d Polus repeatedly assents as Socrates aligns what the agent does with what the patient undergoes.
limits: This records the formal chain only, not the later ethical conclusion.

```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0130
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 476c-476d

greek_terms:
  - ποιεῖν
  - πάσχειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "After securing the general principle that the patient undergoes what the agent does, Socrates forces Polus into a binary choice: is undergoing punishment a case of acting or being acted upon? Polus concedes it must be being acted upon."
textual_basis: At 476d, Socrates asks whether undergoing punishment is a matter of acting or being acted upon, the question explicitly resting on the previously agreed principles. Polus answers that it must be being acted upon.
limits: The observation captures the forced-dichotomy move; it does not assess whether the active/passive framework exhaustively captures the concept of punishment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0131

english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates embeds the specific question about punishment inside a conditional framing: the prior agreed principles serve as a premise platform from which the next step follows, and the move is explicitly conditional on the preceding consensus."
textual_basis: At 476d, the phrase "these things being agreed upon" explicitly marks that the question about punishment rests on the prior general agreement about agent-patient relations. Socrates proceeds to ask about punishment only after this acknowledgment.
limits: This observation identifies a structural move within the elenchus; it does not claim that the conditional framing is unique to this passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0132
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 478a-478b

greek_terms:
  - δίκη
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates builds a parallel craft catalogue: money-making delivers from poverty, medicine from sickness, and justice from licentiousness and injustice (478a-478b).
textual_basis: |
  At 478a Socrates leads Polus to the conclusion through a question chain about where the sick and the unjust are taken. At 478b the three crafts are listed in parallel with their respective evils they remove.
limits: |
  The observation records the parallel structure; it does not address whether justice is fully equivalent to the other crafts or whether Socrates is being ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0133
source_work: Gorgias

greek_terms:
  - ὠφελία
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates proposes a criterion for evaluating which of three practices is finest: it must produce either the most pleasure, the most benefit, or both (478b).
textual_basis: |
  At 478b Socrates asks which is finest among money-making, medicine, and justice, then immediately supplies the disjunctive criterion: it produces either the most pleasure or benefit or both, if indeed it is finest.
limits: |
  The observation notes the criterion stated; it does not claim Socrates endorses it. The criterion is proposed as a shared premise for the ensuing examination.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0134
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 478a-478b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 478a-478b
  start_marker: 478a
  end_marker: 478b
  start_char: 62158
  end_char: 63034
  text_sha256: c97076fa0f968fb71669d43e511415cc5ad1bf2f6d593819d566e3d8e7ca04ac
greek_terms:
  - ἰατρεύεσθαι
  - ἡδύ
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates runs a short assent chain: Polus agrees justice is finest, then agrees the finest must produce pleasure or benefit or both, then denies that medical treatment is pleasant, but agrees it is beneficial (478a-478b).
textual_basis: |
  At 478a Socrates secures Polus's agreement that correct punishers use justice. At 478b Polus agrees justice surpasses the other two crafts, then assents to the pleasure-or-benefit criterion. When asked whether being treated medically is pleasant, Polus says no, but concedes it is beneficial.
limits: |
  This records the chain of assents; it does not evaluate whether the conclusion Socrates is building toward will follow validly.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0135
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 477d-477e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 477d-477e
  start_marker: 477d
  end_marker: 477e
  start_char: 61379
  end_char: 62158
  text_sha256: b567e52b85a12bbf3be57ce368ddaf19ecbd51d5fc809760cd70efb744f8e75e
greek_terms:
  - ἀδικία
  - ἀκολασία
  - ψυχῆς πονηρία
  - αἴσχιστόν
  - μεγίστῃ βλάβῃ
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates leads Polus through an assent chain to the conclusion that injustice and intemperance—forms of psychic wickedness—are the greatest of all evils, exceeding poverty and illness in harm even though they are less painful.
textual_basis: |
  At 477d-477e Socrates secures Polus's agreement that what is most shameful among things is so by surpassing in pain, harm, or both. He then asks whether being unjust and intemperate is more painful than poverty or sickness; Polus denies it. Socrates infers that psychic wickedness must surpass the others in harm alone, concluding it is the most shameful and the greatest evil. The argument inverts a conventional ranking that would treat external or bodily afflictions as greater harms.
limits: |
  The observation records the inversion within the argument at 477d-477e, not a claim about Socrates's own beliefs. The conclusion depends on Polus's acceptance of the shame-harm-pain framework established in the preceding exchange within the same segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - μήτε κολάζεσθαι
  - μήτε δίκην διδόναι
  - Ἀρχέλαον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates ranks the life of unreleased psychic vice as worst, identifying the type as someone committing great injustices while avoiding admonition, punishment, and penalty.
textual_basis: At 478e-479a Socrates moves from the ranking of evils to the tyrant-style case of great injustice left uncorrected.
limits: This records the local ranking and type case; it does not add a broad ordinary-life assumption.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0137
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 478e-479a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 478e-479a
  start_marker: 478e
  end_marker: 479a
  start_char: 63819
  end_char: 64611
  text_sha256: 22a4eb7fcadecade3f8d8752e221b967bdf53ae9b12e30c7f0ad3fdbb137463b
greek_terms:
  - ὥσπερ σὺ φῂς Ἀρχέλαον
  - τυράννους καὶ ῥήτορας καὶ δυνάστας
english_gloss: as you say Archelaus, tyrants and rhetors and dynasts
observation: |
  Socrates names Archelaus, tyrants, rhetors, and dynasts as a catalogue of those who commit the greatest injustices while successfully avoiding punishment. The catalogue is introduced by recalling Polus's own earlier claim about Archelaus.
textual_basis: |
  At 479a Socrates says "as you say Archelaus has arranged" and adds "the other tyrants and rhetors and dynasts." This draws on Polus's earlier praise of Archelaus as a successful unjust man, now repurposed as evidence for the contrary claim that such people live most wretchedly.
limits: |
  This records the prosopographic use of named and typed figures as dialectical evidence within an elenctic argument. It does not analyze the historical accuracy of the claims about Archelaus or assess the argument's validity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0138
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 478e-479a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 478e-479a
  start_marker: 478e
  end_marker: 479a
  start_char: 63819
  end_char: 64611
  text_sha256: 22a4eb7fcadecade3f8d8752e221b967bdf53ae9b12e30c7f0ad3fdbb137463b
greek_terms:
  - τοῖς μεγίστοις νοσήμασιν
  - κάεσθαι
  - τέμνεσθαι
  - ὡσπερανεὶ παῖς
english_gloss: with the greatest diseases, burning, cutting, just like a child
observation: |
  Socrates introduces a medical analogy comparing the person who commits the greatest injustices while avoiding punishment to someone afflicted with the greatest diseases who avoids submitting to doctors' treatment—burning and cutting—out of a childish fear. The analogy maps punishment onto medical treatment.
textual_basis: |
  At 479a Socrates develops the analogy explicitly: the unjust man who avoids punishment is like someone in the grip of the greatest diseases who contrives not to pay the penalty to doctors for bodily errors nor to be treated medically, fearing like a child the burning and cutting. The analogy is introduced by a likeness marker, marking it as a comparison argument.
limits: |
  This records the introduction of the medical-punishment analogy in this passage. It does not evaluate whether the analogy is apt or whether Socrates has previously used medical analogies in the Gorgias.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0139

  - μόνος καὶ ὁμολογῶν καὶ μαρτυρῶν
  - τοὺς ἄλλους ἐῶ χαίρειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explicitly designates Polus as the sole respondent, dismisses all others from the dialectical procedure, and asserts that he will count only Polus's agreement as binding.
textual_basis: At 476a, Socrates states he treats Polus alone as agreeing and bearing witness, and that he dismisses the others, casting only Polus's vote.
limits: This observation records the procedural move at 476a. It does not address whether this move is sincere or ironic, nor does it assess the rhetorical effect on the dialogue's audience.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0140
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 476a-476b

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - τὸ διδόναι δίκην
  - τὸ κολάζεσθαι δικαίως ἀδικοῦντα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asks Polus whether paying the penalty and being justly punished for wrongdoing are the same thing, and Polus agrees to the equivalence before further argument.
textual_basis: At 476a, Socrates asks whether paying the penalty and being justly punished for wrongdoing are the same, and Polus answers affirmatively. This secures a definitional equivalence that will serve as a premise.
limits: This observation records the equivalence move. It does not assess whether the two terms are in fact identical or whether Polus fully grasps the implications of his agreement.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0141
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 476a-476b

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - εἴ τίς τι ποιεῖ, ἀνάγκη τι εἶναι καὶ πάσχον
  - εἴ τις τύπτει, ἀνάγκη τι τύπτεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates introduces a general causal principle that whenever an agent does something, there must be something that is acted upon, and the patient undergoes what the agent does in the manner the agent does it.
textual_basis: At 476b, Socrates poses the premise that if someone does something, there must necessarily be something that is acted upon by that doer, and illustrates with the example that if someone strikes, something must necessarily be struck, and if the striking is hard or fast, the being-struck is correspondingly so.
limits: This observation records the introduction of the causal premise at 476b. It does not evaluate the philosophical adequacy of the agent-patient model or its later application in the argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0142
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 478c-478d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates corrects the prior understanding of happiness. At 478c he states that happiness turned out not to be release from evil but rather not having acquired evil in the first place. Polus assents.
textual_basis: |
  At 478c Socrates explicitly revises what happiness is, negating the prior equation of happiness with release from evil and replacing it with the absence of acquisition of evil.
limits: |
  The observation records only this local correction. It does not assess whether this redefinition is sustained later or whether it resolves the larger argument about justice and happiness.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0143
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 478c-478d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 478c-478d
  start_marker: 478c
  end_marker: 478d
  start_char: 63034
  end_char: 63819
  text_sha256: f68c7b38c0d5914c0bb1c8ff43472bc47c39697a4afd5de540f10c64b0c3305e
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates uses a sustained medical analogy across 478c-478d to argue that undergoing punishment is analogous to medical treatment. At 478d he states that justice makes people more moderate and more just and becomes a medical treatment for wickedness.
textual_basis: |
  At 478c the medical-body analogy is set up: being treated releases one from a great evil, and the happier person is the one not sick at all. At 478d Socrates transfers the analogy explicitly to the soul: undergoing punishment releases one from the greatest evil, wickedness, and justice functions as medicine for wickedness.
limits: |
  This observation records the local analogical mapping. It does not evaluate whether the medical analogy holds under examination or whether it was introduced earlier in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0144
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 478c-478d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 478c-478d

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Polus's responses across 478c-478d are minimal assents: yes, of course, clearly the one not sick, that is so, the one not treated appears to me, for it was, yes. No turn in this span exceeds a few words, and Polus offers no resistance or independent reasoning.
textual_basis: |
  At 478c: yes; of course; clearly the one not sick; that is so. At 478d: the one not treated appears to me; for it was; yes.
limits: |
  This observation notes the assent density in this span. It does not claim that Polus's minimal assent persists throughout the dialogue or that it signals capitulation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0145

greek_terms:
  - ὑγίεια
  - ἀρετὴ σώματος
  - μὴ ὑγιοῦς σώματος μὴ ὑγιεῖ ψυχῇ συνοικεῖν
english_gloss: health, bodily excellence, unhealthy body dwelling with unhealthy soul
observation: Socrates maps the contrast between bodily health (479b) and bodily sickness onto the soul, pairing a sick body with a sick soul as a compound misery (479c).
textual_basis: At 479b Socrates compares the person who avoids the painful part of punishment to someone who sees only the pain of medical treatment and is blind to its benefit. He then extends the analogy at 479c by describing the wretchedness of living with an unhealthy body cohabiting with an unhealthy, unjust, and impious soul (479c).
limits: The observation notes the structural mapping of bodily health/sickness onto soul health/sickness but does not claim Socrates asserts full parallelism between medicine and justice in this span. The analogy is asserted, not demonstrated.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0146
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 479b-479c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 479b-479c
  start_marker: 479b
  end_marker: 479c
  start_char: 64611
  end_char: 65355
  text_sha256: d0b19bef08ceaff8bdee834b27e20c936f74f9a1adad18732cbb8faffb9ca8d3
greek_terms:
  - ὡμολογημένων
  - ὡμολογήκαμεν
  - συμβαίνοντα
  - συλλογισώμεθα
english_gloss: things agreed, we have agreed, consequences, let us syllogize
observation: Socrates signals that the conclusions to follow rest on what has already been agreed (see greek_terms for vocabulary at 479b-c) and asks Polus whether he perceives the consequences or wants them spelled out syllogistically (479c).
textual_basis: 'At 479b Socrates invokes "what has now been agreed by us" (479b). Then at 479c he says "if we have agreed truly, Polus, do you perceive the consequences from the argument? Or do you want us to syllogize them?" Polus assents: "if it seems good to you" (479c).'
limits: This observation records the explicit appeal to prior agreement and the offer of syllogistic deduction. It does not track whether the prior agreements were genuinely secured or examine the validity of the syllogism Socrates is about to construct.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0147
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 479b-479c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 479b-479c
  start_marker: 479b
  end_marker: 479c
  start_char: 64611
  end_char: 65355
  text_sha256: d0b19bef08ceaff8bdee834b27e20c936f74f9a1adad18732cbb8faffb9ca8d3
greek_terms:
  - συλλογισώμεθα
english_gloss: let us syllogize
observation: Before laying out the syllogistic consequences of the agreed premises, Socrates explicitly asks Polus for permission (479c), and Polus grants it, making the respondent complicit in the procedure.
textual_basis: 'At 479c Socrates offers the choice: "do you perceive the consequences from the argument? Or do you want us to syllogize them?" Polus replies "if it seems good to you." This is a procedural turn where the respondent authorizes the next phase of argument rather than being led into it silently.'
limits: This observation identifies a structural turn-management move but does not claim Polus is trapped or that his permission invalidates what follows. It also does not evaluate whether the prior agreements truly compel the coming syllogism.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0148
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 480a-480b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 480a-480b
  start_marker: 480a
  end_marker: 480b
  start_char: 66159
  end_char: 66954
  text_sha256: dcb83e3aac2054085343a457bcf71208c976f0e83395fa59bf427f9564b1ecfc
greek_terms:
  - ῥητορικῆς
  - φυλάττειν
  - ἀδικήσει
  - ἰατρόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates adapts the craft analogy pattern by asking what use rhetoric has, given the agreement that injustice is a great evil. The question inaugurates a sequence in which a craft is evaluated by what it does for someone, resuming a pattern already established earlier in the dialogue.
textual_basis: |
  At 480a Socrates says "let these things be true, Polus" and then asks the question about the great need of rhetoric. He frames the question as following from the agreements. This is the "what use?" or "what need?" move that belongs to the craft-what-to-whom pattern, here applied to rhetoric to determine whether it benefits its possessor or is rather something to be guarded against.
limits: |
  This observation does not claim that the whole argument at 480a-480b is a complete craft analogy. It notes only the opening question-form that belongs to the craft-what-to-whom pattern. The observation does not assess whether the subsequent argument succeeds or fails.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0149

  text_sha256: 42324490f885c069363c416088f0eefb898e0e1edff076371efedb01e58962a6
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates ranks injustice and punishment into an ordered triad: committing injustice is second in magnitude of evils, while committing injustice without paying the penalty is the greatest and first of evils (479d)."
textual_basis: At 479d Socrates states that committing injustice (to adikein) is second in magnitude among evils, while the unjust person who does not pay the penalty is the greatest and first of evils (panton megiston te kai proton kakon). Polus assents to each step.
limits: This observation records the ranking claim as a product of the elenchus chain in this segment. It does not evaluate whether the ranking is valid or whether the argument leading to it is sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0150

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates restates the disputed thesis and confirms it as demonstrated: the unjust person who does not pay the penalty is more wretched than the one who does, and the wrongdoer is always more wretched than the person wronged (479e)."
textual_basis: At 479d-e Socrates recalls the original disagreement with Polus about Archelaus, restating that the person who does injustice without paying the penalty is distinctively wretched, and that the wrongdoer is always more wretched than the person wronged. He then asks whether this has been demonstrated true, and Polus assents (phainetai, 479e).
limits: This records the recapitulation and confirmation move. It does not assess the logical validity of the demonstration or whether Polus's assent is genuine or merely conforming.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Archelaus is invoked as the paradigmatic example of a person who commits the greatest injustices without paying any penalty, used by Polus to assert happiness and by Socrates to assert wretchedness (479d-e).
textual_basis: "At 479d Socrates recalls the earlier dispute: Polus had declared Archelaus happy (eudaimonizon) despite committing the greatest injustices without paying any penalty, while Socrates held the contrary. Archelaus is named explicitly at 479d and his case frames the summary at 479e."
limits: This records Archelaus's dramatic role in the passage. It does not assess the historical Archelaus or whether the argument about him succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0152
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 480c-480d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 480c-480d
  start_marker: 480c
  end_marker: 480d
  start_char: 66954
  end_char: 67746
  text_sha256: fdcd15a5752dcee91105136b0ae8f3d7cadb899ba2f443c6ebe7e03e2313258f
greek_terms:
  - τέμνειν
  - κάειν
  - ἰατρῷ
  - ὑγιὴς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates argues that one should submit to punishment as one submits to a doctor's cutting and burning, pursuing the good and noble without reckoning the pain, so that the wrongdoer becomes healthy.
textual_basis: At 480c Socrates says one should present oneself closing one's eyes well and bravely as to a doctor for cutting and burning, pursuing the good and noble and not reckoning the painful. The goal of this submission is that the wrongdoer becomes healthy (hugies), framing wrongdoing as disease and punishment as curative treatment.
limits: This observation notes the medical analogy for punitive justice in this passage. It does not claim that this is Socrates' own view rather than a conclusion drawn from prior premises in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0153
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 480c-480d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 480c-480d
  start_marker: 480c
  end_marker: 480d
  start_char: 66954
  end_char: 67746
  text_sha256: fdcd15a5752dcee91105136b0ae8f3d7cadb899ba2f443c6ebe7e03e2313258f
greek_terms:
  - κατηγορεῖν
  - κατήγορον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates inverts the conventional use of rhetoric, arguing that one should accuse oneself first, then one's family and friends, bringing wrongdoing into the open so that the wrongdoer can be punished and made healthy.
textual_basis: At 480c-480d Socrates states that one should accuse oneself most of all, then one's relatives and friends who commit injustice, not hiding but bringing the injustice into the open. One should be oneself first the accuser both of oneself and of one's own relatives, using rhetoric for this purpose so that wrongdoing is exposed and the wrongdoer is freed from the greatest evil, injustice. A graded sequence of punishments matched to wrongdoing (blows, bonds, fines, exile, death) follows.
limits: This observation notes the inversion of rhetoric from self-defense to self-accusation. It does not assess whether this argument is sincere or ironic, nor whether Socrates endorses it as his own view or as a conclusion forced by prior premises.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - τοῦ τε Ἀθηναίων δήμου καὶ τοῦ Πυριλάμπους
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates claims that he and Callicles share a parallel erotic predicament, each of two men in love with two objects. Socrates names his own beloved pair as Alcibiades son of Cleinias and philosophy, and Callicles's as the Athenian demos and the son of Pyrilampes.
textual_basis: At 481d Socrates says both he and Callicles are two men each in love with two objects, then enumerates the two pairs explicitly.
limits: Only records the explicit erotic parallelism stated by Socrates; does not assess whether Socrates's self-characterization is sincere or ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0155

greek_terms:
  - οὐ δυναμένου ἀντιλέγειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates describes Callicles as unable to contradict his beloveds, constantly shifting position up and down, changing what he says in the assembly to match whatever the Athenian demos says, and suffering the same toward the son of Pyrilampes.
textual_basis: At 481d-e Socrates says Callicles is incapable of speaking against his beloveds, and at 481e states that in the assembly Callicles shifts to say what the demos wants.
limits: This records Socrates's characterization of Callicles; it does not assess its accuracy or whether it is fair.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0156
source_work: Gorgias

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
```
