```yaml
  text_sha256: 81ea132e801a2c3a4b8f0aaaf04b75dd635088a764a5445c02b9f30d1abbab6f
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles enters the dialogue as a new respondent, replacing Polus mid-conversation. He addresses Chaerephon first, asking whether Socrates is serious or joking, before Chaerephon redirects him to question Socrates directly at 481b-481c.
textual_basis: "At 481b, after Socrates concludes his exchange with Polus, Callicles speaks for the first time: he asks Chaerephon whether Socrates is in earnest or playing. Chaerephon replies that Socrates seems exceptionally serious and advises Callicles to ask Socrates himself. This marks a formal respondent switch from Polus to Callicles."
limits: This observation records the turn-taking mechanism and change of interlocutor. It does not assess whether Callicles will adopt a different argumentative style than Polus, nor does it interpret the content of the earnestness/play question beyond its dramatic function.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0157
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 481b-481c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 481b-481c
  start_marker: 481b
  end_marker: 481c
  start_char: 68553
  end_char: 69417
  text_sha256: 81ea132e801a2c3a4b8f0aaaf04b75dd635088a764a5445c02b9f30d1abbab6f
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles opens his participation by questioning whether Socrates is in earnest or playing, using a third-party interrogation through Chaerephon before addressing Socrates directly at 481b-481c. The question frames the stakes of the preceding argument for the audience.
textual_basis: Callicles asks Chaerephon at 481b whether Socrates is in earnest about these things or is playing. Chaerephon answers that Socrates seems exceptionally serious and suggests Callicles ask Socrates himself. Callicles then eagerly agrees and addresses Socrates directly at 481b-481c, asking whether they should take him to be in earnest or playing.
limits: This observation identifies the seriousness/play question as a recurring dramatic device that frames argumentative commitments. It does not claim that Callicles is accusing Socrates of irony, nor does it interpret the philosophical implications of the earnestness/play distinction beyond its occurrence as a turn-taking mechanism.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0158
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 481b-481c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 481b-481c
  start_marker: 481b
  end_marker: 481c
  start_char: 68553
  end_char: 69417
  text_sha256: 81ea132e801a2c3a4b8f0aaaf04b75dd635088a764a5445c02b9f30d1abbab6f
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Callicles frames the practical consequence of Socrates' thesis at 481c: if Socrates is in earnest and his claims are true, then human life would be turned upside down and people would be doing the opposite of what they should."
textual_basis: "At 481c Callicles says: if you are in earnest and what you say happens to be true, then wouldn't our human life be turned upside down and we would be doing everything contrary, it seems, to what we ought? This converts the abstract ethical claims Socrates has been making into a concrete diagnosis of how ordinary human practice would be inverted."
limits: This observation records the pragmatic framing of a philosophical thesis, a recurrent Socratic-elencic pattern where an interlocutor registers the lived stakes of an abstract position. It does not evaluate whether Callicles' inference is correct or whether Socrates' thesis does in fact invert ordinary life.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates challenges Callicles to refute the claim that doing injustice and not paying the penalty is the greatest of evils, or else Callicles will be in dissonance (diaphonein) with himself for his whole life.
textual_basis: At 482b Socrates says 'either refute her [philosophy]... that doing injustice and not paying the penalty is not the greatest of evils; or if you leave this unrefuted... Callicles will not agree with you, Callicles, but will be in dissonance in his entire life.' The oath 'by the dog, the god of the Egyptians' intensifies the challenge.
limits: This observation records the binding challenge and the diaphonia consequence; it does not evaluate whether the argument succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - καταγέλαστοι
  - πολιτικοί
  - φιλοσοφίας
  - παιδείας
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles says philosophers in civic action and politicians in philosophical discussions become ridiculous outside their domains, then limits philosophy to education and youth.
textual_basis: At 484e-485a Callicles compares each type's weakness outside its own domain and then states the proper educational measure for philosophy.
limits: This records a type-portrait in Callicles' speech, not authorial irony.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0163
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 484e-485a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 484e-485a
  start_marker: 484e
  end_marker: 485a
  start_char: 75952
  end_char: 76740
  text_sha256: 48bd5b121bface0b5b79869d41929417fb2c5a3af45e1515e7c3faedf973b4fe
greek_terms:
  - φιλοσοφίας
  - παιδείας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Callicles draws a boundary inside intellectual practice: philosophy (philosophias) is permissible 'for the sake of education' (paideias charin) and is not shameful for a youth, but becomes shameful and ridiculous when an older man persists in it. The demarcation separates philosophy-as-education from philosophy-as-enduring-practice."
textual_basis: "At 485a, Callicles says: 'to partake of philosophy insofar as it is for the sake of education is noble' (philosophias men hoson paideias charin kalon metechein), and that it is not shameful for a young man to philosophize (kai ouk aischron meirakiōi onti philosophein), but when an older man still philosophizes it becomes ridiculous (katagelaston). The phrase hoson paideias charin ('as much as for the sake of education') establishes a limit on philosophy's legitimate scope."
limits: "This observation records how Callicles defines a permissible quantum of philosophy. It does not claim that Socrates or the dialogue endorses this boundary, nor does it analyze whether this is a genuine definition or a rhetorical move. The label is cross-dialogue: similar demarcations of philosophy's proper scope appear in other dialogues."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0164
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 484e-485a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 484e-485a
  start_marker: 484e
  end_marker: 485a
  start_char: 75952
  end_char: 76740
  text_sha256: 48bd5b121bface0b5b79869d41929417fb2c5a3af45e1515e7c3faedf973b4fe
greek_terms:
  - Εὐριπίδου
  - λαμπρός
  - ἕκαστος
  - ἐπείγεται
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Callicles invokes Euripides by name and quotes a gnomic passage to support his claim that each person excels and strives in their own domain. The poetic citation functions as an argumentative authority: the quoted lines supply the premise that men flee what they are bad at and praise what they are good at, out of self-regard."
textual_basis: "At 484e, Callicles signals the citation with 'the saying of Euripides' (to tou Euripidou), then quotes four fragments marked with {quote} tags in the Greek text: lampros ('brilliant'), hekastos ('each'), kai epi tout' epeigetai ('and to this he hastens'), and the longer closing fragment. At 485a he draws the conclusion from the poetic premise: where a man is base, he flees and reviles that thing, and praises the other, thinking that in doing so he praises himself. The poetic quotation is not decorative; it provides the structural logic for the argument that follows."
limits: This observation records the presence and argumentative function of a poetic citation from Euripides. It does not assess whether the quotation is accurate, whether Callicles misapplies it, or whether Plato is criticizing poetic authority through its placement in Callicles's speech. The feature records the structural move, not its validity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0165
source_work: Gorgias

greek_terms:
  - τὸν κρείττω τοῦ ἥττονος ἄρχειν καὶ πλέον ἔχειν
  - κέκριται
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles defines justice at 483d as the superior ruling the inferior and having more, then supports this definition by appealing to observable evidence from animals and from human cities and races where, he claims, this is how justice is decided.
textual_basis: "At 483d, Callicles states the definitional claim that justice is the better having more than the worse and the more powerful than the less powerful. He immediately adds that this is shown in many places: in other animals and in whole human cities and races, and that justice has been decided this way — the stronger rules the weaker and has more."
limits: This observation records a definitional move with empirical support. It does not assess whether Callicles' examples are correct or whether Socrates accepts this definition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0166
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 483d-483e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 483d-483e

  start_char: 73590

  text_sha256: 0770e3ccd36480a535c71293b26583a47182ba8d437c0d2166d87477c1d6c83d
greek_terms:
  - κατὰ φύσιν τὴν τοῦ δικαίου
  - κατὰ νόμον γε τὸν τῆς φύσεως
  - κατὰ τοῦτον ὃν ἡμεῖς τιθέμεθα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles invokes the law of nature as the standard by which Xerxes and Darius acted justly, and explicitly contrasts it with the conventional law that we lay down.
textual_basis: At 483e, Callicles claims that Xerxes and his father acted according to the nature of justice and by the law of nature, then immediately adds not, perhaps, according to this one which we lay down. This sets nature-based justice directly against human convention.
limits: This observation records the physis/nomos contrast as Callicles frames it. It does not evaluate whether Plato endorses this contrast or whether Callicles' examples actually exemplify natural justice.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - κατεπᾴδοντές τε καὶ γοητεύοντες καταδουλούμεθα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles describes conventional political education as a process of molding the naturally strongest young men like lion cubs, then enslaving them through spells and enchantments.
textual_basis: At 483e, Callicles says that they mold the best and strongest among us, taking them from youth just like lions, and enslave them by singing spells and bewitching them. The metaphor frames conventional moral education as a form of taming or domestication of the naturally powerful.
limits: This observation records the animal-taming metaphor for political education. It does not claim that Callicles is correct about conventional education or that the metaphor is internally consistent.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - εὐδόκιμον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles invokes the ideal of the noble-and-good man at 484d as the standard against which the over-aged philosopher fails, coupling it with 'well-reputed', thus defining the desirable end state in terms of both personal excellence and public reputation.
textual_basis: At the transition from 484c to 484d, Callicles says the philosopher must be inexperienced in what is needed for the man destined to be noble-and-good and well-reputed. The pairing ties virtue to reputation.
limits: This notes the specific ideal term Callicles deploys. It does not claim that Callicles' usage matches Socratic usage of the term elsewhere, nor that Callicles is the only speaker in the dialogue to use this term.

```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0171

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles asserts that nature (physis) and convention (nomos) are for the most part opposed to each other (482e-483a).
textual_basis: At 482e-483a Callicles explicitly states that nature and convention are mostly contrary to one another, then explains that Socrates' refutation of Polus exploited this opposition by shifting the frame between nature and convention.
limits: This observation notes the explicit physis-nomos opposition stated by Callicles but does not assess whether his account of the relationship is consistent or endorsed by the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0172

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Callicles claims that Socrates tricked Polus by exploiting shame: Polus was ashamed to say what he really thought, and Socrates used this to silence him (482e-483a)."
textual_basis: At 482e Callicles states that Polus was tripped up by his own agreement, silenced because he was ashamed to say what he really thought. At 483a he describes Socrates' technique as asking questions according to nature when someone speaks according to convention, and vice versa.
limits: This observation records Callicles' diagnosis of Socrates' elenctic procedure. It does not assess whether Callicles' characterization is accurate or fair.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0173

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Callicles applies the physis-nomos opposition to the evaluative pair doing injustice versus suffering injustice: by nature suffering injustice is more shameful and worse, but by convention doing injustice is more shameful (483a)."
textual_basis: "At 483a Callicles states: by nature everything that is worse is more shameful, so suffering injustice is more shameful; by convention doing injustice is more shameful. This inverts the conventional ranking Callicles attributes to Polus."
limits: This observation records Callicles' application of the physis-nomos opposition to the injustice pair. It does not evaluate the soundness of the claim or whether Callicles' nature-based ranking is consistent elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0175
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 485b-485c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 485b-485c
  start_marker: 485b
  end_marker: 485c
  start_char: 76740
  end_char: 77530
  text_sha256: 49eb4a9fbcafb8890be371402927c8945350e4ccbf7b25cbc9cfab5d0dc319ab
greek_terms:
  - ἀνελεύθερον
  - καλοῦ
  - γενναίου
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Callicles characterizes the youth who philosophizes as free (eleutheros), and the youth who does not philosophize as unfree (aneleutheros) and as one who will never consider himself worthy of anything fine or noble (kalou, gennaiou, 485c). This creates a binary ethical profile for young men based on their engagement with philosophy.
textual_basis: |
  At 485c Callicles says he regards a philosophizing youth as eleutheron and finds it fitting (prepein), while the non-philosophizing youth he calls aneleutheron and declares that such a person will never consider himself worthy of anything kalou or gennaiou. The vocabulary draws a sharp status-and-character boundary.
limits: |
  This observation records Callicles' characterization of philosophical vs. non-philosophical youth as a prosopographic ethical type; it does not assess whether Socrates or Plato endorses this binary. It does not address the later part of Callicles' speech where he attacks adult philosophizing.
review_status: accepted
```


```yaml
textual_basis: At 485d-485e the polemic locates the mature philosopher away from public civic spaces and contrasts that setting with free and adequate speech.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0178
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 485d-485e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - ὅπερ ὁ Ζῆθος πρὸς τὸν Ἀμφίονα ὁ Εὐριπίδου
english_gloss: Callicles likens himself to Zethus addressing Amphion in Euripides.
observation: Callicles explicitly invokes Euripides' Zethus-Amphion antithesis to frame his own speech to Socrates, characterizing his rebuke as analogous to Zethus' rebuke of his brother Amphion for neglecting practical life.
textual_basis: At 485e, Callicles says he experiences toward Socrates what Zethus did toward Amphion in Euripides, whom he just mentioned. He then quotes or paraphrases Zethus' words about Socrates neglecting what he should care for.
limits: This observation records only the poetic framing device. It does not evaluate the accuracy of the Euripidean parallel or make claims about Plato's view of Euripides.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0180
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 484a-484b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  end_marker: 484b
  start_char: 74330
  end_char: 75087
  text_sha256: 1fbf94573182ee124c78053076d52d0d493245f66b26614d092e03a14769f1d5
greek_terms:
  - νόμους τοὺς παρὰ φύσιν
  - τὸ τῆς φύσεως δίκαιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles contrasts conventional laws with natural justice, characterizing the many as imposing laws contrary to nature and declaring that a sufficiently strong man would break free and reveal the justice of nature.
textual_basis: At 484a Callicles describes the strong man trampling on laws all contrary to nature, and at 484a-484b declares that in this moment the justice of nature shone forth. The passage sets conventional law and natural justice as opposing normative sources.
limits: This observation registers the physis-nomos contrast as a textual feature of Callicles' speech. It does not assess whether the argument is internally consistent or whether Callicles' deployment of Pindar is faithful to the original.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0181
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 486c-486d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 486c-486d
  start_marker: 486c
  end_marker: 486d
  start_char: 79239
  end_char: 80098
  text_sha256: 50dd25710fb5e05abd1b8317c261d1fdb8dfa5eeb5a320ad01e79d2dffed9937
greek_terms:
  - ἄτιμον
  - εὐμουσίαν
  - ληρήματα
  - φλυαρίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Callicles advises that a man who fails to defend himself and his friends from injustice will live as an atimos in the city, and that the permissible response to such a man includes physical violence without legal penalty. The argument inverts ordinary ethical expectation: the victim who cannot retaliate is treated as the one who deserves dishonor and physical abuse.
textual_basis: |
  At 486c Callicles describes the man who cannot help himself or his friends as suffering confiscation by enemies and living atimos (literally without honor or rights) in the city. He then adds that such a man may be struck on the cheek without the striker facing justice (eipe ti kai agroikoteron eiristhai, exestin epi korres tuptonta me didonai diken). The structure presents failure of self-defense — not the original injury — as the condition that licenses further abuse.
limits: |
  This observation records the structure of Callicles' inversion claim within this single turn. It does not assess whether Callicles' position is consistent with other passages, does not evaluate the argument's validity, and does not claim that Callicles intends to invert ethics.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0182
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 486c-486d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 486c-486d
  start_marker: 486c
  end_marker: 486d
  start_char: 79239
  end_char: 80098
  text_sha256: 50dd25710fb5e05abd1b8317c261d1fdb8dfa5eeb5a320ad01e79d2dffed9937
greek_terms:
  - παῦσαι δὲ ἐλέγχων
  - πραγμάτων δ’ εὐμουσίαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Callicles exhorts Socrates to stop refuting (paupai de elegkhon) and instead to cultivate the refined practice of public affairs (pragmaton d' eumousian askei). He characterizes Socratic elenchus and its objects as "these small things" (ta mikra tauta), contrasting them with the substantial goods of life, reputation, and other advantages that belong to men who do not spend their time refuting trivialities.
textual_basis: |
  In his speech at 486c, Callicles uses an imperative series: paupai de elegkhon followed by pragmaton d' eumousian askei. He then dismisses Socratic activities as "these fine things" (ta kompsa tauta), which he reclassifies as either nonsense (leremata) or drivel (phluarias), and finally at the segment boundary (486c-486d) calls them "these small things" (ta mikra tauta). The exhortation explicitly deprecates the entire activity of refutation in favor of practical affairs.
limits: |
  This observation records the turn-geometry feature of Callicles attacking the activity of elenchus itself as trivial. It does not evaluate whether elenchus is in fact trivial or whether Callicles' characterization is fair.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0183
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 486c-486d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 486c-486d
  start_marker: 486c
  end_marker: 486d
  start_char: 79239
  end_char: 80098
  text_sha256: 50dd25710fb5e05abd1b8317c261d1fdb8dfa5eeb5a320ad01e79d2dffed9937
greek_terms:
  - χρυσῆν
  - ψυχήν
  - βασανίζουσιν
  - βασάνου
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates replies to Callicles' exhortation with a counter-analogy: if he had a golden soul, he would eagerly seek out the best touchstone (basanos) against which to test it, so that if the stone confirmed the soul had been well cared for, he would know he was in adequate condition and needed no further test. The analogy maps gold-testing craft practice onto the examination of the soul.
textual_basis: |
  At 486d, Socrates begins his reply by introducing the hypothetical of having a golden soul (ei chrusen echon etunchanon ten psuchen), then describes seeking the best of those stones with which they test gold (ton lithon he basanizousin ton chruson, ten aristen), and frames the purpose as confirming that the soul has been well cared for (kalos tetherapeusthai). The touchstone (basanos) is the testing instrument, and Socrates frames the entire reply around the craft of testing precious metal.
limits: |
  This observation records the craft analogy Socrates deploys in his reply. It does not identify what or whom the touchstone represents in the dialogue, does not assess whether the analogy is successful, and does not claim that Socrates' soul is in fact golden.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0185

limits: This observation records the definitional probe. It does not assess whether Callicles actually equivocated or whether the two terms are in fact distinct.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

limits: This observation records the formal restatement request within the elenchus. It does not assess whether the restatement will alter Callicles' position or whether the request is a strategic move.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0187

  - Ναυσικύδην τὸν Χολαργέα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates names four men who became companions in wisdom: Callicles, Teisandros of Aphidna, Andron son of Androtion, and Nausikydes of Cholargos."
textual_basis: At 487c, Socrates identifies himself as knowing these four as a group of wisdom-companions. Two receive demotic identification (Aphidna, Cholargos) and one a patronymic (son of Androtion), conforming to Athenian prosopographical naming conventions.
limits: Does not establish whether the four are historical or fictional, nor whether they appear elsewhere in Plato.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0188
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 487b-487c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 487b-487c
  start_marker: 487b
  end_marker: 487c
  start_char: 80739
  end_char: 81517
  text_sha256: ca14cd47b707232e59cd1d4c0c37d6011c6996670bb038e00d3303afa535f301
greek_terms:
  - ἐπήκουσα
  - βουλευομένων
  - μέχρι ὅποι τὴν σοφίαν ἀσκητέον εἴη
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates reports having once overheard the four wisdom-companions deliberating about how far to pursue the practice of wisdom, embedding a past deliberation within the present dialogue frame.
textual_basis: "At 487c, Socrates uses first-person knowledge-claim and perception verbs to introduce a past scene in which the four debated the proper limits of philosophizing. This creates a second narrative layer: the present conversation reports a past conversation."
limits: Does not establish when or where the overheard deliberation occurred, nor whether Socrates participated or merely listened.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates characterizes two of his interlocutors as deficient in frankness and excessively prone to shame, such that each contradicts himself even about the greatest matters, and contrasts them with Callicles, who possesses sufficient education and goodwill toward Socrates.
textual_basis: At 487b, Socrates describes the two as deficient in parrhesia and more shame-prone than needed, adding that their shame drives them to self-contradiction before many people. At 487b-487c he immediately contrasts Callicles as possessing adequate education and goodwill, setting up Callicles as a superior interlocutor.
limits: Does not specify which two interlocutors are meant, though context suggests Gorgias and Polus. Does not assess whether Socrates' characterization is accurate or ironic.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0190

  - ἰσχυρότερον
  - ταὐτόν
  - διόρισον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates demands that Callicles disambiguate whether 'better,' 'stronger,' and 'more powerful' are the same or different at 488c-d. The demand takes the form of a disjunctive question followed by the imperative 'define this clearly for me.' Socrates prefaces the demand by stating he was unable to understand what Callicles was saying earlier.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates lists three terms and poses a disjunctive question (whether the same definition applies to the better and the stronger) at 488c-d, then issues the imperative for clarification.
limits: |
  This observation records the procedural demand for disambiguation, not whether the disambiguation succeeds or what Socrates' own view is.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0191
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 488c-488d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 488c-488d
  start_marker: 488c
  end_marker: 488d
  start_char: 83287
  end_char: 84161
  text_sha256: f12553091d3efade02137a6c0313da42e4db958d19333e372c6b788a6715fcf5
greek_terms:
  - κρείττους
  - βέλτιον
  - νόμους
  - κατὰ φύσιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  After Callicles answers at 488d that 'stronger' and 'better' are the same, Socrates immediately applies the identification to the many, who are stronger than the one by nature and who set down laws against the one. Socrates thereby derives that the conventional laws of the many are the laws of the stronger, a consequence that threatens Callicles' earlier distinction between nature and convention.
textual_basis: |
  Callicles' answer at 488d and Socrates' immediate pivot at 488d, where he invokes Callicles' own earlier claim that the many set down laws against the one.
limits: |
  The contradiction between the derived claim and Callicles' position is structurally implied but not yet explicitly drawn at 488d. The observation does not resolve whether Callicles can escape the implication.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0192

greek_terms:
  - ἑρμαίῳ
english_gloss: "hermaion: windfall, godsend"
observation: |
  Socrates tells Callicles that meeting him is like finding a hermaion (unexpected gift from the gods), and that whatever Callicles agrees to about the opinions of Socrates' soul will be the truth itself.
textual_basis: |
  At 486e-487a, Socrates says he has encountered Callicles as a hermaion and that if Callicles agrees with what Socrates' soul opines, those things will be the very truth. The exaggerated elevation of Callicles' agreement to the criterion of truth carries the markers of mock-deference.
limits: |
  The passage does not specify whether Socrates is sincere or ironic; the label records only the textual pattern of hyperbolic deference.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0193

greek_terms:
  - ἐπιστήμην τε καὶ εὔνοιαν καὶ παρρησίαν
english_gloss: knowledge, goodwill, and frank speech
observation: |
  Socrates states that someone who will adequately test a soul concerning living rightly requires three qualities: knowledge (episteme), goodwill (eunoia), and frank speech (parrhesia). He attributes all three to Callicles.
textual_basis: |
  At 487a, Socrates enumerates a triad of criteria — episteme, eunoia, parrhesia — as necessary for testing a soul, and then applies them to Callicles, contrasting him with others who lack one or more.
limits: |
  The passage establishes criteria for elenctic testing but does not demonstrate their application here; it does not claim that Callicles actually possesses these qualities, only that Socrates asserts he does.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  Socrates includes parrhesia (frank speech) among the necessary qualities for testing a soul, and contrasts Callicles, who has it, with wise men who refuse to speak the truth because they do not care about Socrates.
textual_basis: |
  At 487a, parrhesia is listed as the third criterion for adequate testing, and Socrates specifies that some who are wise do not speak the truth because of lack of care, implying a communicative norm the dialogue will enforce.
limits: |
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0195
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 490a-490b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 490a-490b
  start_marker: 490a
  end_marker: 490b
  start_char: 86510
  end_char: 87313
  text_sha256: 91929b21593dbd5dbab421e2fbbd7bf4d055782281822d6454d5a49e9770bf99
greek_terms:
  - κρείττων
  - βελτίων
  - φρονιμώτερον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates recasts Callicles' natural justice claim from a thesis about the superior seizing more into a quantitative formula: one intelligent man (heis phronon) is stronger than ten thousand unintelligent ones. He prefaces this with an explicit disclaimer that he is not hunting words (ou rhemati thereuo) and then introduces a hypothetical scene at 490b in which the criterion shifts from strength to intelligence plus expertise (a doctor among a crowd with shared provisions)."
textual_basis: "At 490a Socrates reformulates Callicles' position: 'one man of intelligence is stronger than ten thousand without intelligence, according to your account' (heis phronon murion me phronounton kreitton estin kata ton son logon). He adds the phrase 'and I am not hunting a word' (ou rhemati thereuo). Callicles then restates his thesis explicitly as what is just by nature: 'the better and more intelligent both rule and have more than the inferior' (to beltio onta kai phronimoteron kai archein kai pleon echein ton phauloteron). At 490b Socrates immediately shifts to a hypothetical scenario featuring a doctor among a crowd with food and drink, testing whether intelligence (being phronimoteros about these matters) makes one better (beltion) and stronger (kreitton) regarding those specific provisions."
limits: The observation records a procedural move, not a completed refutation. It does not assess whether Socrates distorts Callicles' position or whether the hypothetical scenario is a fair test.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0196
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 490a-490b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 490a-490b
  start_marker: 490a
  end_marker: 490b
  start_char: 86510
  end_char: 87313
  text_sha256: 91929b21593dbd5dbab421e2fbbd7bf4d055782281822d6454d5a49e9770bf99
greek_terms:
  - φύσει
  - τὸ δίκαιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Callicles restates his thesis at 490a in explicit nature-convention language: he claims that it is just by nature (to dikaion einai phusei) for the better and more intelligent to rule and have more. This aligns justice with a natural hierarchy of intelligence rather than with conventional equality."
textual_basis: "At 490a, after Socrates' reformulation, Callicles confirms and restates: 'I believe that this is the just by nature: that the better and more intelligent both rule and have more than the inferior' (touto gar oimai ego to dikaion einai phusei, to beltio onta kai phronimoteron kai archein kai pleon echein ton phauloteron). The phrase dikaion einai phusei explicitly contrasts justice by nature with any conventional standard."
limits: This observation records Callicles' stated position, not Socrates' response to it. It does not evaluate whether the nature-convention framework is valid or whether Callicles elsewhere qualifies this claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0197
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 490a-490b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 490a-490b
  start_marker: 490a
  end_marker: 490b
  start_char: 86510
  end_char: 87313
  text_sha256: 91929b21593dbd5dbab421e2fbbd7bf4d055782281822d6454d5a49e9770bf99
greek_terms:
  - ἰατρὸς
  - σιτία
  - ποτά
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 490b Socrates introduces a doctor (iatros) as a craft-expert within a hypothetical scenario about shared provisions (sitia and pota) to test whether being more intelligent about a specific domain qualifies someone as better (beltion) and stronger (kreitton) for purposes of that domain. This deploys the craft analogy to delimit the scope of Callicles' claim: technical intelligence in a specific area confers superiority only regarding that area's resources."
textual_basis: "At 490b Socrates constructs a scene: many people gathered with common food and drink, of varied strength, and one among them is 'more intelligent about these things, being a doctor' (heis de hemon e phronimoteros peri tauta, iatros on). He asks whether this person, being more intelligent, is also better and stronger 'regarding these things' (beltion kai kreitton estai eis tauta). Callicles assents fully (panu ge)."
limits: This observation records the introduction of a craft-analogy test. It does not track whether the test succeeds or fails as a refutation of Callicles' broader claim about natural justice. The doctor hypothetical is limited to food and drink, not political rule.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0198
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 486a-486b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - ἥτις εὐφυῆ λαβοῦσα τέχνη φῶτα ἔθηκε χείρονα
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Callicles closes his critique of Socrates' philosophic life by quoting a poetic verse asserting that a craft (techne) which takes a naturally well-endowed man makes him worse, unable to help himself or anyone else from the greatest dangers.
textual_basis: |
  At 486b, Callicles introduces the quotation with a formula that embeds it as evidence for his claim that philosophy is not sophon (wise). The verse is treated as authoritative support for the practical indictment of the philosophic life he has just elaborated.
limits: |
  The observation records the use of a poetic quotation as argumentative support; it does not assess whether Callicles accurately characterizes philosophy or whether the verse is correctly attributed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0199
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 486a-486b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 486a-486b
  start_marker: 486a
  end_marker: 486b
  start_char: 78328
  end_char: 79239
  text_sha256: 8b646ceb364770452764ca236903162c3b0ae2c38bd423318b450743d86e7745
greek_terms:
  - δεσμωτήριον
  - δικαστήριον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Callicles constructs a hypothetical scenario in which Socrates (or anyone like him) is seized and dragged to prison on a false charge, then brought before a court with a base prosecutor seeking the death penalty, in order to demonstrate the practical uselessness of the philosophic life.
textual_basis: |
  At 486a-b, Callicles introduces the scenario with a conditional (ei tis sou labomenos ... apagoi) and develops the imagined sequence through arrest (desmōtērion), court appearance (dikastērion), and capital condemnation, all framed to expose what he presents as philosophy's failure to equip a person for self-preservation.
limits: |
  The observation records the use of a hypothetical scenario as a rhetorical device; it does not assess the validity of Callicles' argument or whether the scenario fairly represents Socratic practice.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0201
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 489b-489c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 489b-489c
  start_marker: 489b
  end_marker: 489c
  start_char: 84879
  end_char: 85714
  text_sha256: 7adcdff0757665d90b5c690214fe774cbe97b50d7c47c31f70db0f522976bcde
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles insists that he has already declared 'better' (beltion) and 'stronger' (kreitton) to be the same, rejecting Socrates' attempt to pry them apart. He preemptively denies that 'stronger' means a mob of slaves and worthless men whose only strength is bodily force.
textual_basis: At 489c Callicles says he has long been saying (ou palai soi lego) that he asserts beltion and kreitton are the same thing (tauton). He then piles on a hypothetical scenario involving a rabble (surphetos) of slaves and men of no worth asserting something as customary (nomima), making clear this is not what he means by 'stronger.' This forestalls a Socratic refutation by narrowing the interpretation of his own term before Socrates can exploit an ambiguity.
limits: The passage shows Callicles clarifying his earlier position under pressure, but does not establish whether Socrates genuinely misunderstood it or was deliberately exploiting an ambiguity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0202
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 489b-489c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 489b-489c
  start_marker: 489b
  end_marker: 489c
  start_char: 84879
  end_char: 85714
  text_sha256: 7adcdff0757665d90b5c690214fe774cbe97b50d7c47c31f70db0f522976bcde
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles interrupts the dialectical exchange with an ad hominem outburst, calling Socrates a man who will not stop talking nonsense (phluaron). Socrates then responds with mock-deferential address (o sophotate Kallikleis) and a procedural restart (eien... houto legeis).
textual_basis: "Callicles breaks out of the question-and-answer format to deliver an accusation (489b-c), then Socrates uses the particles eien ('well then') and the address o sophotate Kallikleis at 489c to absorb the outburst and reset the question: 'Do you mean it this way?' (houto legeis). The pattern is: interruption by respondent, followed by questioner's procedural restart with mock deference."
limits: This observation captures the dramatic and procedural structure of the exchange, not the logical content of either side's position.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0203

observation: Callicles explicitly accuses Socrates of irony, and Socrates counters by accusing Callicles of having been ironic first, swearing by Zethus.
textual_basis: At 489e, Callicles tells Socrates he is being ironic. Socrates responds at 489e by swearing by Zethus that Callicles was being ironic toward him just now.
limits: This records the explicit mutual irony accusation; it does not determine who is actually being ironic or what Socratic irony entails.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 489e, after Socrates asks who Callicles means by the better, Callicles answers with a synonym meaning the better ones. Socrates objects that Callicles is only saying names and revealing nothing, then proposes a candidate meaning the more intelligent, which Callicles accepts emphatically.
limits: This records the synonym-evasion and candidate-proposal move; it does not assess whether 'the more intelligent' captures Callicles' actual position.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0206
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 492c-492d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 492c-492d
  start_marker: 492c
  end_marker: 492d
  start_char: 91554
  end_char: 92359
  text_sha256: 141f1c7965d15c5bd46f432551d72f9d9342641fc24771e4908ac73013146eba
greek_terms:
  - ἀκολασία
  - ἐλευθερία
  - ἀρετή
  - εὐδαιμονία
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 492c Callicles proposes a direct definition: luxury (tryphe), licentiousness (akolasia), and freedom (eleutheria), when they have support (epikouria), are virtue (arete) and happiness (eudaimonia). He explicitly frames this as the truth that Socrates claims to pursue."
textual_basis: 'Callicles marks his statement with the formula "by the truth which you say you pursue, it is as follows" (alētheia hēn phēs su diōkein), then asserts an identity claim: these three things are virtue and happiness. He contrasts them with "fancy ornaments" (kallōpismata) and "conventions contrary to nature" (ta para phusin sunthēmata), reinforcing that this is a positive definition offered in the elenctic context.'
limits: This observation records Callicles' definition claim at 492c. It does not evaluate its truth, nor claim it is a definition-ladder attempt in the Socratic sense. It does not address the earlier definitional moves by Gorgias or Polus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0207
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 492c-492d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 492c-492d
  start_marker: 492c
  end_marker: 492d
  start_char: 91554
  end_char: 92359
  text_sha256: 141f1c7965d15c5bd46f432551d72f9d9342641fc24771e4908ac73013146eba
greek_terms:
  - παρρησιαζόμενος
  - οἱ ἄλλοι διανοοῦνται μέν, λέγειν δὲ οὐκ ἐθέλουσιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 492d Socrates praises Callicles for speaking with frankness (parrhesiazomenos) and declares that Callicles now says openly what others think but are unwilling to say. Socrates then insists Callicles must not slacken, so that how one should live may truly become clear.
textual_basis: Socrates says Callicles is "not ignobly" (ouk agennōs) pursuing the argument with frankness, and states "clearly you now say what others think but are unwilling to say" (saphōs gar su nun legeis ha hoi alloi dianoountai men, legein de ouk ethelousin). He immediately ties this to the goal of making manifest "how one must live" (pōs biōteon).
limits: This observation records Socrates' explicit praise of Callicles' candor as a dialectical device. It does not claim Socrates is insincere or that the praise is ironic. It does not resolve whether others actually hold the view Callicles expresses.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0208
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 492c-492d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 492c-492d
  start_marker: 492c
  end_marker: 492d
  start_char: 91554
  end_char: 92359
  text_sha256: 141f1c7965d15c5bd46f432551d72f9d9342641fc24771e4908ac73013146eba
greek_terms:
  - παρὰ φύσιν
  - συνθήματα
  - τὰ καλλωπίσματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 492c Callicles contrasts what is according to nature (phusis) with human conventions (sunthēmata). He dismisses the conventional virtues as "fancy ornaments" (kallōpismata) that are "contrary to nature" (para phusin) and "worthless rubbish" (phluaria kai oudenos axia).
textual_basis: Callicles labels conventional restraints on desire as "contrary to nature conventions of human beings" (ta para phusin sunthēmata anthrōpōn) and reduces them to "rubbish worth nothing" (phluaria kai oudenos axia). This directly invokes the phusis/nomos contrast that structures the entire exchange with Callicles.
limits: This observation records the occurrence of the phusis/nomos antithesis at 492c. It does not trace the theme across the dialogue, analyze its philosophical merit, or claim that Callicles invented the distinction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0209
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 490c-490e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 490c-490e
  start_marker: 490c
  end_marker: 490e
  start_char: 87313
  end_char: 88576
  text_sha256: 56942491ad65716e0de15fe0c738dc2decbd240c5aeee8dd37c201d0416ad8fa
greek_terms:
  - σιτία
  - ποτὰ
  - ἰατροὺς
  - φλυαρίας
  - οὐ ταῦτα λέγω
  - ἱματίων
  - ὑποδήματα
  - φλυαρεῖς
english_gloss: nonsense, drivel
observation: |
  Callicles rejects Socrates' domain-specific craft examples for interpreting the claim that the superior should have more, treating food, drink, medical, clothing, shoe, and seed cases as irrelevant to his intended claim.
textual_basis: |
  At 490c-490e Callicles dismisses food, drink, and doctor examples as drivel, denies that food or drink is at issue, and then dismisses Socrates' clothing, shoe, and agricultural seed examples in the same way.
limits: |
  This records Callicles' rejection of craft examples in this turn, not a theoretical rejection of craft analogy as a method. It does not prove that Socrates' analogies are invalid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0210
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 492a-492b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 492a-492b
  start_marker: 492a
  end_marker: 492b
  start_char: 90632
  end_char: 91554
  text_sha256: 7b70460634c00de7141024b8c177dfa618d914e75a9710adff2b60e38e993ab3
greek_terms:
  - ἀνδρείαν
  - φρόνησιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles enlists the traditional virtue terms courage (andreia) and wisdom (phronesis) as instruments for serving the greatest desires at 492a, treating them not as ends but as means by which one adequately ministers to appetite.
textual_basis: At 492a, the construction 'through courage and wisdom' uses a preposition of instrumentality (dia + accusative) to position these virtues as the means by which desires are adequately served, reversing their conventional role as governors of appetite.
limits: This observation records the instrumental grammar in this one phrase. It does not claim Callicles systematically instrumentalizes all virtues, nor that he offers a worked-out alternative ethics.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0211
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 492a-492b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 492a-492b
  start_marker: 492a
  end_marker: 492b
  start_char: 90632
  end_char: 91554
  text_sha256: 7b70460634c00de7141024b8c177dfa618d914e75a9710adff2b60e38e993ab3
greek_terms:
  - ἀδυναμίαν
  - ἀνανδρίαν
  - σωφροσύνην
  - δικαιοσύνην
  - ἀκολασίαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles claims at 492a-492b that the many praise moderation (sophrosyne) and justice (dikaiosyne) not from genuine valuation, but to conceal their own incapacity (adynamia) to fulfill their desires, and that they do so through their own cowardice (anandria). He further claims they call licentiousness (akolasia) shameful out of shame (aischyne) about their weakness, thereby enslaving those who are better by nature.
textual_basis: At 492a, Callicles states the many blame the intemperate through shame, concealing their own incapacity. At 492a-492b, he says through their own cowardice they praise moderation and justice. The passage chains shame, concealment, incapacity, and cowardice into an unmasking explanation of conventional moral language.
limits: This records Callicles' claim about the psychology of the many, not an established fact within the dialogue. The observation identifies the structure of the argument (psychological unmasking of moral language as resentment-concealment), not its validity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0212
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 492e-493a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 492e-493a
  start_marker: 492e
  end_marker: 493a
  start_char: 92359
  end_char: 93133
  text_sha256: d0236b61749a33ec963a4333358e0e931d9b3dcddedd9ddfc98bdefe1c51a0d1
greek_terms:
  - σῶμά ἐστιν ἡμῖν σῆμα
english_gloss: the body is our tomb
observation: Socrates introduces the body-tomb formula at 493a, attributing it to an unnamed wise man (perhaps Sicilian or Italian) and presenting it as something he has heard rather than a claim he asserts on his own authority.
textual_basis: At 493a Socrates says he has heard from the wise that the body is our tomb. He frames this as reported wisdom, using the phrase 'I have heard' and attributing it to a clever man (kompsos aner), possibly Sicilian or Italian.
limits: This observation records the body-tomb formula and its reported-speech framing. It does not determine whether Socrates endorses the formula, whether it is Orphic or Pythagorean in origin, or how it relates to Callicles' hedonism.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0213
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 492e-493a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 492e-493a
  start_marker: 492e
  end_marker: 493a
  start_char: 92359
  end_char: 93133
  text_sha256: d0236b61749a33ec963a4333358e0e931d9b3dcddedd9ddfc98bdefe1c51a0d1
greek_terms:
  - εἰ τὸ ζῆν μέν ἐστι κατθανεῖν
  - τὸ κατθανεῖν δὲ ζῆν
english_gloss: Who knows if living is being dead, and being dead is living?
observation: Socrates quotes two lines from Euripides at the 492e-493a boundary that invert the conventional relation between life and death, using the quotation to pivot toward the body-tomb mythic material.
textual_basis: At 492e Socrates says he would not be surprised if Euripides speaks truly in these lines and then quotes the two-line fragment directly. The Euripidean quotation functions as a poetic authority bridging Callicles' claim about the good life to the mythic claim that we may in truth be dead.
limits: This observation records the explicit poetic quotation and its bridge function. It does not claim that Socrates endorses the Euripidean inversion literally, nor does it interpret the fragment's original context in Euripides.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0214
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 492e-493a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 492e-493a
  start_marker: 492e
  end_marker: 493a
  start_char: 92359
  end_char: 93133
  text_sha256: d0236b61749a33ec963a4333358e0e931d9b3dcddedd9ddfc98bdefe1c51a0d1
greek_terms:
  - πίθον
  - πιθανόν
  - ἀμυήτους
english_gloss: jar, persuasive, convincing, uninitiated
observation: Socrates reports a mythic wordplay at 493a in which a clever man named the soul's appetitive part a jar (pithos) by playing on its persuadable and convincing character, and called the foolish uninitiated.
textual_basis: Socrates describes a clever man who, slightly altering the name, called the appetitive part of the soul a jar because of its being persuadable and convincing. The uninitiated are contrasted with the initiated in this mythic register.
limits: This observation records the presence of etymological wordplay within a mythic register. It does not determine whether the wordplay is Plato's invention, authentic Orphic-Pythagorean material, or Socrates' own improvisation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0215
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 494a-494b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 494a-494b
  start_marker: 494a
  end_marker: 494b
  start_char: 94777
  end_char: 95571
  text_sha256: c6cd35cd9dec6f4de5f0d55b326d8d575e3ad37e9e6d1f56685695b0239c23da
greek_terms:
  - ἀκολάστου
  - κοσμίου
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates poses the choice between the intemperate life and the orderly life as an exhaustive forced alternative, asking Callicles whether he says the intemperate man is happier than the orderly man.
textual_basis: At 494a Socrates asks Callicles whether, given that each life is of such a kind, he says the life of the intemperate man is happier than that of the orderly man. The question is framed as a binary choice with no third option offered.
limits: The observation records the form of the question, not whether the forced alternative is fair or exhaustive. Socrates may be testing a position Callicles has already implied rather than imposing an arbitrary dichotomy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0216
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 494a-494b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 494a-494b
  start_marker: 494a
  end_marker: 494b
  start_char: 94777
  end_char: 95571
  text_sha256: c6cd35cd9dec6f4de5f0d55b326d8d575e3ad37e9e6d1f56685695b0239c23da
greek_terms:
  - οὐ πείθεις
  - ὦ Σώκρατες
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles explicitly rejects Socrates' attempt to persuade him that the orderly life is better than the intemperate one, refusing to concede the conclusion.
textual_basis: "At 494a Callicles responds with a direct refusal: you do not persuade me, Socrates. He then defends his position by arguing that the fulfilled man has no pleasure and lives like a stone, insisting that the pleasant life consists in having as much flow in as possible."
limits: This records Callicles' refusal at this point in the argument. It does not claim that he never concedes anything or that his position remains unchanged throughout the dialogue. The rejection is tied to a specific attempted persuasion about the comparative value of the two lives.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0217

  - χαραδριοῦ
  - νεκροῦ
  - λίθου
  - ἐπιρρεῖν
  - ἀπιὸν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates counters Callicles' stone and corpse image with a plover-life comparison.
textual_basis: At 494b-494c Socrates moves from inflow and outflow to the plover image, in contrast with the prior stone and corpse image.
limits: The repaired span corrects the source-ref mismatch; the old span missed the plover comparison.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0218
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 493b-493c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 493b-493c
  start_marker: 493b
  end_marker: 493c
  start_char: 93133
  end_char: 93925
  text_sha256: 0e38dd0c603172cb3669a1500af990d2869311e00efb58dac0723abd2e38bc36
greek_terms:
  - κόσκινον
  - ἀπληστίαν
  - ἀπιστίαν
  - λήθην
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates recounts a doctrine that likens the appetitive part of the soul to a pierced jar and a sieve. This imagery is attributed to a prior speaker and is placed in an underworld context (the uninitiated in Hades), but the argumentative work it does in 493b-493c is to support Socrates' exhortation to choose a disciplined life over an insatiable one.
textual_basis: |
  At 493b Socrates speaks of the appetitive part of the soul as "not retentive" and likens it to a pierced jar because of its insatiability. At 493c he explains that the sieve represents the soul of the senseless, unable to hold anything because of faithlessness and forgetfulness. These objects function as analogical images that carry the main argument.
limits: |
  The observation does not claim that the jar-sieve imagery is a full myth or esoteric doctrine. It only records that Socrates uses crafted-object analogies to depict a psychic condition, and notes the mention of an underworld context without analyzing the afterlife frame.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0219

observation: Socrates pressures Callicles' pleasure-good claim through the scratching example.
textual_basis: At 494c-494d Socrates introduces itching and scratching while warning against shame, and Callicles maintains the pleasant-life answer.
limits: This records local pressure in the exchange, not the final refutation of the pleasure thesis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0220
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 496a-496b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 496a-496b
  start_marker: 496a
  end_marker: 496b
  start_char: 98703
  end_char: 99396
  text_sha256: 775343854cab0b635e35a242613e77d1dae408b7d82883f61607e0143a59fbf0
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles gives eight consecutive short assents across 496a-496b as Socrates moves through examples from physical conditions to ethical ones.
textual_basis: The responses are "how could it not be," "not at all," "least of all," "certainly," "I say so," "yes," "absolutely," and "entirely, of course" in rapid succession across the segment.
limits: This observation records only the assent density; it does not assess whether Callicles understands the implications for his earlier position.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0221
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 496a-496b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 496a-496b
  start_marker: 496a
  end_marker: 496b
  start_char: 98703
  end_char: 99396
  text_sha256: 775343854cab0b635e35a242613e77d1dae408b7d82883f61607e0143a59fbf0
greek_terms:
  - ἐν μέρει
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates establishes that opposite physical conditions are acquired and lost "in turn" using eye-disease/health, strength/weakness, and speed/slowness as examples, then explicitly extends the pattern to ethical goods and happiness and their opposites at 496b.
textual_basis: At 496a Socrates runs through three pairs of physical opposites, obtaining assent at each step, before asking at 496b whether goods and happiness and their opposites are also acquired and lost in turn.
limits: This observation records the analogical extension procedure; it does not claim that the argument succeeds in establishing the ethical point.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0222
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 496e-497a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 496e-497a
  start_marker: 496e
  end_marker: 497a
  start_char: 100149
  end_char: 100992
  text_sha256: e4ca814ff03570fcf23b31c9ff93dafabb624223960ab015b72a1def02282c3e
greek_terms:
  - ἀκκίζῃ
  - σοφίζῃ
  - ληρεῖς
english_gloss: Callicles accuses Socrates of sophistry, Socrates replies that Callicles is play-acting coy and tells him to go forward so he sees that his previous talk was foolish.
observation: At 496e-497a, Callicles charges Socrates with verbal trickery, and Socrates immediately turns the charge back by claiming Callicles is feigning reluctance and dismissing his earlier position as nonsense.
textual_basis: Callicles says he does not know what Socrates is playing at by sophisms at 497a. Socrates retorts that Callicles knows but plays coy, and tells him to go forward still into what lies ahead so that he may know how wise... (using a deleted phrase noting Callicles talks foolishly). The exchange turns a procedural complaint into an accusation of insincerity.
limits: This observation does not determine whether Socrates is actually engaging in sophistry or whether the argument is valid. It records the in-text charge of mockery and the counter-charge of feigned reluctance.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0223
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 496c-496d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 496c-496d
  start_marker: 496c
  end_marker: 496d
  start_char: 99396
  end_char: 100149
  text_sha256: 2cbbe7fc0fa8c378c3700f14f7b9d475679f2c632396e0a61c109b9050e6d2e2
greek_terms:
  - ἔνδειαν
  - ἐπιθυμίαν
  - ἀνιαρόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates builds an assent chain from particular cases (hunger, then thirst) to a universal generalization that all lack (endeia) and desire (epithymia) are painful at 496c-496d.
textual_basis: Socrates secures Callicles' agreement that hunger itself (to peinen) is painful at 496c, confirms the same for thirst (to dipsen) at 496d, and then asks whether Callicles concedes that all lack and desire are painful. Callicles replies with 'homologo' at 496d.
limits: This records the assent-chain structure; it does not evaluate whether the generalization from two cases is valid or whether Callicles' assent is freely given.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0224
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 496c-496d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 496c-496d
  start_marker: 496c
  end_marker: 496d
  start_char: 99396
  end_char: 100149
  text_sha256: 2cbbe7fc0fa8c378c3700f14f7b9d475679f2c632396e0a61c109b9050e6d2e2
greek_terms:
  - ἀλλὰ μὴ ἐρώτα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles attempts to halt further questioning by saying 'alla me erota' (but do not ask) at 496d, immediately after conceding the universal claim about lack and desire.
textual_basis: After Socrates asks whether to continue questioning or whether Callicles concedes that all lack and desire are painful, Callicles replies 'homologo, alla me erota' at 496d.
limits: This records a procedural pushback in the turn structure; it does not assess Callicles' motivation or whether Socrates respects the request.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0225
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 496c-496d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 496c-496d
  start_marker: 496c
  end_marker: 496d
  start_char: 99396
  end_char: 100149
  text_sha256: 2cbbe7fc0fa8c378c3700f14f7b9d475679f2c632396e0a61c109b9050e6d2e2
greek_terms:
  - ἀπαλλάττεται
  - ἔχει
  - ἀγαθὸν
  - κακόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates frames the argument by stating a conditional principle at 496c: if something is simultaneously released-from (apallattetai) and possessed (echei), it cannot be both the good and the bad together."
textual_basis: Socrates states at 496c that if they find things from which a person is simultaneously released and which he simultaneously has, these clearly cannot be both the good and the bad. He asks Callicles to agree after careful consideration (eu mala skepsamenos apokrinou).
limits: This records the use of a conditional methodological premise; it does not evaluate the premise's truth or how it functions in the broader argument about pleasure and the good.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0226
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 494e-495a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 494e-495a
  start_marker: 494e
  end_marker: 495a
  start_char: 96288
  end_char: 97186
  text_sha256: 7513c7e98c9f1b177af946f9f1d6a4c43b269b4f496b7ece0d9b80d87ff865eb
greek_terms:
  - κεφάλαιον
  - ὁ τῶν κιναίδων βίος
english_gloss: the sum, the life of catamites
observation: Socrates identifies the life of catamites as the logical endpoint of Callicles' position that pleasure is the good, and asks whether Callicles will dare to call such people happy.
textual_basis: At 494e Socrates states that the sum of such pleasures is the life of catamites, and asks whether this life is not terrible, shameful, and wretched, or whether Callicles will dare to call them happy if they have an abundance of what they need.
limits: This observation notes the reductio structure but does not assess whether the catamite example fairly represents Callicles' position or whether the reductio is logically valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0227
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 494e-495a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 494e-495a
  start_marker: 494e
  end_marker: 495a
  start_char: 96288
  end_char: 97186
  text_sha256: 7513c7e98c9f1b177af946f9f1d6a4c43b269b4f496b7ece0d9b80d87ff865eb
greek_terms:
  - οὐκ αἰσχύνῃ
english_gloss: Aren't you ashamed?
observation: Callicles objects to the direction of the argument by accusing Socrates of bringing the discussion into shameful territory rather than addressing the argument's substance.
textual_basis: At 495a Callicles asks Socrates whether he is not ashamed to bring the discussion to such topics. Socrates then redirects responsibility to the one who holds the principle that all pleasures are good without distinction.
limits: This observation identifies a procedural objection based on shame. It does not evaluate whether Callicles' objection is justified or whether Socrates is being intentionally provocative.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0228
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 494e-495a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 494e-495a
  start_marker: 494e
  end_marker: 495a
  start_char: 96288
  end_char: 97186
  text_sha256: 7513c7e98c9f1b177af946f9f1d6a4c43b269b4f496b7ece0d9b80d87ff865eb
greek_terms:
  - ἀνομολογούμενος
  - διαφθείρεις
  - παρὰ τὰ δοκοῦντα σαυτῷ
english_gloss: inconsistent, you corrupt, contrary to your own beliefs
observation: Callicles maintains that pleasure and the good are the same not from conviction but to preserve argumentative consistency, and Socrates accuses him of corrupting their earlier arguments by speaking contrary to his own beliefs.
textual_basis: At 495a Callicles says he asserts the identity of pleasure and good so that his argument may not be inconsistent. Socrates responds that Callicles is corrupting their earlier arguments and will no longer adequately examine the truth with him if he speaks contrary to his own beliefs.
limits: This observation notes the tension between argumentative consistency and genuine belief. It does not claim that Callicles' original position was correct or that his retreat is a concession.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0229
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 497d-497e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 497d-497e
  start_marker: 497d
  end_marker: 497e
  start_char: 101797
  end_char: 102644
  text_sha256: e624e3d4651f9e515731752b12981e9b254e3ff57ad1c7925528a064caada47e
greek_terms:
  - ὡμολόγεις
  - τἀγαθὰ τοῖς ἡδέσιν
  - ἑτέρων ὄντων
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates recalls Callicles' prior admission that goods and evils do not cease simultaneously and uses it to force a distinction between the good and the pleasant. He states that pleasures cease simultaneously with their pains while goods and evils do not, and concludes that therefore the good and the pleasant are not the same thing.
textual_basis: |
  At 497d Socrates says "as you agreed; now you don't agree?" (invoking the prior concession), then states the asymmetrical simultaneity premise and draws the conclusion that goods are not identical to pleasures.
limits: |
  This observation notes the formal move of recalling a prior concession for refutation; it does not assess whether the premise about simultaneity is sound or whether Callicles' prior admission was unforced.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0230
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 497d-497e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 497d-497e
  start_marker: 497d
  end_marker: 497e
  start_char: 101797
  end_char: 102644
  text_sha256: e624e3d4651f9e515731752b12981e9b254e3ff57ad1c7925528a064caada47e
greek_terms:
  - ἀγαθῶν παρουσίᾳ
  - κάλλος παρῇ
  - ἄφρονας καὶ δειλούς
  - ἀνδρείους καὶ φρονίμους
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates proposes a criterion for the application of the term "good": good men are called good by the presence of good things, just as beautiful people are those in whom beauty is present. He then tests this criterion by asking whether Callicles would apply "good" to foolish and cowardly men. Callicles confirms he applies it only to the courageous and wise.
textual_basis: |
  At 497e Socrates states the criterion ("do you not call the good good by the presence of good things, just as the beautiful are those in whom beauty is present?") and immediately applies it to test whether foolish and cowardly men qualify. Callicles' answers confirm the criterion's discriminatory force.
limits: |
  This observation records the explicit statement of a classification criterion. It does not evaluate whether the criterion is adequate or whether the analogy to beauty is sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0231
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 497d-497e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 497d-497e
  start_marker: 497d
  end_marker: 497e
  start_char: 101797
  end_char: 102644
  text_sha256: e624e3d4651f9e515731752b12981e9b254e3ff57ad1c7925528a064caada47e
greek_terms:
  - παῖδα ἀνόητον χαίροντα
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates introduces a counterexample to the claim that pleasure and good are identical: a foolish child and a foolish man experiencing pleasure. If pleasure were the same as good, a foolish person experiencing pleasure would be experiencing good and thus be good. But Callicles does not classify foolish people as good, so pleasure and good cannot be identical.
textual_basis: |
  At 497e Socrates asks whether Callicles has seen a foolish child or a foolish man experiencing pleasure. Callicles answers yes to both. The counterexample is set up by the preceding criterion (good men are those with good present) and Callicles' admission that foolish and cowardly men are not called good.
limits: |
  This observation records the counterexample structure. It does not assess whether the inference from "foolish person experiences pleasure" to "pleasure is not the good" is logically sound, or whether Callicles could have rejected the classificatory criterion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0232
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 498e-499a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 498e-499a
  start_marker: 498e
  end_marker: 499a
  start_char: 104309
  end_char: 105241
  text_sha256: 05b84f27fd03bc48595e5220767c75af71e58d50d21dd19d256929e88603dcd8
greek_terms:
  - συλλόγισαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates halts the rapid assent sequence at the end of 498e to announce a collaborative recapitulation, framing it with a proverbial formula about saying and examining fine things twice or even three times.
textual_basis: At 498e-499a Socrates instructs Callicles to reckon up together with him and invokes a proverbial saying that it is fine to say and examine fine things twice and even three times before beginning a step-by-step restatement of the agreed premises. This marks a procedural turn from forward-moving elenchus to stocktaking summary.
limits: This observation records only the procedural marker of recapitulation. It does not evaluate whether the premises Socrates restates were fairly obtained or whether the eventual conclusion follows from them.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0233
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 499a-499b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 499a-499b
  start_marker: 499a
  end_marker: 499b
  start_char: 104829
  end_char: 105674
  text_sha256: de524e954cbb7cc322190babec2ec16946308a976628798b0c82143ea380f92c
greek_terms:
  - ὁμοίως
  - κακὸς καὶ ἀγαθὸς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates draws a reductio from Callicles' hedonist assents: if joy marks goodness, distress marks badness, and good and bad people can rejoice and be distressed alike, the bad person becomes good and bad like the good person, or even more good."
textual_basis: At 499a-499b Socrates restates the agreed pairings of prudent/brave with good, foolish/cowardly with bad, rejoicing with good, and grieving with bad. He then applies the admission that good and bad men rejoice and grieve similarly, or that the bad man does so more, to produce the paradoxical conclusion.
limits: This records the local reductio conclusion. It does not assess whether Callicles' hedonist premises are fairly represented or whether the argument equivocates.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 498a-498b Callicles gives compact replies as Socrates builds the comparison across wisdom and courage cases.
limits: This records the compact chain; it does not inflate the passage into a precise large count of assents.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - τρόπον ζῆν

observation: Socrates tells Callicles not to answer in play or receive Socrates' words as play, because the question is how one should live.
textual_basis: At 500b-500d the play warning is joined to the seriousness of the life-choice question.
limits: "This corrects the prior reversed framing: Socrates rejects the play frame rather than adopting it."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0236
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 500a-500b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates recapitulates a previous distinction between two kinds of preparation, those aimed only at pleasure, ignorant of the better and worse, and those that know the good and the bad. He assigns cooking to the first class as an experience not an art, and medicine to the second as an art.
textual_basis: At 500a-500b Socrates recalls what he said to Polus and Gorgias and restates the dichotomy explicitly, attaching cooking to the pleasure-aiming side and medicine to the good-aiming side.
limits: This observation does not assess whether cooking is consistently treated elsewhere in the dialogue; it tracks only the recapitulation here. It does not claim that this classification is Socrates's own final view rather than a dialectical move.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0237
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 499d-499e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 499d-499e
  start_marker: 499d
  end_marker: 499e
  start_char: 106071
  end_char: 106925
  text_sha256: 2e15b7284e38f6b0d8a254a6b287923a8080af179eda0afa4ebe86ae92792616
greek_terms:
  - ὠφέλιμοι
  - βλαβεραί
  - χρησταί
  - πονηραί
  - ἀγαθόν
  - τέλος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates introduces a criterion for classifying pleasures and pains: beneficial (ophelymai) pleasures are good (agathai), harmful (blaberai) ones are bad (kakai), and the same distinction holds for pains (chrestai vs. ponerai)."
textual_basis: At 499d Socrates asks whether beneficial pleasures are good and harmful ones bad, then extends the same binary to pains at 499e. Callicles assents to each step.
limits: This only establishes a classification schema; it does not determine which specific pleasures and pains fall into each category, nor does it define the good itself.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0238
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 499d-499e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 499d-499e

  text_sha256: 2e15b7284e38f6b0d8a254a6b287923a8080af179eda0afa4ebe86ae92792616
greek_terms:
  - τέλος
  - ἀγαθόν
  - πράξεων
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates recalls a prior conclusion from the exchange with Polus — that all actions are to be done for the sake of the good (telos ... to agathon) — and asks Callicles whether he too agrees with this teleological premise.
textual_basis: "At 499e Socrates explicitly invokes the earlier agreement with Polus: 'for the sake of the good all things seemed to us to be to be done, if you remember, both to me and to Polus.' He then asks Callicles to confirm the same principle."
limits: This observation records the appeal to prior agreement as an argumentative move; it does not assess whether Callicles genuinely shares the premise or merely assents under dialectical pressure.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0239
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 499d-499e

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - ὑγίειαν
  - ἰσχὺν
  - ἀρετὴν τοῦ σώματος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates uses bodily pleasures as a domain for testing the good/bad distinction: pleasures that produce health, strength, or other bodily excellence (arete) are good, those producing the opposite are bad. This establishes a pattern that can be transferred to the psychic domain."
textual_basis: At 499d-e Socrates illustrates his classification with reference to the pleasures of eating and drinking discussed earlier, asking whether those that produce health, strength, or other bodily excellence in the body are good, and those producing the contrary are bad.
limits: Socrates uses bodily pleasure as a parallel domain for classification, but the passage does not yet make the transfer to pleasures of the soul explicit.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0240

  - μεμνησόμεθα
  - Ἀλωπεκῆθεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates recapitulates Callicles' thesis before the elenctic examination begins.
textual_basis: "At 495d Socrates marks the moment with a formula meaning \"let us remember these things\" and restates Callicles' position: that the pleasant and the good are the same, while knowledge and courage are different from each other and from the good. Both speakers are identified by deme (Callicles of Acharnae, Socrates of Alopeke) within the recapitulation."
limits: Does not indicate whether the recapitulation is accurate or whether it will be accepted by Callicles; the recapitulation is a procedural move before testing.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0241
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 495d-495e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 495d-495e
  start_marker: 495d
  end_marker: 495e
```
