```yaml
  end_char: 98703
  text_sha256: bb4e5e2eae6660092b164fc53af0d120454556fc309849bfe310bb189ad01be0
greek_terms:
  - εὖ πράττοντας
  - κακῶς πράττουσιν
  - ὑγιείας
  - νόσου
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates draws a structural parallel between the opposition of bodily conditions (health and disease) and the opposition of conditions of the person (doing well and doing poorly), using the somatic model to clarify the logic of opposites.
textual_basis: "At 495e Socrates argues conditionally: if doing well and doing poorly are opposite to each other, they must behave like health and disease. He states that a person cannot be healthy and sick at the same time, nor be simultaneously rid of both — implying the same exclusivity holds for the opposed conditions of doing well and doing poorly."
limits: The parallel is presented as conditional and the elenctic conclusion is not drawn within this segment; the observation records the analogy's introduction, not its application.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0242
greek_terms:
  - χαριεῖσθαι
  - τῷ ὄχλῳ
  - ἡδονῆς χάριν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates reopens the question of whether musical and poetic performances aim at the best or only at giving pleasure, asking Callicles whether the entire art of kithara-singing and dithyrambic composition was invented for the sake of pleasure (502a). Callicles assents unconditionally.
textual_basis: Socrates asks at 502a whether kitharodic art and dithyrambic poetry were devised for pleasure (hedones charin), and Callicles answers 'to me, yes' (emoige). This extends the earlier exclusion-of-excellence pattern from rhetoric and aulos-playing to music and lyric poetry.
limits: The passage does not state whether Socrates himself endorses this claim; he is questioning Callicles and has not yet turned to tragedy, which begins at 502b.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0243
greek_terms:
  - τῆς τραγῳδίας ποίησις
  - χαρίζεσθαι
  - διαμάχεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates frames tragedy as a forced alternative: either its serious endeavor is only to gratify spectators, or it fights against them whenever something is pleasant but base, saying and singing what is beneficial even if it causes displeasure (502b)."
textual_basis: "At 502b Socrates poses a dichotomy: is the serious endeavor of tragedy only to please the spectators (charizesthai tois theatais monon), or does it also fight against them (diamachesthai) to speak what is beneficial (ophelimon) even if unpleasant (aedes)? This is a classic forced-alternative question structure."
limits: The observation records only the framing of the question; Callicles' answer begins at 502b and continues beyond the segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0244
greek_terms:
  - Κινησίου
  - Μέλης
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates names two specific musical figures, Cinesias and his father Meles, as evidence in his questioning about whether performers aim at the best or only at pleasure (502a). Meles is cited as having caused pain to the spectators by his singing rather than aiming at the pleasant.
textual_basis: At 502a Socrates asks about Cinesias (Kinesiou) and his father Meles, claiming Meles did not even aim at the pleasant (oude pros to hediston) but caused distress (enia) to the spectators. These named figures serve as concrete examples in the argumentative sequence.
limits: The passage does not provide independent testimony about Cinesias or Meles beyond what Socrates asserts; their function here is as illustrative examples in a questioning sequence.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0245
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 500e-501a

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - ἐμπειρία
  - ἰατρική
  - τέχνη
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates contrasts cookery (opsopoiike) as mere empeiria with medicine (iatrike) as a genuine techne, recapitulating a distinction he made earlier in the dialogue.
textual_basis: At 500e-501a, Socrates recalls his earlier claim that cookery is not a techne but an empeiria, while medicine is a techne because it investigates the nature and cause of what it treats.
limits: This observation does not assess whether the earlier distinction was adequately established; it only notes the recapitulation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0246
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 500e-501a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 500e-501a
  start_marker: 500e
  end_marker: 501a
  start_char: 108727
  end_char: 109348
  text_sha256: 907bb1c6bba80fc4dda559a5b3df403ee16dbbeaf87f22bf2dc00be726b456d5
greek_terms:
  - φύσιν
  - αἰτίαν
  - λόγον
  - δοῦναι
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates states a three-part criterion for distinguishing a genuine techne from mere empeiria: a techne investigates the nature (phusis) of its object, investigates the cause (aitia) of what it does, and can give an account (logon didonai) of each."
textual_basis: At 501a, Socrates says that medicine investigates the nature of what it treats and the cause of what it does, and can give an account of each; cookery fails all three tests by aiming at pleasure without investigating its nature or cause and being entirely without rational account.
limits: This observation records the criteria as stated; it does not assess whether these criteria are sufficient or whether cookery actually fails them.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates recalls a distinction he previously made to Polus and Gorgias and asks Callicles to confirm that what he said then was true, using prior agreement as a premise for the current argument.
textual_basis: At 500e, Socrates asks Callicles to agree and then to confirm what he earlier said to the others, explicitly recalling the cookery-vs-medicine distinction as a prior claim to be reaffirmed.
limits: This observation only notes the procedural move of recalling prior agreement; it does not assess whether Callicles was present for the earlier discussion.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0248
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 501b-501c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - τεχνικαί
  - κολακείαν
  - προμήθειάν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates extends the technikos/kolakeia distinction from bodily practices to practices concerning the soul, asserting that some psychic practices are artful (technikai) with forethought for the best, while others only pursue pleasure without regard for better or worse.
textual_basis: "At 501b, Socrates introduces the parallel: 'consider...whether there are also concerning the soul other such practices, some of which are artful, having some forethought for what is best concerning the soul, while others neglect this...focused only on the pleasure of the soul.' At 501c he restates the classification: 'such a thing is flattery both concerning body and concerning soul and concerning anything else.'"
limits: Does not establish whether Socrates has previously made this extension; the passage asserts the parallel without proving it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0249
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 501b-501c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 501b-501c
  start_marker: 501b
  end_marker: 501c
  start_char: 109348
  end_char: 110220
  text_sha256: 5eb24b63247d618f00b4cb11654825d0934c9a107b0f3442cdb92688c58dc264
greek_terms:
  - συγχωρῶ
  - χαρίσωμαι
  - χαρίζεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles concedes to Socrates' claim about kolakeia but explicitly states he does so to let the argument conclude and to gratify Gorgias, not because he agrees with the position. The verb used for 'gratify' (charisomai) echoes Socrates' use of charizesthai moments earlier to define the activity of kolakeia.
textual_basis: At 501c, Callicles says he concedes 'so that your argument may be completed and I may gratify Gorgias here.' Socrates had just defined kolakeia at 501b-c as that which aims only 'to gratify only, whether for better or worse,' using the same verbal root.
limits: Does not prove Callicles is being ironic or that the lexical echo was intended by Plato; only records the stated motive and the verbal repetition across the two speakers.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0250
source_work: Gorgias

  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates expands the scope of his argument from a single soul to two or many souls, and then to crowds collectively, pressing Callicles to confirm that gratification without concern for the best applies at the collective level as well as the individual.
textual_basis: At 501d Socrates asks whether gratifying without considering the best applies to one soul only or also to two and many; Callicles agrees it applies to both. Socrates then asks about crowds together, and Callicles again assents, establishing that the argument framework operates at the collective level.
limits: This observation records the scope expansion move; it does not evaluate whether the expansion is valid or whether Callicles' assent is warranted.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0251

  - διθυράμβων ποίησις
  - βελτίους γίγνοιντο
  - χαριεῖσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates catalogs public musical and poetic performance practices as candidates for practices that pursue audience pleasure rather than improvement.
textual_basis: At 501e Socrates introduces flute-playing as pursuing pleasure only, then adds contest kithara-playing, chorus teaching, and dithyramb composition. At 502a, the Cinesias example completes the contrast between making listeners better and gratifying the crowd.
limits: This is a practice-catalog step inside the pleasure-versus-improvement classification. It does not use expert authority as a judge, and it does not yet brand the pleasure-directed practice as kolakeia.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0252
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 501d-501e
source_ref:

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates establishes a yes/no answering procedure for testing whether a given practice belongs to the class of pleasure-pursuing activities: Callicles is to affirm or deny each candidate as Socrates names them."
textual_basis: At 501d-e Socrates instructs Callicles to say yes if a practice seems to be of the relevant kind and no if it does not. He then immediately applies this procedure by proposing to examine flute-playing first.
limits: This observation records the procedural framing; it does not evaluate whether the procedure is fairly applied.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0253
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 502e-503a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 502e-503a
  start_marker: 502e
  end_marker: 503a
  start_char: 112690
  end_char: 113594
  text_sha256: d314d61a4f3450cf34ee17c524f40235fbfbaf6da46966543e9d07954edbd312
greek_terms:
  - οὐχ ἁπλοῦν
  - κολακεία
  - αἰσχρὰ δημηγορία
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 502e-503a Socrates sets up a forced binary between two kinds of political speech: one aiming at the best for the citizens, the other aiming to gratify them. When Callicles replies that the question is not simple because both kinds exist, Socrates immediately reduces the twofold answer back into a binary opposition by asserting that one half is flattery and shameful demagoguery and the other is noble, namely the preparation of citizens' souls to be as good as possible.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates' question at 502e asks whether orators always speak for the best or for gratification. Callicles at 503a answers that the question is not simple because some orators care for the citizens and others are of the sort Socrates describes. Socrates then declares that if the matter is twofold, one half is flattery and shameful popular oratory and the other half is noble—the preparation of souls. This move collapses Callicles' qualified two-type answer back into a forced binary that aligns the two types with the original opposition.
limits: |
  The observation records the forced-binary structure but does not claim that Socrates' reduction is unwarranted or that the passage contains esoteric meaning. The observation stays within the textual move at 502e-503a.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0254
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 502e-503a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 502e-503a
  start_marker: 502e
  end_marker: 503a
  start_char: 112690
  end_char: 113594
  text_sha256: d314d61a4f3450cf34ee17c524f40235fbfbaf6da46966543e9d07954edbd312
greek_terms:
  - τὸ παρασκευάζειν
  - αἱ ψυχαί
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 503a Socrates uses the twofold character of political speech to define what noble rhetoric is: it is the activity of preparing the souls of the citizens so that they will be as good as possible, and of contending by speaking what is best whether it is pleasant or unpleasant to the hearers. The definition is produced by contrasting the noble half with the shameful half (flattery and demagoguery), so that the positive term is defined through opposition to the rejected term.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates at 503a describes the noble half as preparing the souls of the citizens to be best and contending by speaking what is best whether pleasant or unpleasant to the hearers. This positive formulation of noble rhetoric is produced as the opposite of the flattery half, so the definition is structured as a dichotomous contrast.
limits: |
  The observation records the dichotomous-contrast definitional form but does not claim this is the only or final definition of rhetoric in the dialogue. It stays local to the 502e-503a passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0255
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 502e-503a

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  end_char: 113594
  text_sha256: d314d61a4f3450cf34ee17c524f40235fbfbaf6da46966543e9d07954edbd312
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 502e Socrates describes orators who speak for gratification as addressing the people just like children, trying only to gratify them and not caring whether the citizens become better or worse through these things. The comparison of a democratic audience to children serves as a craft analogy that frames the gratifying orator as lacking the serious purpose of a proper educator or statesman.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates at 502e says that gratifying orators associate with the demos as with children, trying only to gratify them and not caring whether the citizens become better or worse. The comparison to children implies that the audience is treated as immature and that the orator's activity lacks the seriousness of a proper craft like education or statesmanship.
limits: |
  The observation records the child-comparison analogy at 502e but does not claim that Socrates' use of it settles the argument or that it contains esoteric meaning.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0256
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 504c-504d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  start_char: 115960
  end_char: 116864
  text_sha256: e4442e6872843e511be34bc98af9235bb4b934e87e1ba87174725344f79b0ecd
greek_terms:
  - τάξεσι
  - κοσμήσεσιν
  - νόμιμόν
  - νόμος
  - κόσμιοι
  - δικαιοσύνη
  - σωφροσύνη
english_gloss: ""
ownership: ""
observation: Socrates maps the ordering and arrangement of the soul onto the concepts of the lawful and law, producing justice and moderation as the soul's excellence, in parallel with the body's ordering producing health and bodily excellence.
textual_basis: "At 504c-504d Socrates explicitly sets up a parallel: just as bodily orderings are called 'healthy' and produce health and bodily virtue, so the orderings and arrangements of the soul are called 'lawful' and 'law,' from which people become lawful and orderly, namely justice and moderation. The craftsman-orator is to look to these as his aim."
limits: The passage establishes an analogy between body-craft products and soul-craft products but does not specify the craft that produces soul-order. The identification of the true rhetor as a craftsman is asserted, not argued for.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0257

greek_terms:
  - ἐγὼ ἐρῶ
  - φάθι
  - ἔλεγχε
  - μὴ ἐπίτρεπε
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Callicles declines to answer Socrates' question and tells Socrates to speak himself; Socrates then imposes explicit conditions on how Callicles should respond: agree if Socrates speaks well, refute if not, and do not concede."
textual_basis: "At 504c, when Socrates asks Callicles to find and state the name for what arises in the soul from order and arrangement, Callicles replies 'Why don't you speak yourself, Socrates?' (504c). Socrates then takes the turn but lays down rules: say yes, refute, and do not concede (504c)."
limits: This captures a single procedural exchange. It does not establish a pattern across the whole dialogue, though similar episodes occur elsewhere in Gorgias.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates defines the skilled and good orator by orientation toward lawful order in souls: such an orator directs speeches, actions, gifts, and removals toward producing justice and moderation in citizens."
textual_basis: At 504d-504e Socrates identifies the soul's orderings with lawfulness, justice, and moderation, then says the skilled and good orator looks to these outcomes in every rhetorical or civic action.
limits: This is Socrates' normative description of a true orator. It does not describe an existing orator or prove that such rhetoric exists in practice.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0259
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 497b-497c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 497b-497c
  start_marker: 497b
  end_marker: 497c
  start_char: 100992
  end_char: 101797
  text_sha256: b07aa30ca13a7fa5698219bfc634b5d757a788bb397fdd3909f24cb63468f3bb
greek_terms:
  - εὐδαίμων
  - μεμύησαι
  - τὰ μεγάλα
  - τὰ σμικρά
  - θεμιτὸν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates tells Callicles he is fortunate (eudaimon) because he has been initiated (memuesai) into the great mysteries before the small ones, adding that Socrates did not think this was permitted (themiton). The remark uses mystery-cult initiation vocabulary to mock Callicles' resistance to answering small-scale questions.
textual_basis: |
  At 497c, after Callicles reluctantly agrees to continue only because Gorgias approves, Socrates says: "eudaimon ei, o Kallikleis, hoti ta megala memuesai prin ta smikra; ego d' ouk oimen themiton einai." The vocabulary of initiation (memuesai) and what is ritually permissible (themiton) is drawn from mystery-cult terminology, applied ironically to Callicles' insistence on grand topics over small dialectical questions.
limits: |
  The observation identifies an irony marker in the text; it does not interpret the doctrinal content of the mysteries or claim that Socrates endorses mystery-cult practice. The remark could be read as praise rather than irony, but the context (Callicles having just dismissed Socratic questioning as "small and narrow") makes irony the most straightforward reading.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0260
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 497b-497c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 497b-497c
  start_marker: 497b
  end_marker: 497c
  start_char: 100992
  end_char: 101797
  text_sha256: b07aa30ca13a7fa5698219bfc634b5d757a788bb397fdd3909f24cb63468f3bb
greek_terms:
  - σμικρὰ
  - ὀλίγου ἄξια
  - ἀνερωτᾷ
  - ἐξελέγχει
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Callicles characterizes Socrates' questioning as always (aei) consisting of small (smikra) and worthless (oligou axia) questions that he uses to refute (exelenchei). This is an in-text meta-description of Socratic elenctic procedure delivered by a reluctant interlocutor.
textual_basis: |
  At 497b, when Gorgias urges Callicles to answer, Callicles replies: "all' aei toioutos estin Sokrates, o Gorgia; smikra kai oligou axia anerotai kai exelenchei" (Socrates is always like this, Gorgias; he asks small and worthless things and refutes). The complaint explicitly names the question-and-refutation pattern and evaluates it negatively from within the dialogue.
limits: |
  This observation records a character's description of Socratic method, not an external assessment of its validity. Callicles' description is rhetorical and hostile; it does not establish that Socratic questions are in fact trivial.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0261
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 497b-497c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 497b-497c
  start_marker: 497b
  end_marker: 497c
  start_char: 100992
  end_char: 101797
  text_sha256: b07aa30ca13a7fa5698219bfc634b5d757a788bb397fdd3909f24cb63468f3bb
greek_terms:
  - ἀποκρίνου
  - ἡμῶν ἕνεκα
  - περανθῶσιν οἱ λόγοι
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Gorgias intervenes to redirect Callicles back to answering Socrates, telling him to answer for the sake of the group so that the arguments may be completed (peranthosin hoi logoi). He further dismisses Callicles' concern about reputation, saying Callicles' honor is not at stake.
textual_basis: |
  At 497b, when Callicles says he does not know what Socrates means (ouk oida hoti legeis), Gorgias steps in: "medamos, o Kallikleis, all' apokrinou kai hemon heneka, hina peranthosin hoi logoi." He continues: "pantos ou se haute he time, o Kallikleis; all' hyposches Sokratei exelenxai hopos an bouletai." A named character who is not the primary interlocutor redirects the turn structure by urging continued response.
limits: |
  This observation records a procedural intervention. It does not establish that Gorgias endorses Socratic method, only that he wants the argument to reach a conclusion. The intervention may reflect Gorgias' own rhetorical interest in completed speeches (logoi).
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0262
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 504e-505a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 504e-505a
  start_marker: 504e
  end_marker: 505a
  start_char: 116864
  end_char: 117677
  text_sha256: fb110df0b343f75c2b77a94481547b64a2a52323ddf4c93cc90613c7dff8949e
greek_terms:
  - ψυχαῖς
  - ἰατροὶ
  - ὑγιαίνοντα
  - κάμνοντα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates draws a parallel between the medical treatment of a sick body and the condition of the soul: just as doctors do not allow a sick person to indulge appetites (eating or drinking as much as they wish), while they permit it for the healthy, so the soul requires justice and temperance to be well-ordered rather than indulging its desires (504e-505a)."
textual_basis: "At 504e Socrates secures Callicles' agreement that justice and temperance should enter souls while injustice and licentiousness depart. He then immediately (504e-505a) asks what benefit there is in giving a sick and wretchedly disposed body abundant pleasant food or drink that does not help it, and states it is not beneficial to live with a diseased body. He extends this to the doctors' practice: they allow the healthy to eat and drink as they wish, but do not allow the sick to indulge their appetites. The bodily medical regime serves as an explicit parallel for the soul's need for virtue."
limits: This observation records the structural analogy between bodily medical treatment and psychic condition. It does not claim that Socrates is making a full argument for the tripartite soul or that the analogy is original to this passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0263
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 504e-505a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 504e-505a
  start_marker: 504e
  end_marker: 505a
  start_char: 116864
  end_char: 117677
  text_sha256: fb110df0b343f75c2b77a94481547b64a2a52323ddf4c93cc90613c7dff8949e
greek_terms:
  - συγχωρῶ
  - ἔστω
  - ναί
  - ἔγωγε
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles gives a series of minimal assents as Socrates builds a stepwise argument from the soul-virtue premise through the medical analogy to the conclusion about controlling appetites (504e-505a).
textual_basis: "In rapid succession across 504e-505a, Callicles responds to short Socratic questions or assertions with four distinct one-word affirmations: he agrees that justice and temperance should enter souls (504e), concedes the point about a sick body not benefiting from indulgence (505a), affirms that living with a diseased body is not beneficial (505a), and agrees that doctors do not allow the sick to indulge their appetites (505a). Each assent enables Socrates to advance to the next step of the analogy without resistance."
limits: This observation records the assent pattern within this span. It does not analyze whether Callicles' assents are genuine or whether the argument is valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0264
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 502c-502d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 502c-502d
  start_marker: 502c
  end_marker: 502d
  start_char: 111907
  end_char: 112690
  text_sha256: dacf3ace48e09cfb9312a2ed1e814fe33e36f71a7ff5c15239a0e70f56a82820
greek_terms:
  - δημηγορία
  - ποιητική
  - ῥητορική
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates argues that if melody, rhythm, and meter are removed from all poetry, what remains is speeches (logoi), and since these speeches are addressed to a large crowd and populace, poetry is a kind of public harangue (demegoria), and therefore poetic activity is a kind of rhetoric addressed to a mixed audience.
textual_basis: At 502c Socrates proposes the thought-experiment of stripping poetry of melody, rhythm, and meter, leaving only logoi; he then secures Callicles' assent that these logoi are addressed to a large crowd and demos, and concludes that poetry is demegoria and thus a species of rhetoric (502c-502d).
limits: This observation notes Socrates' classificatory move but does not evaluate whether the reduction of poetry to its verbal component is fair or whether Callicles' rapid assent signals resistance or indifference.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0265
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 502c-502d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 502c-502d
  start_marker: 502c
  end_marker: 502d
  start_char: 111907
  end_char: 112690
  text_sha256: dacf3ace48e09cfb9312a2ed1e814fe33e36f71a7ff5c15239a0e70f56a82820
greek_terms:
  - κολακεία
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates recapitulates the earlier classification by recalling that poetry's orientation toward pleasure and gratifying spectators was agreed to be flattery (kolakeia), and after reducing poetry to rhetoric he reaffirms that this rhetoric is kolakike, i.e., of the flattering kind.
textual_basis: At 502c Socrates reminds Callicles that what aims at pleasure and gratifying spectators was just agreed to be kolakeia; after the reduction argument at 502d, Socrates concludes that the rhetoric they have discovered — aimed at a mixed demos of children, women, men, slaves, and free persons — is kolakike (502c-502d).
limits: This observation does not assess whether the reduction argument supports the flattery classification or whether the enumeration of audience components at 502d adds independent force to the claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - παίδων
  - γυναικῶν
  - ἀνδρῶν
  - δούλων
  - ἐλευθέρων
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Callicles' responses across 502c-502d form a rapid assent sequence: pany ge, ananke, phemi, phainetai, emoige, pany ge — six consecutive affirmations without resistance, culminating in the acceptance that poetry is a species of flattering rhetoric."
textual_basis: From 502c to 502d Callicles assents with pany ge (twice), ananke, phemi, phainetai, and emoige, each response ratifying a step in Socrates' reduction of poetry to demegoria and then to kolakike rhetoric.
limits: This observation records the formal assent pattern; it does not interpret whether the density signals genuine agreement, fatigue, or dramatic irony.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0267
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 504a-504b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 504a-504b
  start_marker: 504a
  end_marker: 504b
  start_char: 115265
  end_char: 115960
  text_sha256: 5710d9586d2a0bf34bbfdb46fce623eac47dcf5505178865ee006132eb293eae
greek_terms:
  - τεταγμένον
  - κεκοσμημένον
  - τάξεως
  - κόσμου
  - συντάττουσιν
  - κοσμοῦσί
  - Ὑγίειαν
english_gloss: ordered and adorned; arrangement; order; arrange; adorn; health and strength
observation: |
  At 504a–b Socrates extends a craft-based principle—that a well-ordered product is good—from houses, ships, and bodies to the soul, pressing Callicles to accept that the soul too benefits from order.
textual_basis: |
  At 504a Socrates lists craftsmen and specifically body-crafts (trainers and doctors) who order and arrange the body. He then obtains agreement that an ordered house or ship is good (504a–b). When Socrates asks whether the same applies to the soul (504b), Callicles concedes under necessity. The sequence—house, ship, body, soul—shows the craft principle being extended to a non-craft domain.
limits: |
  The passage does not assert that the soul is literally a craft product; it uses the order-goodness inference from crafted objects as an analogical premise to reach a conclusion about the soul.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0268

greek_terms:
  - Ὑγίειαν
english_gloss: health and strength
observation: |
  At 504b Socrates asks what name applies to the product that comes from order and arrangement in the body, and Callicles supplies a short catalogue: health and strength.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates poses a definiendum question at 504b: "What is the name for what comes to be in the body from order and arrangement?" Callicles answers with two items—health and strength—treating them as the characteristic outcomes of bodily order. This matches the pattern of listing defining traits of a target concept under examination.
limits: |
  The passage lists only two body-excellence terms; it does not provide a full definition of either health or strength. It is a catalogue response to a naming question, not a formal definition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0270

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 505c Callicles declares he does not understand what Socrates is saying and tells him to ask someone else, then states he does not care about anything Socrates says and that he only answered for Gorgias's sake. Socrates asks whether to break off the argument midway.
textual_basis: "Callicles shifts from assenting ('I agree' at 505b) to a complete refusal to engage: 'I don't know what you're saying, Socrates, ask someone else' and 'I don't care at all about what you say.' This is a respondent collapse: the interlocutor withdraws from the dialectical exchange rather than face further refutation."
limits: Does not establish whether Callicles genuinely does not understand or is being evasive. Does not address the philosophical content of the preceding argument about punishment and the soul.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates leads Callicles through an assent chain at 505b: a corrupt soul should be restrained from its appetites so it may become better; restraining from what it desires is punishing; therefore being punished is better for the soul than licentiousness. Callicles assents to each step (I agree; certainly; yes) and then, when the conclusion is drawn, collapses into refusal at 505c."
textual_basis: The chain runs from the analogy of body/soul treatment through restrained desires to the claim that punishment is better than licentiousness. Callicles assents at every link, then at the conclusion does not reject it on logical grounds but instead refuses to continue the conversation.
limits: Does not address whether the argument is logically valid. Does not assess whether Callicles's earlier position is truly contradicted.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0272
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 505b-505c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 505b-505c
  start_marker: 505b
  end_marker: 505c
  start_char: 117677
  end_char: 118494
  text_sha256: e0b0bdcda227c443aa7fcf74650567dbd5dc44ba116ad7bcd2fccc6bc8a68710
greek_terms:
  - μεταξὺ τὸν λόγον καταλύομεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: When Callicles refuses to continue, Socrates asks at 505c whether they will break off the argument midway, then remarks that even myths are not supposed to be broken off in the middle. The myth/proverb is invoked precisely at the point where the dialectical conversation threatens to collapse.
textual_basis: Socrates says 'Shall we break off the argument in the middle?', and when Callicles says 'You decide,' Socrates adds that even myths are not supposed to be left in the middle. The appeal to a conventional prohibition about myth-narrative is used as a dialectical pressure tactic to prevent the conversation from ending.
limits: Does not establish which specific myth or proverb is being cited. Does not assess whether the appeal to myth-norms is itself a valid argumentative move in dialectic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0273
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 506a-506b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 506a-506b
  start_marker: 506a
  end_marker: 506b
  start_char: 119232
  end_char: 120079
  text_sha256: 3c1af7bfecfaf84b526cc02cd42830a0475e3b354c8ac71364654a94abf4fc79
greek_terms:
  - ζητῶ κοινῇ μεθ’ ὑμῶν
english_gloss: I inquire in common with you
observation: |
  At 506a, Socrates characterizes his own speech as tentative and collaborative: he says he does not speak with knowledge but seeks (zeteo) in common with his listeners. He also offers that he will be the first to concede if his interlocutor appears to say something true.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates explicitly disavows knowledge at 506a and frames the discussion as common inquiry (koine zetesis). He promises to yield first (protos synchoresomai) if his opponent's point appears correct. The passage marks a shift from adversarial elenchus to a joint search model.
limits: |
  This observation notes the framing language; it does not assess whether Socrates actually conducts the inquiry jointly or whether this is a rhetorical posture. The hedge appears at 506a in a transition where Socrates addresses the audience more broadly.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0274

english_gloss: let us leave off at once and depart
observation: |
  At 506a, Socrates offers the option to abandon the discussion entirely: if the audience does not wish to complete the argument, they may let it go and depart.
textual_basis: |
  The conditional at 506a is explicit: if you do not wish it, let us leave off already and depart. This puts procedural control in the hands of the audience.
limits: |
  This records the offer to terminate. It does not interpret whether the offer is sincere, ironic, or a pressure tactic. The offer is addressed to the broader group, not only to Callicles.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: |
  At 506b Socrates explicitly names Amphion and Zethus and casts the exchange as the substitution of one speech for another. The named mythic pair supplies the paradigm for the argumentative roles in this local transition.
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles is forced by Socrates' questioning at 499b to concede that some pleasures are better and some worse, revising his earlier identification of pleasure with the good.
textual_basis: Socrates at 499b presses the consequence that if pleasant and good are the same, then the bad man would be good. At 499b-c Callicles responds by conceding that he distinguishes better pleasures from worse pleasures.
limits: This observation records the concession that revises the definition. It does not assess whether Callicles is sincere or whether the revision ultimately survives further elenctic testing.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0277

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates at 499c accuses Callicles of treating him like a child, playing and deceiving him, and laments that he expected not to be deceived by a friend.
textual_basis: At 499b-c Socrates exclaims that Callicles is a rogue who treats him like a child, now saying one thing, now another, and deceiving him. Socrates then invokes the old saying to make the best of the present and accept what is given by Callicles.
limits: This observation marks the ironic register of Socrates' complaint. It does not determine whether Callicles actually shifts position or whether Socrates genuinely believes he has been deceived.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles at 499b characterizes his ongoing assent to Socrates' questions as a playful concession that Socrates seizes on like a child, indicating that his answers do not reflect his considered position.
textual_basis: At 499b Callicles says he has been going along with Socrates' questioning for some time, noting that if someone playfully concedes anything to Socrates, Socrates grabs hold of it gleefully like children do.
limits: This observation notes the procedural frame Callicles invokes to distance himself from his own concessions. It does not evaluate the validity of his claim that the assents were merely playful.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0279
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 505d-505e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 505d-505e
  start_marker: 505d
  end_marker: 505e
  start_char: 118494
  end_char: 119232
  text_sha256: cb467e423be114af09ee14e1a7ad60a95ac6573d1e2762dc12e6a00bb0019d8b
greek_terms:
  - κεφαλήν
  - κεφαλῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates demands that Callicles continue the argument so that the logos may acquire a 'head' (505d), using the image of a headless body to insist on completion. Callicles resists and proposes alternatives: Socrates should either let the argument go or talk with someone else. When Socrates refuses to leave the logos incomplete, Callicles asks whether Socrates cannot go through the argument by himself (505d). Socrates then accedes to soliloquy, quoting Epicharmus: what two men were saying before, he will now be sufficient as one (505e)."
textual_basis: "At 505d Callicles tells Socrates to let this argument go or converse with someone else. At 505e Socrates quotes Epicharmus and resumes alone. The function is turn_geometry because the passage negotiates who will continue the question-and-answer exchange, and it matches argument_heir_designation: the respondent is explicitly refusing to continue, the questioner declines to find a replacement, and the questioner designates himself as the next interlocutor."
limits: This observation records the procedural handoff of the respondent role to Socrates himself, not any claim about why Callicles withdraws. It does not assess whether Socrates' soliloquy is effective or ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0280

greek_terms:
  - βίαιος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles addresses Socrates by characterizing him as violent at 505d, directly describing Socrates' insistence on continuing the argument as forceful. This occurs when Socrates demands that the logos be completed rather than abandoned headless.
textual_basis: At 505d Callicles exclaims 'How violent you are, Socrates!' — the epithet is a direct characterization of Socrates by an interlocutor and expresses hostility to the procedural demand.
limits: This observation records a single hostile characterization by Callicles. It does not assess whether the characterization is fair or whether Socrates is in fact violent.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0281
source_work: Gorgias

greek_terms:
  - φιλονίκως
  - κοινὸν γὰρ ἀγαθὸν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "After agreeing to soliloquy, Socrates announces a procedural norm for the coming argument at 505e: everyone should strive with love of victory toward knowing what is true and what is false about the subject, because it is a common good for it to become manifest to all."
textual_basis: At 505e Socrates says that if they do this, everyone ought to strive with love of victory toward knowing what is true about what they are saying and what is false; for it is a common good for all that it become manifest. This is a procedural framing that explicitly states the norms for the argumentative exchange that follows.
limits: This observation records the procedural norm stated before Socrates begins his solo argument. It does not assess whether the norm is actually followed in the subsequent speech.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0285
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 507d-507e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 507d-507e
  start_marker: 507d
  end_marker: 507e
  start_char: 122575
  end_char: 123355
  text_sha256: fd95ab02e0b86cdc8e540090f74708c9cf2a84fd9e3c62bf39d876d32182a406
greek_terms:
  - δίκην
  - εὐδαίμων
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates claims that one must actively pursue punishment for oneself, one's relatives, or one's city as a condition for happiness at 507d-e.
textual_basis: At 507d Socrates states that one should prepare so as not to need punishment, but if one does need it — whether oneself, a relative, or a city — one must impose justice and be punished "if one is to be happy." This inverts the ordinary valuation of punishment as an evil to be avoided, presenting it instead as a necessary means to happiness.
limits: This observation records the inversion claim; it does not assess whether Socrates' argument for this position succeeds or whether it is consistent with claims elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0286
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 507d-507e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 507d-507e
  start_marker: 507d
  end_marker: 507e
  start_char: 122575
  end_char: 123355
  text_sha256: fd95ab02e0b86cdc8e540090f74708c9cf2a84fd9e3c62bf39d876d32182a406
greek_terms:
  - κοινωνία
  - φιλία
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates argues at 507e that the licentious person cannot be dear to either human or god because community (koinonia) is a necessary condition for friendship (philia).
textual_basis: At 507e Socrates states that such a person is incapable of sharing, and where there is no community, there can be no friendship. From this conditional premise he concludes that the licentious person is dear to neither god nor human, making the argument's force depend on the claim that friendship requires community.
limits: This observation identifies the logical structure of the argument; it does not evaluate whether the premise that friendship requires community is established or defended elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0287

greek_terms:
  - λῃστοῦ βίον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates characterizes the life devoted to filling licentious desires as a "robber's life" at 507e.
textual_basis: At 507e Socrates calls the endless attempt to satisfy licentious desires an endless evil and describes it as "living a robber's life." This deploys a negative criminal social type to characterize a specific mode of life as predatory and antisocial.
limits: This observation notes the use of a criminal type as a characterization device; it does not claim that Socrates is making a legal argument about actual robbery or that this characterization is unique to the Gorgias.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0288
draft: true
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 507b-507c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 507b-507c
  start_marker: 507b
  end_marker: 507c
  start_char: 121680
  end_char: 122575
  text_sha256: 4dc4578d377664a68bcce1a3e5d1c4787295e30fcc5cdb0eb0a7751f7e02fbec
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 507b-c Socrates consolidates a chain in which acting fittingly toward humans is just action, acting fittingly toward gods is pious action, and the one who acts justly and piously must be just and pious. From this he derives that the temperate man must also be courageous, and that the temperate, just, courageous, and pious man is completely good, acts well and nobly, and is blessed and happy.
textual_basis: The sequence of necessity claims (ananke...ananke) and the connecting particles (kai men...kai men de kai...hoste polle ananke) at 507b-c show Socrates assembling prior agreed points about fitting action into a culminating definitional chain that ties temperance to justice, piety, courage, goodness, and happiness.
limits: This observation records the consolidation of the chain but does not assess whether each link in the chain was adequately established earlier in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0289
draft: true
source_work: Gorgias

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  text_sha256: 4dc4578d377664a68bcce1a3e5d1c4787295e30fcc5cdb0eb0a7751f7e02fbec
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 507b-c Socrates delivers a dense sequence of necessity statements leading to a climactic conclusion, naming his interlocutor Callicles by name. The passage functions as a long speaker-driven assent chain in which the respondent's intervening assents are already presupposed or elicited earlier and the speaker now draws the final inferences.
textual_basis: The repeated ananke formulations (507b-c), the vocative address to Kallikles, and the explicit reference back to what was gone through earlier (hosper dielthomen, 507c) mark this as the culminating stage of an assent chain built up over the preceding argument.
limits: The record does not verify that Callicles actually assented to every prior premise; it only notes the structural function of this passage as the terminal link in an assent chain.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0290
draft: true
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 507b-507c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 507b-507c
  start_marker: 507b
  end_marker: 507c
  start_char: 121680
  end_char: 122575
  text_sha256: 4dc4578d377664a68bcce1a3e5d1c4787295e30fcc5cdb0eb0a7751f7e02fbec
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 507b-c Socrates constructs a complete ethical profile of the temperate man (sophron) by deriving justice, piety, courage, complete goodness, noble action, and blessed happiness from temperance alone. He then names the opposite type, the akolastos (intemperate man), and explicitly notes that Callicles praised this opposite type.
textual_basis: The passage defines the sophron positively through multiple derived attributes (507b-c) and then defines the akolastos as the opposite (ho enantiōs echōn tō sōphroni, 507c), adding that Callicles praised him (hon su epēneis, 507c).
limits: This record identifies the construction of opposed ethical type profiles but does not assess whether the derivation from sophrosyne to the other virtues is philosophically sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0291
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 498c-498d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 498c-498d
  start_marker: 498c
  end_marker: 498d
  start_char: 103505
  end_char: 104309
  text_sha256: a7ddaffacc5ad2bcfc75301f61670888d838e67f016333cb27a5cab505ffe2b5
greek_terms:
  - παραπλησίως
  - ἀγαθοὶ
  - κακοί
english_gloss: similarly; good (men); bad (men)
observation: Socrates secures a rapid series of assents from Callicles at 498c (cowards feel pleasure and pain similarly to the brave; the wise and brave are good, the cowardly and foolish are bad; therefore the good and bad feel pleasure and pain similarly) and then asks whether the good and bad are therefore similarly good and bad, driving the premises toward a contradiction.
textual_basis: The passage at 498c moves through four consecutive assents before Socrates poses the destabilizing question about whether the good and bad are similarly good and bad, to which Callicles at 498d responds with confusion rather than assent.
limits: This observation records the assent-chain structure; it does not evaluate whether the contradiction is valid or whether Callicles' prior commitments are correctly represented.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0292

observation: Callicles at 498d declares explicitly that he does not understand what Socrates is saying, marking a breakdown in comprehension produced by the elenctic chain of questions at 498c.
textual_basis: After Socrates' rapid series of assents and the destabilizing question about whether good and bad are similarly good and bad at 498c, Callicles responds at 498d with an oath-strengthened confession of incomprehension.
limits: This observation records the reported effect of the elenchus on the respondent; it does not assess whether Callicles' confusion is genuine or feigned.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0293
source_work: Gorgias

greek_terms:
  - παρουσίᾳ
english_gloss: by presence
observation: "At 498d, after Callicles declares his confusion, Socrates explicitly recalls Callicles' earlier framework: the good are good by the presence of good things, and good things are pleasures while bad things are pains. Socrates then uses this recalled concession to construct a refutation by showing that both cowards and the brave would be good whenever they feel pleasure."
textual_basis: Socrates at 498d explicitly recalls the parousia doctrine and the identification of good with pleasure that Callicles had earlier conceded, then proceeds to draw the refutative consequence.
limits: This observation records the structural move of recalling an earlier concession for refutative purposes; it does not assess whether Socrates' recall is faithful to Callicles' original formulations.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0294
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 506c-506d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 506c-506d
  start_marker: 506c
  end_marker: 506d
  start_char: 120079
  end_char: 120934
  text_sha256: 71ae40f3d0d4de6f5a9ea252a139d6dbf4189914729fb14be07d4baa20e1445d
greek_terms:
  - τάξει
  - ὀρθότητι
  - τέχνῃ
  - ἀρετὴ
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates claims that the excellence (arete) of every thing — including equipment, body, soul, and every living creature — comes to be present not by chance but through order, correctness, and craft.
textual_basis: |
  At 506d, Socrates asserts that the excellence of each thing (skeuos, soma, psyche, zoon) arises not by chance (toi eike) but by taxis, orthotes, and techne that is assigned to each. This extends the craft-order principle explicitly to the soul and every living being.
limits: |
  The claim is an assertion made within an assent chain; it does not constitute a demonstration that soul-excellence is produced by craft. The observation records the extension of the craft-order principle to soul, not a proof.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0295
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 506c-506d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  start_marker: 506c
  end_marker: 506d
  start_char: 120079
  end_char: 120934
  text_sha256: 71ae40f3d0d4de6f5a9ea252a139d6dbf4189914729fb14be07d4baa20e1445d
greek_terms:
  - τὸ ἡδὺ
  - τὸ ἀγαθὸν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates explicitly restarts the argument from the beginning (ex arches), recapitulating prior agreements with Callicles that the pleasant and the good are not the same, and that the pleasant is to be done for the sake of the good.
textual_basis: |
  At 506c, Socrates announces he will take up the argument from the beginning (ex arches emou analambontos ton logon), then recapitulates two agreed points: the pleasant and good are not the same, and the pleasant is done for the sake of the good. This formal restart reestablishes the foundation for the subsequent argument.
limits: |
  The recapitulation restates agreements that were reached earlier in the dialogue. It does not verify that those agreements remain Callicles' view at this point; Callicles' minimal responses (lege, akoue de, pany ge) mark formal assent rather than renewed commitment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0298
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 508a-508b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 508a-508b
  start_marker: 508a
  end_marker: 508b
  start_char: 123355
  end_char: 124256
  text_sha256: 1f82388e4ff0d59478797cf8c1c3af6ec69b54dd7312438c76ba222fef3a96fb
greek_terms:
  - ἰσότης
  - γεωμετρικὴ
  - πλεονεξίαν
  - κόσμον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates invokes geometric equality as a cosmic ordering principle that binds earth, gods, and humans, and notes that the whole is called kosmos (order) because of these ordering virtues, not disorder or license.
textual_basis: At 508a Socrates enumerates community, friendship, orderliness, moderation, and justice as what holds the cosmos together, and asserts that geometric equality has great power among gods and humans. He then reproaches Callicles for pursuing overreaching and neglecting geometry.
limits: This records a mathematical-cosmic analogy used to characterize ethical order. It does not assess whether geometric equality is the same concept as geometric proportion discussed elsewhere in Plato, nor does it evaluate the validity of the analogy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0299
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 508a-508b

greek_terms:
  - εἶεν
  - ἐξελεγκτέος
  - σκεπτέον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates marks a transition and frames the next phase of inquiry as a disjunctive choice: either refute this argument (logos) or, if it is true, examine what follows from it."
textual_basis: "At 508a-b Socrates states with two verbal adjectives: either this argument must be refuted ('exelenkteos'), or if it is true, the consequences must be examined ('skepteon'). This frames the procedural alternatives explicitly before proceeding."
limits: This records the procedural framing device. It does not evaluate which horn Socrates pursues or whether the framing is argumentatively fair.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - τὰ πρόσθεν ἐκεῖνα
  - συμβαίνει πάντα
  - κατηγορητέον
  - ῥητορικῇ
  - ἀληθῆ ἄρα ἦν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates recapitulates earlier consequences: self-indictment for injustice, rhetoric's use toward that end, the truth of the Polus concession about doing injustice, and the implication of Gorgias' justice concession."
textual_basis: At 508b-508c Socrates marks that the earlier points all follow, recalls Callicles' question about whether he was serious, and restates the conclusions about accusation, rhetoric, Polus' shame-concession, and Gorgias' admission about teaching justice.
limits: This records the local recapitulation function. It does not assess whether the recalled conclusions follow validly or how Callicles should respond.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0301
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 503d-503e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 503d-503e
  start_marker: 503d
  end_marker: 503e
  start_char: 114433
  end_char: 115265
  text_sha256: fb4738f1370ebbbfeacf848210b628c38815b3d8d48d27a4375c67adacbfe045
greek_terms:
  - τέχνη
  - τάξιν
  - πρέπον
  - ἁρμόττειν
english_gloss: The passage introduces techne (craft) as the principle governing good speech
observation: Socrates proposes that the good man who speaks for the best (epi to beltiston) does not speak at random (eikei) but looks toward something (apoblepon pros ti), and then immediately equates this to how every craftsman (demiourgoi) looks toward his work and imposes order (taxis) and fit (prepon, harmottein) on it.
textual_basis: "At 503d Socrates asks whether the good man who speaks for the best speaks at random or aims at something (503e: apoblepon pros ti). He then answers by analogy: just as all craftsmen look to their own work, not choosing randomly, but so that what they produce has a certain form (eidos), placing each thing into an arrangement (taxis) and compelling each part to fit and harmonize with the others (503e)."
limits: This observation establishes the craft-analogy framework applied to speech but does not adjudicate whether the analogy holds for rhetoric in particular, which is the contested point in the wider dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0303
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 510a-510b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 510a-510b
  start_marker: 510a
  end_marker: 510b
  start_char: 127502
  end_char: 128379
  text_sha256: 4d2585acd99185a0343383e208fa3b4d738303b73909f47d3afd231ee5a8e38f
greek_terms:
  - παρασκευαστέον
  - δύναμίν
  - τέχνην
english_gloss: So we must prepare a power and a craft to prevent being wronged.
observation: "Socrates applies the craft analogy to the problem of self-protection from injustice: one must prepare a power and a craft to avoid being wronged, and then proposes three specific political means—ruling, tyranny, or being a companion of the existing constitution—to achieve this end."
textual_basis: "At 510a Socrates states that we must prepare some power and craft for this, so that we do not commit injustice. He then enumerates three concrete political instruments: ruling in the city, exercising tyranny, or being a companion of the existing constitution. This follows the pattern of applying a craft to a specific harm-prevention goal—a 'what-to-whom' sequence already seen elsewhere."
limits: The observation notes only that Socrates frames self-protection as a craft problem; it does not claim that the three options are exhaustive or that the craft is actually teachable. The passage does not develop the analogy further within this segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0304
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 510a-510b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 510a-510b
  start_marker: 510a
  end_marker: 510b
  start_char: 127502
  end_char: 128379
  text_sha256: 4d2585acd99185a0343383e208fa3b4d738303b73909f47d3afd231ee5a8e38f
greek_terms:
  - παλαιοί
  - ὁ ὅμοιος τῷ ὁμοίῳ
english_gloss: "The ancient and wise say: like to like."
observation: Socrates invokes a traditional maxim ('like to like') as a shared premise to advance the argument about political friendship and self-protection, after proposing three concrete political options. This recapitulates the direction of inquiry from a more general principle.
textual_basis: At 510b Socrates appeals to the saying of the ancient and wise that like is friend to like. This maxim is introduced as a general principle under which the specific political options (ruling, tyranny, companion of the constitution) can be subsumed, effectively recasting the problem of self-protection in terms of likeness and friendship rather than institutional power.
limits: The observation identifies the citation of a traditional maxim as a recapitulation device; it does not assess whether the maxim is true or whether Socrates genuinely endorses it. The maxim is only introduced, not developed, within the segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: Socrates explicitly marks that the conclusions about injustice are being recalled from earlier in the dialogue.
textual_basis: At 508e, Socrates uses a phrase meaning 'these things that appeared to us above there in the previous arguments' (tauta hemin ano ekei en tois prosthen logois houto phanenta), marking an explicit recapitulation of prior argumentative conclusions.
limits: This is a recapitulation, not a new argument; Socrates is summarizing, not introducing fresh premises.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 508e-509a Socrates says the previous conclusions are held and bound by iron and adamantine arguments unless Callicles or someone younger loosens them.
limits: This records the binding metaphor, not a formal entailment claim.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0307
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 508e-509a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - οὐκ οἶδα ὅπως ἔχει
  - καταγέλαστος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates disavows knowledge of the matter while simultaneously claiming that no interlocutor has ever been able to refute his position without becoming ridiculous.
textual_basis: At 509a, Socrates says 'I do not know how these things are' (ouk oida hopos echei), immediately followed by the claim that no one he has encountered has been able to speak otherwise without being ridiculous (katagelastos).
limits: The observation does not interpret whether the knowledge disavowal is sincere or ironic; it only records the textual co-occurrence of disavowal with the empirical claim about past interlocutors.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0308
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 509d-509e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 509d-509e
  start_marker: 509d
  end_marker: 509e
  start_char: 126757
  end_char: 127502
  text_sha256: 13aa6d1c060c44fa7d84b4ec682792433ee945568a3fcce28ebb0851847db0f4
greek_terms:
  - πότερον
  - δύναμιν
  - βούλησιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates structures his questioning at 509d-509e as a series of disjunctive alternatives using the 'whether...or...' construction, forcing Callicles to choose between two mutually exclusive options.
textual_basis: "At 509d Socrates asks whether the dual benefit requires power or will, then refines with a disjunction about suffering injustice: whether it is sufficient not to wish to suffer injustice or one must prepare power. At 509e he extends the same whether-or pattern to committing injustice."
limits: This observation notes the formal questioning structure; it does not evaluate whether the disjunction is exhaustive or whether Callicles' assent is forced.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0309
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 509d-509e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 509d-509e
  start_marker: 509d
  end_marker: 509e

  end_char: 127502
  text_sha256: 13aa6d1c060c44fa7d84b4ec682792433ee945568a3fcce28ebb0851847db0f4
greek_terms:
  - ἐν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν λόγοις
  - μηδένα βουλόμενον ἀδικεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 509e Socrates invokes a prior agreement from the earlier discussion with Polus as a premise in his current questioning of Callicles, asking whether that earlier agreement was correctly reached under compulsion.
textual_basis: Socrates asks Callicles at 509e whether they were correctly compelled to agree in the earlier arguments with Polus when they agreed that no one willingly commits injustice but all who commit injustice do so unwillingly.
limits: This observation records that Socrates invokes a prior agreement as a dialectical move. It does not assess whether the prior agreement was correctly reached or whether Callicles is bound by it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0310
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 509d-509e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 509d-509e
  start_marker: 509d
  end_marker: 509e
  start_char: 126757
  end_char: 127502
  text_sha256: 13aa6d1c060c44fa7d84b4ec682792433ee945568a3fcce28ebb0851847db0f4
greek_terms:
  - ἀδικεῖσθαι
  - ἀδικεῖν
  - δύναμιν
  - βούλησιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates first applies the power/will distinction to the case of suffering injustice at 509d, obtains Callicles' assent that power is required, then extends the same distinction to the case of committing injustice at 509e.
textual_basis: At 509d Socrates asks about suffering injustice whether refraining from wishing not to suffer it suffices or whether one must prepare power. Callicles answers that power is needed. Socrates then immediately extends the parallel question to the domain of committing injustice at 509e, asking whether not wishing to commit injustice is sufficient or whether power and craft must also be prepared for that.
limits: This observation records the parallel structural extension of a question framework across two domains. It does not claim that the parallel is logically valid or that the two cases are symmetrical.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0311
source_work: Gorgias

  - ἐπιστήμονα τῶν δικαίων
english_gloss: knowledgeable about just things
observation: At 508c Socrates recalls that Polus said Gorgias conceded — from shame — that the would-be rhetor must be just and knowledgeable about just things.
textual_basis: "The passage contains a reference back to a prior concession in the dialogue: Socrates states that Polus claimed Gorgias agreed (homologesai) through shame (dia aischunen). This exemplifies the recurrent pattern of recalling a previous interlocutor concession during an elenctic refutation."
limits: This observation only notes the explicit reference to a prior concession. It does not assess whether the concession was coerced or evaluate the fairness of Socrates's use of it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0312
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 508c-508d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 508c-508d
  start_marker: 508c
  end_marker: 508d
  start_char: 124256
  end_char: 125113
  text_sha256: 94ad1427eeeca1bdb60b81dface76d6f7e858b491b3c89a51446beac6939c008
greek_terms:
  - ἄτιμοι
  - τοῦ ἐθέλοντος
english_gloss: disenfranchised, at the mercy of whoever wills
observation: "Socrates at 508c-d reformulates Callicles's reproach against him in a vivid catalog: that Socrates is unable to defend himself or friends and is at the mercy of anyone who wants to strike him on the cheek, take his property, expel him from the city, or kill him."
textual_basis: Socrates explicitly frames what 'you reproach me with' (ha su emoi oneidizeis) and then recasts Callicles's earlier accusations as a set of escalating scenarios, culminating in being killed. This is the opponent's position restated in the speaker's own terms before a counter-argument.
limits: This observation records the reformulation pattern. It does not evaluate the fairness of the paraphrase or whether Callicles would endorse this formulation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0313
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 508c-508d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 508c-508d
  start_marker: 508c
  end_marker: 508d

  end_char: 125113
  text_sha256: 94ad1427eeeca1bdb60b81dface76d6f7e858b491b3c89a51446beac6939c008
greek_terms:
  - σκεψώμεθα τί ποτ’ ἐστὶν
english_gloss: let us examine what exactly it is
observation: At 508c Socrates marks a transition with the phrase 'let us examine what exactly it is' (skepsometha ti pot' estin), introducing the examination of Callicles's reproach.
textual_basis: The formula skepsometha ti pot' estin explicitly signals a procedural turn from recalling prior concessions to examining a new target. The adverb pote (exactly) reinforces that the inquiry will scrutinize the content of the reproach.
limits: This observation only notes the transition marker. It does not assess whether the examination that follows fulfills the promise of the transition formula.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0314
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 511b-511c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 511b-511c
  start_marker: 511b
  end_marker: 511c
  start_char: 129866
  end_char: 130717
  text_sha256: d69bd8b4ee6ee0fc47d9249ba7d6b2f2e89758cb6ba757509b202832081c7cbf
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates introduces the craft of swimming (he tou nein episteme) as a deliberately humble counterexample to rhetoric: both arts save from death, yet Callicles regards swimming as trivial while he exhorts Socrates to practice courtroom rhetoric."
textual_basis: At 511c Socrates asks whether the knowledge of swimming seems a grand thing (semne) to Callicles; Callicles denies it. Socrates then notes that swimming too saves people from death, paralleling the life-saving claim Callicles makes for rhetoric at 511b-c. The juxtaposition exposes an inconsistency in Callicles' evaluation of life-saving crafts.
limits: Socrates does not here develop a full craft taxonomy or define what makes a craft grand versus trivial; the analogy is presented as a question rather than a conclusion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0315
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 511b-511c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 511b-511c
  start_marker: 511b
  end_marker: 511c
  start_char: 129866
  end_char: 130717
  text_sha256: d69bd8b4ee6ee0fc47d9249ba7d6b2f2e89758cb6ba757509b202832081c7cbf
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates refutes the value Callicles places on courtroom rhetoric by introducing swimming as a counterexample: swimming saves from death just as rhetoric is said to do, yet Callicles considers swimming a small thing."
textual_basis: At 511b-c Callicles advises Socrates to practice rhetoric because it saves one from dangers in the lawcourts. Socrates then asks at 511c whether the science of swimming also seems grand, since it too saves from death. Callicles denies swimming is grand, creating a contradiction between his high valuation of rhetoric's life-saving function and his dismissal of another life-saving craft.
limits: This observation records the counterexample structure of the argument. It does not assess whether the analogy between swimming and rhetoric is valid or whether Callicles would accept the implicit conclusion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0316
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 511b-511c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 511b-511c
  start_marker: 511b
  end_marker: 511c
  start_char: 129866
  end_char: 130717
  text_sha256: d69bd8b4ee6ee0fc47d9249ba7d6b2f2e89758cb6ba757509b202832081c7cbf
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates challenges Callicles' view that a man should prepare to live as long as possible by cultivating arts that deliver from danger, asking whether even the lowly art of swimming deserves esteem on the same grounds as courtroom rhetoric.
textual_basis: At 511b Socrates asks whether one ought to prepare oneself to live the longest possible time and practice those arts that always save us from dangers, including the courtroom rhetoric Callicles urges. At 511c he pivots to swimming, which also saves from death when one falls into water, and asks Callicles whether it seems a grand thing. Callicles denies it.
limits: The observation records the structural use of a humble craft as a test case for a claim about a valued craft, without evaluating whether the argument defeats Callicles' position.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0317
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 512a-512b

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
```
