```yaml
  start_char: 131541
  end_char: 132423
  text_sha256: ac2ab4c64a66fb98df0cfe90c88c5d54b4be57a59dbec08e5de830a9784a356b
greek_terms:
  - ψυχῇ
  - νοσήματα
  - ἀνίατα
  - σώσῃ
  - μοχθηρῷ
  - κακῶς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates limits the value of preservation crafts by asking whether saving a badly diseased soul benefits the person saved.
textual_basis: At 512a-512b the argument shifts from bodily disease to incurable soul disease and the bad person's necessity of living badly.
limits: This changes the defective label to the local preservation-craft argument; it adds no broad doctrine about craft advantage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0318
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 512c-512d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 512c-512d
  start_marker: 512c
  end_marker: 512d
  start_char: 132423
  end_char: 133299
  text_sha256: 3bd3dbba7c58d7d9f3aeba0df2d1916086a90000b7937d6b500b9a8895f3b54d
greek_terms:
  - σῴζειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates groups the mechanic and the doctor as examples of crafts made for the sake of preservation, using the mechanic's social stigma to test whether Callicles can consistently both despise the mechanic and equate the good with self-preservation.
textual_basis: At 512c, Socrates argues that one would be ashamed to be called a mechanic and would refuse marriage alliance with a mechanic's family. At 512d, he states that if the good is merely preserving oneself and one's property (to sozein hauton kai ta heautou onta), then criticism of the mechanic, the doctor, and the other crafts becomes laughable, because those crafts have been made for the sake of preserving (tou sozein heneka pepoientai).
limits: This observation identifies the grouping of crafts under a single function. It does not assess whether these crafts are reducible to preservation or whether Socrates endorses this grouping.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0319
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 512c-512d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 512c-512d
  start_marker: 512c
  end_marker: 512d
  start_char: 132423
  end_char: 133299
  text_sha256: 3bd3dbba7c58d7d9f3aeba0df2d1916086a90000b7937d6b500b9a8895f3b54d
greek_terms:
  - γενναῖον
  - ἀγαθὸν
  - σῴζειν
  - σῴζεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates exposes Callicles' implicit definition that the noble and the good equal self-preservation, then challenges it by pointing to the unacceptable consequence that this would remove any basis for despising the mechanic.
textual_basis: "At 512d, Socrates asks: \"Look, lest the noble and the good be something other than preserving and being preserved\" (hora me allo ti to gennaion kai to agathon e i to sozein te kai sozesthai). This follows the argument at 512c-d that if the good is preservation, then the criticism of the mechanic becomes laughable (katagelastos), since the mechanic's craft also aims at preservation."
limits: This observation notes the exposure of an implicit definiendum through its consequences. It does not evaluate whether Callicles actually holds this definition or whether the exposure is valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0320
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 512c-512d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 512c-512d
  start_marker: 512c
  end_marker: 512d
  start_char: 132423
  end_char: 133299
  text_sha256: 3bd3dbba7c58d7d9f3aeba0df2d1916086a90000b7937d6b500b9a8895f3b54d
greek_terms:
  - μακάριε
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates addresses Callicles as "blessed one" immediately before challenging his view about the nature of the good.
textual_basis: At 512d, Socrates says "all', o makarie, hora me..." (but, blessed one, look lest...), using the vocative address o makarie just before questioning whether the noble and the good is reducible to self-preservation.
limits: This observation notes one instance of the address. It does not claim that every use of makarie in Plato is ironic or that the irony is directed at Callicles personally rather than at the position.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0321
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 512e-513a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 512e-513a
  start_marker: 512e
  end_marker: 513a
  start_char: 133299
  end_char: 134026
  text_sha256: ab8cb6cab02b0e97502c049cc9cb9b42da7df2e7fc642656d4656cc514343ea4
greek_terms:
  - Θετταλίδας
  - σελήνην
  - καθαιρούσας
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates invokes the folk belief about Thessalian women who pull down the moon as a negative analogy for the consequences of pursuing political power through self-assimilation to the Athenian people.
textual_basis: At 513a, Socrates says the equivalent of 'as they say the Thessalian women do who pull down the moon' — he explicitly marks the belief as reported speech, introduces a bounded folkloric reference, and uses it to warn Callicles that the pursuit of political power will bring harm to oneself and those dearest. The mythic/folk reference is demarcated from rational argument by its reported-speech framing and serves as an analogy for the dialogue's practical stakes.
limits: This observation does not claim the reference is a full myth narrative or that Socrates endorses the folk belief; it only notes the demarcated folkloric reference used as an argumentative analogy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0322
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 512e-513a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 512e-513a
  start_marker: 512e
  end_marker: 513a
  start_char: 133299
  end_char: 134026
  text_sha256: ab8cb6cab02b0e97502c049cc9cb9b42da7df2e7fc642656d4656cc514343ea4
greek_terms:
  - λυσιτελεῖ
  - φιλτάτοις
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates frames the choice about political assimilation as one that concerns both himself and Callicles jointly, stating that the consequences will involve their dearest ones.
textual_basis: At 513a, Socrates says 'consider whether this benefits you and me' and 'with those dearest to us will be our choice' — he explicitly includes himself in the stakes and extends the risk to those closest to both of them.
limits: This observation does not claim Socrates genuinely fears for himself; it only notes the textual framing of shared risk.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0323
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 513a-513b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 513a-513b
  start_marker: 513a
  end_marker: 513b
  start_char: 133579
  end_char: 134477
  text_sha256: 887e4fc538afb2a09ab35319e9ccdf6039c870b1641f29c4e4c740901d260f10
greek_terms:
  - παραδώσειν
  - τέχνην τινὰ τοιαύτην
  - μέγα δύνασθαι
  - ἀνόμοιον
  - ὁμοιότατον
  - πολιτικὸν καὶ ῥητορικόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates doubts that anyone can hand over a craft that would make Callicles powerful in Athens while unlike the Athenian constitution; political and rhetorical success is said to require becoming naturally similar to the people addressed.
textual_basis: At 513a-513b Socrates introduces the supposed transmissible craft, states that civic power would require becoming like the regime and the Athenian people, and rejects the prospect that Callicles could gain power while remaining unlike them.
limits: This records the craft-transmission test inside Socrates' political warning. It does not settle whether political education is impossible or whether assimilation is Socrates' final account of civic power.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0324
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 510c-510d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 510c-510d
  start_marker: 510c
  end_marker: 510d
  start_char: 128379
  end_char: 129200
  text_sha256: 4026eed0236f56403574dd32277966ffe6239708b957d3991001532a63ed5a84
greek_terms:
  - ἔστι ταῦτα
  - ἀληθῆ
  - ναί
english_gloss: ""
observation: Throughout 510c-510d, Callicles responds to Socrates' questions with minimal one- or two-word assents, conceding each step of the argument without elaboration.
textual_basis: Callicles' four responses in this span are a bare affirmation at 510c, a two-word assent at 510c, and two single-word assents at 510d. Each is an unqualified concession with no resistance or expansion.
limits: This observation does not claim that Callicles fully understands or endorses the implications of what he assents to; minimal assent may reflect compliance rather than conviction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0325
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 510c-510d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 510c-510d
  start_marker: 510c
  end_marker: 510d
  start_char: 128379
  end_char: 129200
  text_sha256: 4026eed0236f56403574dd32277966ffe6239708b957d3991001532a63ed5a84
greek_terms:
  - ὁμοήθης
  - ὁδός
  - ὅμοιος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates argues at 510c-510d that the only path for a young man to gain great power and avoid being wronged in a city ruled by a tyrant is to make himself as similar as possible to the tyrant from youth — sharing his pleasures and pains and praising and blaming the same things.
textual_basis: "At 510c Socrates eliminates the inferior person as a possible friend of the tyrant, leaving only the like-charactered person who shares the ruler's praises and blame and submits to his rule. At 510d he states this is the path for a young man: to accustom himself from youth to share the master's pleasures and pains and become as similar as possible to him. Avoiding injustice is achieved not by resisting the unjust ruler but by assimilating to him."
limits: Socrates presents this as the logical consequence of premises Callicles has accepted, not as his own endorsement. The observation records the argument structure, not a claim about Socratic recommendation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0326
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 514a-514b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 514a-514b
  start_marker: 514a
  end_marker: 514b
  start_char: 135622
  end_char: 136368
  text_sha256: a4151cb5d5ea6f4438baf0897fa44a07c129df1fc1c3960dcd824cf1fef5f6df
greek_terms:
  - οἰκοδομική
  - καλὸν
  - αἰσχρόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates proposes that before undertaking public political work, one should apply the same test one would apply to the building craft: (1) whether one knows the craft and from whom one learned it, and (2) whether one has a private track record of completed products that can be assessed as beautiful or shameful."
textual_basis: At 514a-514b, Socrates uses the building craft as an explicit analogy for political expertise. He asks whether, if they were called to public building projects (walls, shipyards, temples), they should first examine whether they know the craft and from whom they learned, and second whether they have ever built anything privately that is beautiful or shameful. The two criteria are presented as a hypothetical test translatable to political expertise.
limits: The observation records the two-part test structure within a craft analogy but does not claim this is Socrates' definitive account of craft knowledge; the criteria are introduced hypothetically as a condition for entering public service.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0327
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 514a-514b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 514a-514b
  start_marker: 514a
  end_marker: 514b
  start_char: 135622
  end_char: 136368
  text_sha256: a4151cb5d5ea6f4438baf0897fa44a07c129df1fc1c3960dcd824cf1fef5f6df
greek_terms:
  - πάνυ γε, εἴ σοι ἥδιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles agrees with Socrates' normative premise using a formula that signals compliance for the sake of the conversation rather than genuine agreement.
textual_basis: At 514a, after Socrates states the premise about not bringing forward anyone whose mind is not beautiful and good if they are to receive money, rule, or power, Callicles responds with a phrase meaning "certainly, if it pleases you." This formula grants the premise while marking the respondent's distance from it.
limits: This records a conversational move where the respondent formally agrees while signaling disengagement. It does not interpret Callicles' psychological motive or claim that his later arguments are insincere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0328
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 514c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 514c
  start_marker: 514c
  end_marker: 514c
  start_char: 136368
  end_char: 136816
  text_sha256: 798136607ec794f5e591691e277cae82eae133c9b37e9258a7cdcf06b59f6d86
greek_terms:
  - οἰκοδομήματα
  - δημοσίοις ἔργοις
  - διδασκάλων
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates uses building as a craft analogy for public competence: public works make sense only after someone has teacher-backed and independent private works to show."
textual_basis: At 514c Socrates says it would be sensible to go into public works after building many fine structures with teachers and on one's own, but foolish to attempt public works without a teacher or worthwhile buildings to display.
limits: This records the public-works consequence of the building analogy. The preceding two-part competence test and the following medical parallel are recorded separately.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0329
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 514c-514d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 514c-514d
  start_marker: 514c
  end_marker: 514d
  start_char: 136368
  end_char: 137219
  text_sha256: fdbb38cfa82181ac00002c82cd2deddeb710f7fc535fa7bfdb0eb9484c5ef927
greek_terms:
  - ἰατροὶ
  - ὑγίειαν
  - νόσου
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates extends the building analogy to medicine, claiming the same principle applies: those presenting themselves as competent doctors should be examined by whether they have cured anyone, including themselves."
textual_basis: "At 514d Socrates extends the principle to everything and then applies the identical structure to medicine: he would examine Callicles and Callicles would examine him — asking whether Socrates himself is healthy and whether anyone, slave or free, has been cured of illness through him."
limits: This observation records the parallel-craft structure. It does not evaluate whether medicine is a more or less fitting analogy than building for the target domain of political activity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0330
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 514c-514d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 514c-514d
  start_marker: 514c
  end_marker: 514d
  start_char: 136368
  end_char: 137219
  text_sha256: fdbb38cfa82181ac00002c82cd2deddeb710f7fc535fa7bfdb0eb9484c5ef927
greek_terms:
  - φῶμεν
  - ὀρθῶς
  - πάνυ γε
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates secures Callicles' assent to the building-analogy principle before extending it to the parallel medical domain.
textual_basis: At 514c-d Socrates asks Callicles whether the building-analogy principle is correctly stated. Callicles answers with strong assent. Only after this assent does Socrates extend the principle and introduce the medical analogy at 514d.
limits: This records the procedural structure of the elenchus at this point. It does not claim that Callicles' assent is coerced or that the extension is fallacious.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0331

  - σεμνύνεται

observation: Socrates compares pilotage to rhetoric as a saving craft that remains modest and orderly.
textual_basis: At 511d-511e Socrates introduces pilotage, saving, the comparison with rhetoric, and the pilot's modest demeanor.
limits: This does not overread the saved souls phrase as a separate psychology claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0332
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 511d-511e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 511d-511e
  start_marker: 511d
  end_marker: 511e
  start_char: 130717
  end_char: 131541
  text_sha256: 26bc00554469e682a4b6b7989adb351a476fa31a47d0c181605b84ac1d561dfc
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The pilot charges a modest fee (two obols for a short trip, two drachmas for a long voyage) despite performing a great benefaction — saving the passenger, children, possessions, and wife — and then walks along the shore in a moderate demeanor (en metrio schemati).
textual_basis: "At 511d-511e, Socrates contrasts the pilot's modest fee scale with the magnitude of the benefit conferred: from Aegina to Athens the pilot charges two obols, from Egypt or the Pontus at most two drachmas, despite having saved people, children, possessions, and wives. After disembarking, the pilot walks alongside the sea and the ship in a moderate manner."
limits: This observation does not assert that the pilot's fee scale is historically accurate; it only records the analogy as Socrates presents it in the text. The observation does not assess whether this contrast successfully undermines rhetoric's claims.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0333
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 511e-512a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - λογίζεσθαι
  - ὠφέληκεν
  - ἔβλαψεν
  - βελτίους
english_gloss: ""
observation: The pilot reasons that it is unclear whom he benefited or harmed by saving, since passengers disembark no better.
textual_basis: At 511e-512a Socrates says the pilot can calculate that it is unclear whom he benefited or harmed because the passengers leave no better than they boarded.
limits: This repairs a source-ref mismatch and family error; it is not a knowledge-disavowal irony marker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0334
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 513b-513c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - ἀνόμοιον
  - μιμητὴν
  - αὐτοφυῶς ὅμοιον
  - ὁμοιότατον
  - ῥητορικόν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates says power in Athens requires likeness to the regime and people, not merely imitation.
textual_basis: At 513b-513c Socrates contrasts being unlike, being an imitator, being naturally similar, and being made political and rhetorical.
limits: This records the political likeness requirement, not a general doctrine of authentic versus mimetic craft.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0335
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 515b-515c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 515b-515c
  start_marker: 515b
  end_marker: 515c
  start_char: 138102
  end_char: 138900
  text_sha256: f560c15904ff7c3d998f79113908cde38286fd8231e401a97398b67ddcbe0328
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: When Socrates asks Callicles to name a single person he has made better through his private association before entering public life, Callicles refuses to answer the question and instead accuses Socrates of being contentious.
textual_basis: At 515b Socrates asks Callicles to state whom he has improved through his company while still a private citizen. Callicles does not answer but replies that Socrates is contentious (philonikos). The move deflects the demand for an answer by recasting the question as an aggressive move rather than a genuine inquiry.
limits: This observation records the refusal and the accusation of contentiousness; it does not assess whether Callicles genuinely has someone to name or whether Socrates' question is fair.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0336
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 515b-515c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 515b-515c
  start_marker: 515b
  end_marker: 515c
  start_char: 138102
  end_char: 138900
  text_sha256: f560c15904ff7c3d998f79113908cde38286fd8231e401a97398b67ddcbe0328
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: When Callicles hesitates to answer, Socrates answers for him on a prior point of agreement, stating that they have repeatedly agreed that the political man must act so that the citizens become as good as possible, and then supplies the answer himself by saying 'we have agreed; I will answer on your behalf.'
textual_basis: At 515c Socrates recapitulates a prior agreement about the task of the political man and, having received no answer from Callicles, supplies the answer himself to move the argument forward.
limits: This observation notes Socrates' procedural move of answering for a silent interlocutor; it does not evaluate whether the prior agreement was genuinely shared or whether Callicles' silence indicates substantive disagreement rather than reluctance.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0337
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 514e-515a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 514e-515a
  start_marker: 514e
  end_marker: 515a
  start_char: 137219
  end_char: 138102
  text_sha256: a32015b9dc586ea5d6f73f7918523689a394945d271a43f6bb2b6be1234a6b16
greek_terms:
  - ἐν τῷ πίθῳ τὴν κεραμείαν
english_gloss: the pottery in the jar — learning pottery by starting with the largest vessel
observation: "Socrates uses a craft analogy about pottery to argue that someone who cannot demonstrate having made individual citizens better should not enter public life, just as someone who has never trained in pottery should not publicly practice it. The analogy reverses Callicles' earlier exhortation: the craft standard Callicles invoked for expert knowledge is now applied back to Callicles himself to test whether he has improved anyone."
textual_basis: At 514e Socrates asks whether it would be ridiculous for someone who has never improved anyone's body to practice medicine publicly, and then invokes the proverbial 'pottery in the jar' (en toi pithoi ten kerameian) at 514e to say one should not try to learn pottery — or public affairs — on the public stage. At 515a he immediately turns the test on Callicles, asking whether Callicles has made any citizen better.
limits: The observation does not assess whether Socrates' analogy is valid or whether Callicles really views politics as a craft. It only records that the craft-analogy frame is used as a reversal device.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0338
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 514e-515a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 514e-515a
  start_marker: 514e
  end_marker: 515a
  start_char: 137219
  end_char: 138102
  text_sha256: a32015b9dc586ea5d6f73f7918523689a394945d271a43f6bb2b6be1234a6b16
greek_terms:
  - βελτίω πεποίηκεν
  - πονηρὸς ὤν
  - καλός τε κἀγαθὸς
english_gloss: has made better; being base; noble and good
observation: Socrates redirects elenctic questioning onto Callicles personally, asking him to name any citizen he has improved. The question picks up Callicles' own earlier rebuke of Socrates for not engaging in politics, and turns it into a Socratic demand for evidence of Callicles' political benefit to individuals.
textual_basis: "At 515a Socrates says: 'Come then, has Callicles already made any citizen better? Is there anyone who, previously being base (poneros), unjust, licentious, and foolish, has become noble and good (kalos te kagathos) through Callicles?' The pattern of using the interlocutor's own standard against him is a core elenctic move. Here the standard is political; Callicles is challenged to produce a named beneficiary."
limits: This observation does not evaluate whether Callicles can or does answer. It notes the structural elenctic move, not the dialogue's outcome. It does not claim Socrates believes in the craft analogy he deploys.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0339
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 514e-515a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 514e-515a
  start_marker: 514e
  end_marker: 515a
  start_char: 137219
  end_char: 138102
  text_sha256: a32015b9dc586ea5d6f73f7918523689a394945d271a43f6bb2b6be1234a6b16
greek_terms:
  - ξένος ἢ ἀστός
  - δοῦλος ἢ ἐλεύθερος
  - Καλλικλῆς
english_gloss: foreigner or citizen; slave or free; Callicles
observation: Socrates challenges Callicles by framing the absence of any named beneficiary as evidence against his claim to political competence. The categories of person who might have been improved — foreigner or citizen, slave or free — are enumerated explicitly, and the absence of a named individual from any category is presented as telling.
textual_basis: "At 515a Socrates asks: 'Is there anyone who…through Callicles has become noble and good, whether foreigner or citizen (xenos e astos), slave or free (doulos e eleutheros)? Tell me.' The exhaustive social taxonomy sets up a test: if even one person cannot be named, the claim to political expertise fails. This uses prosopographic categories as a verification mechanism rather than for dramatic characterization."
limits: The observation does not claim that Socrates genuinely expects Callicles to name someone; it records the rhetorical structure. It does not assess whether Callicles historically improved anyone. It does not address the later part of the dialogue where Callicles may or may not respond.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0342
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 516c-516d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 516c-516d
  start_marker: 516c
  end_marker: 516d
  start_char: 140681
  end_char: 141442
  text_sha256: 7ebf0f6d05742df7e9086d1b85d297936bd9789be12b18a82e02906f68bf0a59
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 516c-516d Socrates pivots from Pericles to three additional Athenian political figures — Cimon, Themistocles, and Miltiades — using the same dialectical pattern: the statesman was exiled or punished by the very citizens he supposedly cared for.
textual_basis: |
  At 516c Socrates secures Callicles' agreement that Pericles made the Athenians more savage and therefore more unjust and worse, then concludes Pericles was not good at politics by that reasoning. At 516d Socrates immediately extends the pattern without waiting for a new agreement, asking Callicles about Cimon (ostracized by those he served so they would not hear his voice for ten years), Themistocles (also ostracized and additionally punished with exile), and Miltiades (the one at Marathon, thrown into the pit). The series is presented as if the same inference from treatment-by-citizens to political incompetence applies.
limits: |
  This observation records the pattern of recalling earlier concessions and applying them to new cases in a chain. It does not evaluate the validity of the inference that exile proves political incompetence. The observation does not claim that Socrates' extension to further figures is a formal elenchus; Callicles' response to the new cases is not quoted in the excerpt.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0343
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 519b-519c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 519b-519c
  start_marker: 519b
  end_marker: 519c
  start_char: 146265
  end_char: 147104
  text_sha256: 1bac5c3a0761e060965323ef0f0b2b2b5333bc8aa8ee252c28cc9417fca482f6
greek_terms:
  - πολιτικοὶ
  - σοφισταί
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 519b-c Socrates draws a parallel between politicians and sophists by noting that both groups claim to be practitioners of a beneficial art yet blame their own beneficiaries for ingratitude and harm. He reports that when the city treats a politician as unjust, the politician complains of suffering injustice despite having done many good things for the city; sophists likewise claim to be teachers of virtue but accuse their students of wronging them by withholding fees and gratitude.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates explicitly compares politicians who pretend to serve the city and sophists who claim to teach virtue: both groups, having allegedly conferred benefits, protest that their beneficiaries treat them unjustly (519b-c). The structure of the comparison runs from the politician's complaint about the city to the sophist's complaint about students who withhold pay and gratitude, making the parallel explicit.
limits: |
  This observation does not assess whether Socrates endorses the parallel or whether the comparison is meant to disparage both groups equally. It registers only the structural analogy drawn between the two professional types at this point in the argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - πάνυ γε
  - φαίνεται
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates secures two consecutive assents from Callicles at 510e: first that the imitator of the ruler will accomplish things in the city, then that the preparation will enable him to commit as many injustices as possible without punishment."
textual_basis: At 510e Socrates asks whether the imitator will also avoid injustice. He immediately answers his own question in the negative and presents the contrary claim that the preparation is for committing injustice unpunished. Callicles assents with the second response term at 510e.
limits: This observation records the assent structure, not whether Callicles' assent is genuine or reluctant.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: Callicles tells Socrates at 511a that he does not know how Socrates twists the arguments up and down each time. He then shifts to a threat about the imitator's power to kill and dispossess.
limits: This observation records the accusation of rhetorical manipulation, not whether Socrates' argument is actually fallacious.
```


```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0347

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates recalls and recaps an earlier distinction between two kinds of care (therapeia) for body and soul, then maps that dual schema onto a prescription for how to treat the city and its citizens.
textual_basis: "At 513d Socrates announces he is resuming the earlier two-part division of care for body and soul: one kind aiming at pleasure, the other at what is best. At 513e he asks whether they must apply this same approach to the city and its citizens by making the citizens as good as possible."
limits: This record notes only the mapping from the body/soul care schema onto civic care. It does not evaluate whether the analogy succeeds or fails, nor does it claim anything about the political implications beyond what is explicit in the question at 513e.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0348

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explicitly brands the kind of care that aims at pleasure as base (agennes) and nothing other than flattery (kolakeia), using the same evaluative term applied earlier in the dialogue to rhetoric.
textual_basis: At 513d-513e Socrates states that one of the two kinds of care is pros hedonen, and that it is agennes and nothing other than kolakeia. This repeats and reinforces the branding of pleasure-oriented practice as kolakeia that runs throughout the Gorgias.
limits: This record notes only the repetition of the kolakeia charge against pleasure-directed care in this segment. It does not claim that this implies anything about Callicles' specific rhetorical or political practice, nor does it assess whether the label is justified.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0349

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explicitly resumes an earlier agreed-upon distinction (the two kinds of care for body and soul) before extending it to a new domain, using a recall question to secure Callicles' assent.
textual_basis: At 513d Socrates says 'Do you recall that we said there are two kinds of preparation for caring for each thing, both body and soul... was this not what we were defining then?' Callicles answers 'Certainly.' This is a textbook case of Socrates anchoring a new step in prior agreed premises before moving forward.
limits: This records only the procedural move of recalling a prior agreement. It does not evaluate whether Callicles genuinely endorses the premise or whether the earlier agreement was properly secured.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0350
source_work: Gorgias

  text_sha256: 465c4354c0ac6bfa23fe2bf05844d32a2ebe1a37151c1fc4f3fba95c70e4d84d
greek_terms:
  - δημηγορεῖν
  - οὐκ ἐθέλων ἀποκρίνεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states that Callicles' refusal to answer has forced him into making long public speeches (demegorein). Callicles responds by asking whether Socrates could speak at all without someone answering him.
textual_basis: At 519d Socrates says Callicles has compelled him to deliver public oratory by not being willing to answer (ouk ethelon apokrinesthai). At 519e Socrates repeats that he is stretching out many speeches because Callicles will not answer. Callicles retorts by asking whether Socrates could speak if no one answered him.
limits: Documents this particular instance of respondent refusal producing macrology; does not establish a general rule about all cases of respondent non-cooperation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0351
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 519d-519e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  start_marker: 519d
  end_marker: 519e
  start_char: 147104
  end_char: 147869
  text_sha256: 465c4354c0ac6bfa23fe2bf05844d32a2ebe1a37151c1fc4f3fba95c70e4d84d
greek_terms:
  - ἄλογον
  - πονηρός
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates secures Callicles' assent to the principle that it is absurd for someone who claims to have made a person good to then blame that person for being wicked. He then applies this principle to those who claim to educate people in virtue, implying they contradict themselves when they blame their own students for being base.
textual_basis: "At 519d-e Socrates poses the absurdity: a teacher removes injustice and instills justice, yet the student commits injustice with what they no longer possess. Callicles assents at 519e that this is absurd. Socrates then asks whether Callicles hears those who profess to educate people in virtue saying just such things, applying the agreed-upon absurdity to a class of claimants."
limits: Reports the reductio structure and its application to the class of virtue-teachers. Does not independently verify whether the virtue-teachers actually make this claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0352

  - διακόνους

  - ἐκπορίζειν
  - μεταβιβάζειν τὰς ἐπιθυμίας
  - ἔργον
  - ἀγαθοῦ πολίτου
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates distinguishes between servants (diakonoi) of the city who provide what it desires and the true work (ergon) of a good citizen, which is to redirect desires and make citizens better.
textual_basis: "At 517b, Socrates calls the statesmen 'servants of the city' and says they were 'more serviceable' (diakonikōteroi) and better at providing (ekporizein) what the city desired. He then contrasts this with the work of a good citizen at 517b-517c: redirecting desires (metabibazein tas epithymias) and not indulging them, persuading and compelling toward what makes citizens better."
limits: This observation records the servant-vs-craftsman distinction in political craft; it does not assess whether the distinction is valid or whether the historical statesmen actually possessed or lacked the art of politics.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates explicitly comments on the interlocutory deadlock, describing the conversation as circling back to the same point without understanding each other.
textual_basis: At 517c, Socrates says 'you and I are doing something ridiculous (geloion) in our speeches' because 'throughout the whole time we've been conversing we never stop circling back to the same point (eis to auto aei peripheromenoi) and not understanding each other's meaning (agnoountes allēlōn ho ti legomen).'
limits: This records an explicit procedural observation by a character; it does not claim the dialogue is actually circular or that Plato endorses Socrates' diagnosis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 517c, Socrates says 'I think you have often (pollakis) agreed (hōmologēkenai) and recognized (egnōkenai) that there are two kinds (dittē hautē tis)...' The sentence breaks off after 'this twofold something,' leaving the referent incomplete in this span.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0355
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 517d-517e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 517d-517e
  start_marker: 517d
  end_marker: 517e
  start_char: 143104
  end_char: 143954
  text_sha256: 750d0c39325fb76245da854ded00d559396a593fc86e28c53600db7a78dae071
greek_terms:
  - διακονική
  - θεραπεία
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates maps a two-part structure (body/soul care) onto the body domain, then distinguishes subordinate provision crafts from master therapeutic crafts. The subordinate crafts (baker, sauce-maker, weaver, cobbler, tanner) supply goods (food, drink, clothing, bedding, shoes) for bodily desires but are not genuine body-therapy (517d-517e). Only gymnastic and medicine are the true therapeutic crafts that should rule the subordinate ones.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates explicitly states that the practical activity (he pragmateia) concerns both body and soul, then elaborates only the body side (517d). He lists provision crafts by example and contrasts them with gymnastic and medicine as the two genuine master crafts (technai) of the body (517e).
limits: |
  The passage announces a body-soul parallel but does not yet develop the soul side; the mapping remains asymmetric within this segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0356
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 517d-517e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - ποριστικὸν
  - θεραπευτὴν
  - γυμναστική
  - ἰατρική
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates argues that subordinate provision crafts appear to be body-therapists both to their own practitioners and to the ignorant, but true body-therapy belongs only to gymnastic and medicine, which must rule over the provision crafts and use their products (517d-517e).
textual_basis: |
  Socrates says it is not surprising that the provision craftsman seems to be a body-therapist (therapeuten somatos) to anyone ignorant of gymnastic and medicine, and that these two ruling crafts properly command and use the products of the subordinate crafts (517e).
limits: |
  This observation captures the craft hierarchy claim within the body domain; the passage does not yet extend this hierarchy to the soul domain.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0357
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 516a-516b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 516a-516b
  start_marker: 516a
  end_marker: 516b
  start_char: 139858
  end_char: 140681
  text_sha256: 7b99ce4511baa60d61aa596c4d5ca11f800d9c72eb5ca994c710d92b7a50a84b
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates compares Pericles' political leadership to the craft of an animal caretaker (epimeletes). The criterion for a good caretaker is that animals received tame should not become wilder under his care. Socrates then secures the premise that man is one of the animals and that Pericles took care of men, mapping the caretaker criterion onto political rule.
textual_basis: At 516a-516b, Socrates states that a caretaker of donkeys, horses, and cattle who received them not kicking, butting, or biting but made them wild would be considered bad (kakos). He then asks Callicles whether man is one of the animals (516b), secures agreement, and notes Pericles took care of men, setting up the implication that Pericles made the Athenians worse rather than better.
limits: This observation records the structure of the craft analogy. It does not assess whether the analogy is sound or whether Socrates' conclusion about Pericles follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0358
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 516a-516b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 516a-516b
  start_marker: 516a
  end_marker: 516b
  start_char: 139858
  end_char: 140681
  text_sha256: 7b99ce4511baa60d61aa596c4d5ca11f800d9c72eb5ca994c710d92b7a50a84b
greek_terms:
  - ἵνα σοι χαρίσωμαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles explicitly signals that his assent is not genuine agreement but a concession to gratify Socrates, using the phrase 'in order that I might gratify you' (hina soi charisomai) at 516b.
textual_basis: At 516b, after Socrates asks whether a caretaker who makes animals wilder is bad, Callicles responds with 'Certainly, in order that I might gratify you.' The purpose clause marks the assent as a social concession rather than genuine agreement.
limits: This observation records Callicles' explicit qualification of his assent. It does not determine whether he actually agreed with the premise or was merely being dismissive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0359
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 520c-520d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 520c-520d
  start_marker: 520c
  end_marker: 520d
  start_char: 148681
  end_char: 149477
  text_sha256: 6d74179426a811ef199cae43bffa884de7e40bea38c97b5d90ea89df044ae6c2
greek_terms:
  - ἀδικίαν
  - ἀγαθοὺς ποιεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates distinguishes the benefit of removing injustice — making people good — from craft benefits such as physical training and house-building by arguing that only the former is safe to offer without payment, because a recipient made good will not commit injustice against the benefactor.
textual_basis: At 520c-d Socrates contrasts the paidotribes making someone fast with the person who removes injustice. With craft benefits the recipient might withhold gratitude if no fee was arranged; but one who removes injustice need not fear being wronged, so it is uniquely safe to offer this benefit without pay. He concludes that charging for other kinds of counsel such as building is not shameful.
limits: This observation does not address whether Socrates claims to remove injustice or make people good. It records only the structural argument distinguishing moral improvement from craft benefits by the criterion of payment safety.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0360
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 520e-521a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 520e-521a
  start_marker: 520e
  end_marker: 521a
  start_char: 149477
  end_char: 150331
  text_sha256: 6d6c81b7b710f6901008f47ec0768b9203b53b29beced8c7b58118b59b479b51
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates frames his potential role in the city through a medical analogy, asking Callicles to distinguish between two kinds of therapeia tes poleos: the one that contends with the Athenians so that they become as excellent as possible, like a doctor, and the one that serves and converses to gratify them."
textual_basis: At 521a, Socrates asks Callicles to specify epi poteran ten therapeian tes poleos — to the kind that fights to make the Athenians as best as possible (hos iatron, like a doctor), or to the kind that serves and speaks for their gratification (hos diakonesonta kai pros charin homilesonta). The medical analogy structures a binary choice about political engagement, mapping the doctor's corrective role onto the political domain.
limits: This observation records the craft analogy as a textual structuring device at this moment. It does not interpret whether Socrates endorses one option, whether the analogy extends to other dialogues, or whether the doctor analogy is consistent with other craft comparisons in the Gorgias.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0361
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 520e-521a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 520e-521a
  start_marker: 520e
  end_marker: 521a
  start_char: 149477
  end_char: 150331
  text_sha256: 6d6c81b7b710f6901008f47ec0768b9203b53b29beced8c7b58118b59b479b51
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates imposes a strict binary choice on Callicles, demanding he specify which of two mutually exclusive modes of political engagement Socrates should pursue, and reinforces the demand with an appeal to Callicles's own frank-speaking character.
textual_basis: "At 521a, Socrates presents the question as a pair introduced by epi poteran ... ten ... e hos (to which kind ... or as). He then explicitly demands a truthful answer (talethe moi eipe), invokes Callicles's obligation to continue his frank speech (dikaios gar ei, hosper erxo parresiazesthai pros eme, diatelein ha noeis legon), and closes with an emphatic double imperative (kai nun eu kai gennaios eipe). Callicles answers with only three words: lego toinun hoti hos diakonesonta."
limits: This observation records the binary choice structure and the procedural demand for an answer. It does not claim that the binary is exhaustive or that Callicles's three-word response signals capitulation or irony.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0362
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 520e-521a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 520e-521a
  start_marker: 520e
  end_marker: 521a
  start_char: 149477
  end_char: 150331
  text_sha256: 6d6c81b7b710f6901008f47ec0768b9203b53b29beced8c7b58118b59b479b51
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates reframes his role relative to the city by asking Callicles to position him on one side of a political dichotomy, explicitly invoking Callicles's prior commitment to frank speech (parrhesia) as the warrant for demanding a truthful answer.
textual_basis: Socrates calls back to Callicles's earlier self-presentation as a frank speaker with the words hosper erxo parresiazesthai pros eme at 521a. He uses that past commitment to justify a new demand for a binding, public-facing answer on the kind of political role Socrates should adopt. This transforms the conversation from a debate about rhetoric into an explicit personal-political positioning.
limits: This observation notes that Socrates uses the dramatic history of the conversation (Callicles's claim to parrhesia) to extract a consequential self-positioning answer. It does not interpret whether Socrates's move is sincere, ironic, or intended to trap Callicles.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0363
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 522a-522b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 522a-522b
  start_marker: 522a
  end_marker: 522b
  start_char: 152042
  end_char: 152916
  text_sha256: 025c627383c675bca05c89bf96b00b5b4f2825d212a9565d70de14afe1ddbd91
greek_terms:
  - ἀπορίᾳ
english_gloss: at a loss, perplexity
observation: Socrates extends the doctor/cook craft analogy into a courtroom scenario. The doctor who administered bitter but healthy treatments would be at a complete loss when judged by a jury of children accustomed to pleasant and varied fare. The doctor can only say he acted for their health, and Socrates predicts the jury would shout him down.
textual_basis: At 522a, the doctor's hypothetical defense is that he did everything for health, and Socrates asks Callicles how loudly such jurors would shout. Callicles agrees it would be loud.
limits: This records the courtroom extension of the craft analogy; it does not assess whether the analogy is valid or whether Socrates' self-comparison to the doctor is accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0364
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 522a-522b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 522a-522b
  start_marker: 522a

  end_char: 152916
  text_sha256: 025c627383c675bca05c89bf96b00b5b4f2825d212a9565d70de14afe1ddbd91
greek_terms:
  - πάθος
  - ἀπορεῖν
  - πικροὺς λόγους
english_gloss: suffering/experience; to make perplexed; harsh words
observation: Socrates explicitly maps the hypothetical doctor's courtroom predicament onto his own anticipated trial experience, stating he would suffer the same thing if he entered a lawcourt. He anticipates he could not point to pleasures he has provided and would be accused of corrupting the young by making them perplexed and of speaking harsh words to elders in private or public.
textual_basis: At 522a-b, Socrates says he knows he would suffer the same experience and lists what he could not say in court and what accusations he would face, including corrupting the young and harsh speech against elders.
limits: This records Socrates' self-characterization of anticipated courtroom vulnerability. It does not assess whether this matches the Apology trial narrative or whether the self-characterization is accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0365
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 515d-515e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 515d-515e
  start_marker: 515d
  end_marker: 515e
  start_char: 138900
  end_char: 139858
  text_sha256: 22c244b65ea7dd88837e6f633e71be3394157e51ef3d292b5118db38c766de12
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates forces Callicles to accept that if Pericles was a good citizen, then by their prior agreement (the good man makes citizens better) he necessarily made the Athenians better. Socrates invokes this prior agreement from the earlier discussion as a binding premise that compels the conclusion regardless of Callicles' hedging with 'perhaps' (isos).
textual_basis: Socrates says 'not perhaps, best of men, but necessarily from what has been agreed, if indeed that man was a good citizen' (515d). He explicitly calls back to a previously established premise (the good citizen improves citizens) and rejects Callicles' attempt to answer with a qualified 'perhaps' rather than assent to the necessary implication.
limits: This observation records the move of binding an interlocutor to a prior agreement. It does not evaluate whether Pericles was in fact a good citizen or whether the premise is sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0366
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 515d-515e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 515d-515e
  start_marker: 515d
  end_marker: 515e
  start_char: 138900
  end_char: 139858
  text_sha256: 22c244b65ea7dd88837e6f633e71be3394157e51ef3d292b5118db38c766de12
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates introduces a catalogue of named Athenian political leaders — Pericles, Cimon, Miltiades, and Themistocles — as test cases for the claim that good citizens make citizens better. He then turns public reputation against Pericles by reporting the charge that Pericles made Athenians lazy, cowardly, talkative, and money-loving by introducing payment for public service (misthophoria).
textual_basis: "Callicles names four leaders at 515d: 'Pericles and Cimon and Miltiades and Themistocles.' At 515e Socrates reports what he has heard: 'Pericles made Athenians idle and cowardly and talkative and money-loving, having first established them in pay for service (misthophoria).' Callicles counters that Socrates is listening to men with 'broken ears' (i.e., people who have been beaten up)."
limits: This observation records the introduction of historical figures as test cases and the deployment of public reputation for refutation. It does not assess the historical accuracy of the charge against Pericles.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0367
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 515d-515e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 515d-515e
  start_marker: 515d
  end_marker: 515e
  start_char: 138900
  end_char: 139858
  text_sha256: 22c244b65ea7dd88837e6f633e71be3394157e51ef3d292b5118db38c766de12
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: When Socrates asks whether the Athenians were worse when Pericles first began to speak in the assembly than when he gave his final speeches, Callicles answers with a hedging 'perhaps' (isos). Socrates immediately rejects the hedge as impermissible, insisting that the conclusion follows necessarily from what has been agreed, and reasserts procedural control by saying 'tell me this further point' (tode moi eipe epi toutoi).
textual_basis: "At 515d Callicles says 'isos' (perhaps), which Socrates rejects: 'Not perhaps, best of men, but necessarily from what has been agreed' (ouk isos de, o beltiste, all' ananke ek ton homologemenon). At 515e Socrates adds: 'But tell me this further point about him' (alla tode moi eipe epi toutoi), redirecting the inquiry."
limits: This observation records the procedural move of rejecting a respondent's hedging answer and redirecting the argument. It does not claim that Callicles' 'perhaps' constitutes a formal procedural violation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0368
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 518c-518d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 518c-518d
  start_marker: 518c
  end_marker: 518d
  start_char: 144809
  end_char: 145593
  text_sha256: b179f438187c204c8266bf316670c013121221222679f0a2ec6ebb96b3a08700
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates deploys the medical body-care analogy against the political flatterers by casting food-providers as merely appetitive preparers (paraskeuastai epithymion) who lack gymnastic knowledge. The victims later misattribute the cause of disease to late-arriving advisers rather than to the original feeders, paralleling the claim that the Athenian demos blames present rhetors rather than the earlier flatterers who caused the harm.
textual_basis: At 518c-518d Socrates describes cooks and food-providers as diakonoi and preparers of appetites (epithymion paraskeuastas) who know nothing kalon kagathon. They fill and fatten bodies (emplêsantes kai pachynantes ta sômata), then victims blame those present when disease strikes rather than the original feeders. This is the medical-analogy vehicle for the political argument.
limits: This records the medical-body analogy at 518c-518d only; it does not evaluate whether the analogy succeeds or assess the wider political target outside this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0369
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 518c-518d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 518c-518d
  start_marker: 518c
  end_marker: 518d
  start_char: 144809
  end_char: 145593
  text_sha256: b179f438187c204c8266bf316670c013121221222679f0a2ec6ebb96b3a08700
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates introduces an extended hypothetical scenario at 518c-518d: an imagined interlocutor would grow angry (aganaktein) if told that the food-providers he listed are ignorant of gymnastic and are merely appetitive preparers. The scenario traces a causal chain where victims misattribute the cause of delayed disease to late-arriving advisers rather than to the original feeders."
textual_basis: At 518c Socrates marks the hypothetical frame with 'isos an oun eganakteis, ei soi elegon ego...' introducing a counterfactual address to an imagined interlocutor. The scenario then unfolds across 518c-518d, describing how the fattened victims later blame (aitiasontai, psexousin, kakon ti poiesousin) those present at the time of illness rather than the original preparers.
limits: This records the hypothetical-prompt device at 518c-518d; it does not claim that this is the only such device in the Gorgias or that it constitutes a proof. The argumentative force of the hypothetical is not evaluated.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0370
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 523b-523c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 523b-523c
  start_marker: 523b
  end_marker: 523c
  start_char: 154478
  end_char: 155369
  text_sha256: f79326cde6c8281647abfc226e0dc67f15b8ecde9cb7ed0624456b322d6090c3
greek_terms:
  - μακάρων νήσους
  - Τάρταρον
  - Πλούτων
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The mythic narrative at 523b-523c names two distinct post-mortem destinations — the Isles of the Blessed (for the piously lived) and Tartarus (for the unjust and impious) — and populates the administrative structure with Pluto and overseers from the Isles of the Blessed who report to Zeus about unworthy arrivals.
textual_basis: |
  At 523b Socrates reports that those who lived piously go to the Isles of the Blessed and those unjustly and impiously to the prison of punishment and justice called Tartarus. Pluto and the overseers from the Isles of the Blessed then complain to Zeus (523b-523c) that unworthy people arrive at both destinations.
limits: |
  This observation records the named locations and divine personnel of the afterlife judgment myth. It does not analyze the philosophical significance of the topography or its relation to other Platonic afterlife accounts.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0371
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 523b-523c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 523b-523c
  start_marker: 523b
  end_marker: 523c
  start_char: 154478
  end_char: 155369
  text_sha256: f79326cde6c8281647abfc226e0dc67f15b8ecde9cb7ed0624456b322d6090c3
greek_terms:
  - ἀμπεχόμενοι
  - ἠμφιεσμένοι
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The myth describes a flawed prior judgment procedure in which living judges judged the living on their day of death (523b), resulting in bad verdicts. Zeus diagnoses the cause at 523c: those being judged are concealed by beautiful bodies, lineage, and wealth, and bring many witnesses to testify to their just lives, making the true condition of the soul invisible to the judges.
textual_basis: |
  At 523b the judges under Cronus and early Zeus are described as living judging the living on the day they are about to die, with the result that cases were badly decided. At 523c Zeus announces he will stop this, explaining that the judged are clothed in fine bodies, families, and wealth, and that many witnesses come forward to testify on their behalf.
limits: |
  This records the structural flaw diagnosed within the mythic narrative and Zeus's announced reform. It does not evaluate whether the diagnosis maps onto Athenian judicial practice or Socratic claims about the soul elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0372
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 521b-521c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  end_char: 151141
  text_sha256: 82dba9732a5eee172b47d83b90ecd8fc039a225462ad576276637028635a1e89
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates interrupts Callicles mid-threat and preemptively supplies his own counter-argument, preventing Callicles from completing a threat he has made repeatedly in the dialogue.
textual_basis: At 521b, Callicles begins "if you don't do these things—" and Socrates cuts him off with "Don't say what you've often said, that whoever wishes will kill me." Socrates then immediately provides his own pre-formulated response before Callicles can finish the threat.
limits: This does not prove that Callicles would have completed the same threat; it only records that Socrates anticipates and preempts it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0373
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 521b-521c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - ἀδίκως
  - αἰσχρῶς
  - κακῶς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates delivers a rapid three-step cascade from "unjustly" to "shamefully" to "badly" without pausing for interlocutor assent.
textual_basis: "At 521b-c, Socrates states: if he took it unjustly, he will use it unjustly, and if unjustly, shamefully, and if shamefully, badly. Each step follows from the previous without any interlocutor response."
limits: This observation records the formal structure of the move, not whether the chain is logically valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0374
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 521b-521c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 521b-521c
  start_marker: 521b
  end_marker: 521c
  start_char: 150331
  end_char: 151141
  text_sha256: 82dba9732a5eee172b47d83b90ecd8fc039a225462ad576276637028635a1e89
greek_terms:
  - ἀνόητος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates calls himself senseless for allegedly trusting he would never be prosecuted, while the statement actually asserts the serious point that anyone in Athens is vulnerable to prosecution.
textual_basis: At 521c, after Callicles suggests Socrates trusts he lives out of harm's way, Socrates responds "I'm truly senseless, Callicles, if I don't think that in this city whoever it might be could suffer whatever turns out." The apparent self-deprecation frames a serious claim about Athenian legal vulnerability.
limits: This does not determine whether Socrates' professed confidence about his own fate is genuine or feigned.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0375
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 524c-524d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 524c-524d
  start_marker: 524c
  end_marker: 524d
  start_char: 157037
  end_char: 157919
  text_sha256: 9d22495b597d245b1c3ec5685d49f9cc04a84c7a1257623a7fc5f4d6128fbb01
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates develops an analogy at 524c-524d in which the condition of the body after death—its size, fatness, hair, scars from whippings, and broken or distorted limbs—remains visible on the corpse, and he extends this principle to the soul: after the soul is stripped of the body, everything belonging to its nature and the affections it acquired through practices become visible."
textual_basis: At 524c Socrates enumerates bodily markers (megas, pachys, kometes, mastigias with oulai, kateagota mele, diestrammena) that persist on the corpse and says these same things are endela even after death. At 524d he draws the parallel with tauto tout' ara kai peri ten psychen, stating that endela panta estin en te psyche once it is gymnothe tou somatos, including the tes physeos and pathemata acquired dia ten epitedeusin.
limits: This observation records the structural analogy between post-mortem bodily appearance and post-mortem soul visibility; it does not assess whether the analogy holds or what specific soul conditions are visible.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0376
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 524c-524d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 524d the soul, stripped of the body (gymnothe tou somatos), arrives at the judge (para ton dikasten) for post-mortem judgment, continuing the mythic judgment framework of the Gorgias closing myth.
textual_basis: At 524d Socrates says epeidan oun aphikontai para ton dikasten and immediately identifies arrivals hoi men ek tes Asias, placing the stripped-soul analogy in the service of the mythic underworld judgment scene that runs through the remainder of the dialogue.
limits: This observation captures that the bodily-psychic analogy feeds into the mythic afterlife-judgment frame already established earlier in the Gorgias myth; it does not analyze the judgment content or verdict criteria.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0377
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 523d-523e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 523d-523e
  start_marker: 523d
  end_marker: 523e
  start_char: 155369
  end_char: 156210
  text_sha256: e117b2f589db6253f0a2a2b57636be41b7fa37d5db84470700183c5e41925cda
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates reports a myth in which the judges of the dead are deceived by the clothing and bodies of the judged; the current procedure is that people are judged while still alive and clothed, and Zeus proposes two reforms: first, that people should no longer know their death in advance (a change already communicated to Prometheus at 523d-e), and second, that both the judged and the judge should be stripped bare and dead so that judgment occurs soul to soul."
textual_basis: "At 523d, the mythical narrative reports that judges are dazzled (ekplettontai) because they judge with their own soul covered by eyes and ears and the whole body, and that the garments of both judges and judged obstruct (epiprosthen) the process (523d). The reform is stated at 523d-e: first, people must cease knowing death in advance, a task already assigned to Prometheus; second, they must be judged naked of all these things (gumnous ... hapanton touton), dead, and the judge must be dead, examining soul with soul (523e)."
limits: This observation records the mythic reform narrative as presented by Socrates; it does not assess whether the myth is intended literally or allegorically, nor does it evaluate the philosophical implications of the soul-to-soul judgment image.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0378
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 523d-523e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 523d-523e
  start_marker: 523d
  end_marker: 523e
  start_char: 155369
  end_char: 156210
  text_sha256: e117b2f589db6253f0a2a2b57636be41b7fa37d5db84470700183c5e41925cda
greek_terms:
  - γυμνὸν
  - τεθνεῶτα
  - αὐτῇ τῇ ψυχῇ αὐτὴν τὴν ψυχὴν θεωροῦντα
english_gloss: ""
observation: The myth specifies that the judge must be naked and dead, examining soul itself with soul itself (523e), and that the judged soul must be stripped of all kinship ties and leave behind all the adornment on earth so that the judgment may be just.
textual_basis: "At 523e, Socrates reports Zeus's decree: the judge must be naked and dead, gazing upon soul with soul itself (autei tei psychei auten ten psychen theorounta) of each person suddenly deceased, bereft of all kin and having left behind all that earthly adornment, in order that the judgment may be just (hina dikaia he krisis ei)."
limits: This observation records the soul-to-soul judgment image within the myth; it does not claim that this image directly maps onto any philosophical doctrine about the soul's nature, nor does it interpret the stripping of kinship and adornment as an ethical argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0379
source_work: Gorgias

  end_char: 156210
  text_sha256: e117b2f589db6253f0a2a2b57636be41b7fa37d5db84470700183c5e41925cda
greek_terms:
  - Μίνω
  - Ῥαδάμανθυν
  - ὑεῖς ἐμαυτοῦ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates adds that he, knowing these things before his interlocutors, appointed his own sons as judges: two from Asia, Minos and Rhadamanthys (523e). This embeds the mythic appointment of afterlife judges within the reported speech and ties the myth to established Greek mythic figures."
textual_basis: "At 523e, Socrates states: 'I, having recognized these things before you, appointed as judges my own sons, two from Asia, Minos and Rhadamanthys' (ego men oun tauta egnokos proteros e hymeis epoiesamen dikastas hyeis emautou, dyo men ek tes Asias, Mino te kai Rhadamanthyn). The passage attributes the appointment to Zeus and names the specific mythic judges."
limits: This observation notes the naming of mythic judges and the embedding of the appointment within the mythic frame; it does not evaluate the selection criteria for the judges or the significance of their geographic origin.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0380
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 525b-525c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 525b-525c
  start_marker: 525b
  end_marker: 525c
  start_char: 158584
  end_char: 159478
  text_sha256: 05360fedcbcf235b2bb40938051ebe3aac6ffaf0aa80dcbde4f9d43373886eff
greek_terms:
  - ἰάσιμα ἁμαρτήματα
  - ἀνίατοι
  - παραδείγματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Within the myth of final judgment, Socrates distinguishes two classes of punished souls: those with curable wrongs who are benefited and improved through punishment, and those with extreme, incurable wrongs who are not themselves benefited but serve as examples for others.
textual_basis: |
  At 525b the account states that those being punished are either improved and benefited or become an example so that others seeing them become better. At 525c, those with curable wrongs receive benefit through pain, while those who commit the worst wrongs and become incurable serve as examples, not benefiting themselves but benefiting others who see them.
limits: |
  The observation records the curable/incurable distinction within the eschatological myth; it does not assess whether this penal theory is endorsed by Socrates beyond the mythic frame.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0381

  - νουθετήματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The incurable wrongdoers in Hades are described as being hung up as examples in the prison-house, explicitly serving as spectacles and warnings for the unjust souls who arrive there.
textual_basis: |
  At 525c, Socrates says that the incurable become examples, hung up in the prison-house in Hades, as spectacles and warnings for each arriving group of unjust souls.
limits: |
  The observation records the exemplary-display function attributed to the incurable within the eschatological myth; it does not claim this is a penal theory Socrates endorses outside the mythic frame.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0382
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 518a-518b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 518a-518b
  start_marker: 518a
  end_marker: 518b
  start_char: 143954
  end_char: 144809
  text_sha256: 38863422e91078755e1ca6fd3a7b53862498fca206c57d1061615dd2ec8a4673
greek_terms:
  - δουλοπρεπεῖς
  - διακονικὰς
  - ἀνελευθέρους
  - δεσποίνας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates maps a master/servant hierarchy onto two pairs of bodily crafts: gymnastic and medicine are the mistresses, while other bodily practices are servile, menial, and illiberal (518a). He then claims the same distinction applies to the soul and signals that Callicles has not grasped this structural parallel."
textual_basis: At 518a Socrates states that gymnastic and medicine are justly the mistresses of the other bodily practices, which are servile, menial, and illiberal. He then asserts that the same structure holds concerning the soul (518a) and remarks that Callicles seems to understand momentarily before reverting to naming 'noble and good' citizens at 518b.
limits: The passage asserts a structural parallel but does not develop the soul-side mapping in this span. It leaves unspecified which psychic practices correspond to the mistress and servile crafts.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0383
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 524e-525a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 524e-525a
  start_marker: 524e
  end_marker: 525a
  start_char: 157919
  end_char: 158584
  text_sha256: a0ce87e601c9ee3f1376af7c06efb83ec686146fa40f9b2226b54f30298ac64d
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "524e-525a: Rhadamanthys inspects the soul of each deceased person. When he seizes the soul of someone who was a great king or dynast, he finds it scarred and full of welts from perjuries and injustice, and sends it dishonored straight to the guardhouse (phroura) where it must endure fitting sufferings."
textual_basis: "The passage describes a judicial inspection of bare souls after death (524e: Rhadamanthys examines each soul without knowing whose it is), specifies the marks left by wrongdoing (whip-marks and scars from perjuries and injustice, 524e-525a), and assigns a punitive destination (the phroura, 525a). This extends the mythic afterlife topography introduced earlier in the dialogue."
limits: This observation does not address whether the guardhouse is the same as Tartarus mentioned elsewhere in the myth, or whether souls eventually return. It only records the penal inspection and dispatch described at 524e-525a.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0384
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 524e-525a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 524e-525a
  start_marker: 524e
  end_marker: 525a
  start_char: 157919
  end_char: 158584
  text_sha256: a0ce87e601c9ee3f1376af7c06efb83ec686146fa40f9b2226b54f30298ac64d
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "524e-525a: Souls bear permanently visible marks of their deeds. The soul of the great king is described as scarred (oulon meste) and crooked (skolia) from falsehood and imposture, having been nurtured without truth. The bodily metaphor of scarring gives the soul a legible surface that Rhadamanthys inspects."
textual_basis: At 524e-525a Socrates says each action stamps its imprint on the soul (hekaste he praxis exomorzato), producing welts from perjury and injustice and making the soul crooked from lies and pretension. The soul is described as full of asymmetry and shamefulness (asummetrias te kai aischrotetos gemousan), visible to the judge.
limits: This does not claim that the scar-metaphor is unique to Plato; it records only that this passage introduces a legible-soul motif within the mythic judgment scene. It does not address whether this is a doctrine held by Socrates outside the myth.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0385
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 524e-525a

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  text_sha256: a0ce87e601c9ee3f1376af7c06efb83ec686146fa40f9b2226b54f30298ac64d
greek_terms:
  - οὐκ εἰδὼς
  - μεγάλου βασιλέως
  - δυνάστου
  - ἀτίμως
english_gloss: ""
observation: "In the judgment myth, worldly rank gives no protection: Rhadamanthys does not know whose soul he is examining, and the soul of a great king or dynast can be found disordered and sent away dishonored."
textual_basis: At 524e-525a Rhadamanthys examines souls without knowing whose they are. The example of a great king or dynast is judged by the soul's wounds, crookedness, falsehood, luxury, hubris, and lack of measure, and is sent dishonorably to punishment.
limits: This records the local inversion of civic status under mythic soul-judgment. It does not claim that a private-person counterexample appears in this span or that the same inversion pattern has the same function elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0386

observation: The afterlife judgment singles out the philosopher who minded his own business and did not meddle as the most likely soul to be sent to the Isles of the Blessed.
textual_basis: At 526c Socrates says that when Rhadamanthys sees a soul that has lived piously and with truth, especially a philosopher who did his own things and did not meddle in life, he admires it and sends it to the Isles of the Blessed.
limits: Does not claim that only the philosopher reaches the Isles of the Blessed; the passage mentions any private man or other person who lived piously. Does not establish a general doctrine of philosophical salvation.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0387

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: A Homeric quotation describing Minos holding a golden scepter and giving judgment to the dead is embedded within Socrates' mythic account of the afterlife judgment.
textual_basis: "At 526c-d Socrates cites Homer's Odysseus as witness: Minos sits overseeing judgment alone with a golden scepter, as Odysseus reports seeing him (Odyssey 11.569). The quotation is set off as direct poetic citation within the mythic narrative."
limits: Records the presence of a poetic authority citation within a mythic frame, not whether Socrates endorses Homeric authority in general or whether the citation is accurate to the Homeric text.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0388
```
