```yaml
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates declares he is personally persuaded by these mythic accounts and will orient his life toward presenting a healthy soul to the judge, dismissing the honors of the many.
textual_basis: At 526d Socrates states he is persuaded by these accounts and will try to show the judge a soul as healthy as possible; he will dismiss the honors of the many, practice truth, and strive to be as good as possible both in life and in death.
limits: Records Socrates' stated persuasion and practical resolution, not whether the myth is presented as literal truth or whether Socrates' commitment extends to every detail of the mythic narrative.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0389
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 527b-527c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 527b-527c
  start_marker: 527b
  end_marker: 527c
  start_char: 162614
  end_char: 163517
  text_sha256: 18a24c2c70619a438634da7b9432207258d1afdfb27844c25ef24a04ee607020
greek_terms:
  - εὐδαιμονήσεις
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates recapitulates the single argument that has survived refutation (527b): doing injustice is to be guarded against more than suffering it, being good rather than seeming good is what must be practiced both privately and publicly, and if someone becomes bad in some respect, he must be punished as the second-best good after being just. He then extends the summary to prescribe avoidance of all flattery and the use of rhetoric always for the just (527c). He closes by urging Callicles to follow where the argument leads, promising happiness in life and death if he does so."
textual_basis: Socrates uses a compact chain (527b-527c) framed by the statement that "this argument alone remains undisturbed" (527b) while the others have been refuted. He enumerates the positive conclusions and then gives the exhortation to follow (527c), concluding with the promise of eudaimonia.
limits: This observation records the final argument-summary-and-exhortation structure. It does not assess whether the argument's conclusions are valid or whether Callicles is persuaded.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0390
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 527b-527c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 527b-527c
  start_marker: 527b
  end_marker: 527c
  start_char: 162614
  end_char: 163517
  text_sha256: 18a24c2c70619a438634da7b9432207258d1afdfb27844c25ef24a04ee607020
greek_terms:
  - εὐλαβητέον ἐστὶν τὸ ἀδικεῖν μᾶλλον ἢ τὸ ἀδικεῖσθαι
  - οὐ τὸ δοκεῖν εἶναι ἀγαθὸν ἀλλὰ τὸ εἶναι
  - κολαστέος ἐστί
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'At 527b Socrates identifies a single argument as the sole survivor ("this argument alone remains undisturbed") among many that have been tested and refuted. He synthesizes three ethical claims under that one surviving argument: (1) doing injustice is to be guarded against more than suffering it, (2) one must practice being good rather than seeming good, privately and publicly, and (3) punishment is a second-best good after being just.'
textual_basis: Socrates explicitly states that in such a great number of arguments, with the others refuted, only this argument remains undisturbed (527b). He then presents the three claims as its content. This is a synthesis of the dialogue's elenctic results into a single surviving position.
limits: This observation records the synthesis gesture, not an evaluation of whether the argument actually withstands all refutations. It also does not comment on Callicles' earlier silence or withdrawal.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0391
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 527c-527c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 527c-527c
  start_marker: 527c
  end_marker: 527c
  start_char: 163068
  end_char: 163517
  text_sha256: c30fca628f9f8a70866b480274d5b56ee7f8d1364b286c3be2582c536d13b2fa
greek_terms:
  - κολακείαν
  - ῥητορικῇ
  - εὐδαιμονήσεις
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'At 527c Socrates delivers a direct ethical exhortation to Callicles: "Be persuaded by me and follow where, having arrived, you will be happy both living and having died, as the argument indicates." He also tells Callicles to let anyone despise him as foolish and abuse him, and even to strike him with confidence, implying that following the just life renders external dishonor irrelevant.'
textual_basis: Socrates uses first-person imperative language ("follow me") and a happiness promise conditioned on following the argument's lead (527c). The exhortation is embedded in the argument-summary closure of the dialogue.
limits: This observation records the exhortation form and its placement. It does not claim that Callicles accepts the exhortation or that Socrates' argument has been proven.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0392
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 520a-520b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 520a-520b
  start_marker: 520a
  end_marker: 520b
  start_char: 147869
  end_char: 148681
  text_sha256: 60c619dac3077d860e7687d2c85498e80fbca05c64a4ee5bac86ec2bc30b14c7
greek_terms:
  - σοφιστὴς
  - ῥήτωρ
english_gloss: sophist, rhetor
observation: Socrates again equates the sophist and the rhetor (or calls them nearly the same), echoing his earlier remark to Polus, and then claims that in truth sophistic is finer than rhetoric in the same proportion that legislation is finer than judging and gymnastic finer than medicine.
textual_basis: "At 520a-b Socrates says the sophist and rhetor are the same or close to it, as he had told Polus. He then reverses what Callicles expects by declaring sophistic finer than rhetoric, supporting the claim with a two-part craft analogy: nomothetic to judicial, gymnastic to medical."
limits: The observation does not assess whether the craft hierarchy is defensible or consistent with other passages in the Gorgias; it only records the claim and its analogical structure in this span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0393
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 520a-520b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 520a-520b
  start_marker: 520a
  end_marker: 520b
  start_char: 147869
  end_char: 148681
  text_sha256: 60c619dac3077d860e7687d2c85498e80fbca05c64a4ee5bac86ec2bc30b14c7
greek_terms:
  - δημηγόροις
  - σοφισταῖς
english_gloss: popular orators, sophists
observation: Socrates argues that popular orators and sophists alone cannot consistently blame the very activity they teach for being harmful to their pupils, because doing so would also be an accusation against themselves for having failed to benefit those they claim to benefit.
textual_basis: At 520b Socrates states that he thought only the demagogues and sophists could not find fault with the practice they themselves teach as harmful to themselves, or else on the same argument they would simultaneously accuse themselves of having provided no benefit.
limits: This observation records the logical structure of the self-refutation charge. It does not evaluate whether Socrates himself teaches a practice that could be subject to the same charge.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0394
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 525d-525e

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  text_sha256: 05af4a813840de34df96eeeb15ddcf9df6e96a5303a786229cb0f3d159afe19d
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates cites Homer's depiction of Tantalus, Sisyphus, and Tityus as eternal sufferers in Hades to support the claim that the greatest wrongdoers come from tyrants, kings, and dynasts.
textual_basis: At 525d-e Socrates says Homer bears witness to the claim that rulers commit the greatest injustices; Homer made Tantalus, Sisyphus, and Tityus suffer eternally in Hades, while private wrongdoers like Thersites are not shown undergoing great punishments.
limits: The observation records only that Homer's authority is invoked as evidence in a moral argument. It does not assess whether the citation is accurate or whether Socrates' use of Homer is ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0395
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 525d-525e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  text_sha256: 05af4a813840de34df96eeeb15ddcf9df6e96a5303a786229cb0f3d159afe19d
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates frames the Homeric underworld narrative as a witness (martyrei) to his argument that tyrants, kings, and dynasts commit the greatest injustices, treating poetic myth as corroborating testimony rather than as a separate narrative mode.
textual_basis: Socrates introduces the Homeric reference with 'Homer also bears witness to these things' at 525d-e, which casts the epic depictions of Hades as evidentiary support for a moral claim about rulers.
limits: The observation notes the framing device of 'witness' but does not determine whether Socrates is using Homer seriously, ironically, or as a rhetorical concession to his interlocutors. It also does not assess the broader Gorgias treatment of myth.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0396

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates names three mythical figures — Tantalus, Sisyphus, and Tityus — as examples of eternal punishment in Hades, and contrasts them with Thersites, a private individual, who is not depicted as suffering great punishments.
textual_basis: At 525e Socrates lists Tantalus, Sisyphus, and Tityus as those whom Homer made to suffer for all time in Hades, and then notes that Thersites is not shown undergoing great punishments, marking a contrast between public figures (tyrants/kings) and a private wrongdoer (idiotes).
limits: The observation records only the named cast and the public/private contrast within the passage. It does not analyze the mythological background of each figure or the fuller argument about power and injustice in the Gorgias.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0397
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 522c-522d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 522c-522d
  start_marker: 522c
  end_marker: 522d
  start_char: 152916
  end_char: 153738
  text_sha256: 22554d22fc8c732804223f3c7a2809d6f2bc115a0deab2be8d899297843ccc25
greek_terms:
  - βοηθεῖν
  - βεβοηθηκὼς
  - βοηθείας
  - κρατίστη
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates recalls a prior agreement with Callicles about what constitutes the strongest self-defense (kratiste boetheia): having done and said nothing unjust toward human beings or gods. He then frames the threat of being refuted on this point—inability to provide this specific defense—as the only outcome he would be ashamed of or distressed over."
textual_basis: Socrates explicitly invokes what Callicles has agreed many times (ho su pollakis homologesas, 522c). The definition of the strongest help (aute gar tes boetheias...kratiste, 522c-d) is recalled from their earlier shared admissions. Socrates says he would feel shame only if convicted of being unable to provide this defense (522d).
limits: The passage reports the content of prior agreements without adjudicating whether those agreements were genuine or coerced. The observation does not assess whether the prior agreement argument succeeds against Callicles.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0398
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 522c-522d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 522c-522d
  start_marker: 522c
  end_marker: 522d
  start_char: 152916
  end_char: 153738
  text_sha256: 22554d22fc8c732804223f3c7a2809d6f2bc115a0deab2be8d899297843ccc25
greek_terms:
  - κολακικῆς ῥητορικῆς
  - ἐνδείᾳ
  - ῥᾳδίως
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates contrasts dying through lack of flattering rhetoric (kolakikes rhetorikes endeia) with dying through failure of the just self-defense he has defined. He claims he would bear the former easily, implying that flattering rhetoric is both not worth possessing and that his own forthcoming death may be attributed to lacking it.
textual_basis: Socrates says he would easily (rhaidios) be seen bearing his death if it came through lack of kolakikes rhetorikes (522d). The contrast with the shameworthy death by injustice frames the flattering-rhetoric-lacking death as untroubled.
limits: This observation registers the ironic contrast between two types of death and Socratic self-positioning. It does not claim to decode the full force of the irony or assess whether the claim about flattering rhetoric is sincere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0399
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 522c-522d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 522c-522d
  start_marker: 522c
  end_marker: 522d
  start_char: 152916
  end_char: 153738
  text_sha256: 22554d22fc8c732804223f3c7a2809d6f2bc115a0deab2be8d899297843ccc25
greek_terms:
  - ἀδύνατος
  - ἑαυτῷ βοηθεῖν
  - βοήθειαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Callicles poses a challenge to Socrates' prior self-characterization as helpless in the city by asking whether a man so disposed in a city, and unable to help himself (adunatos on heauto boethein, 522c), is well off. Socrates replies by revising the definition of self-help: true self-help (boetheia) is having done no injustice toward humans or gods (522c-d), not the ability to defend oneself in court by any means."
textual_basis: Callicles' question at 522c uses boethein in the conventional political sense of defending oneself. Socrates at 522c-d redefines the term by recalling the agreed-upon kratiste boetheia as freedom from unjust word and deed toward humans and gods. The move revises the definition away from political self-preservation toward moral-psychological integrity.
limits: The observation tracks the redefinition move, not whether Socrates' revised definition is compelling or whether Callicles accepts it. It does not assess whether the prior agreement being invoked was fairly obtained.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0400
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 524a-524b

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  end_char: 157037
  text_sha256: 776c793d72c74765ac2651d08fa70c2b4ef2677032be649b7b59d0c031aa084f
greek_terms:
  - πρεσβεῖα
  - ἐπιδιακρίνειν
  - λειμῶνι
  - τριόδῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates names three judges of the dead — Rhadamanthys for Asia, Aeacus for Europe — with Minos granted the prerogative of final adjudication if the other two are at a loss, to ensure the most just judgment concerning the path of humans.
textual_basis: At 524a, Socrates specifies the two primary judges by geographical jurisdiction and gives Minos a tie-breaking role, continuing the mythic account of post-mortem judgment with the meadow and crossroads topography.
limits: This observation records the mythic judgment structure as narrated; it does not assess whether Socrates endorses it literally.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἀληθῆ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates declares that he has heard this mythic account and believes it to be true.
textual_basis: At 524a-524b, Socrates transitions from reporting the myth to stating his own epistemic stance, addressing Callicles and asserting that what he has heard he believes to be true.
limits: Records Socrates' stated belief; does not determine whether this is a genuine profession or a protreptic stance.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0402

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  end_char: 157037
  text_sha256: 776c793d72c74765ac2651d08fa70c2b4ef2677032be649b7b59d0c031aa084f
greek_terms:
  - διάλυσις
  - ψυχῆς
  - σώματος
  - ἕξιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates proposes a definition of death as nothing other than the dissolution of two things, soul and body, from each other; after dissolution, each retains the condition it had during life.
textual_basis: At 524b, Socrates states that death is the dissolution of soul and body from each other, and that afterward each retains its own hexis just as when the person was alive, with the body displaying its nature, treatments, and affections.
limits: Records the definition as stated by Socrates within the mythic context; does not evaluate its metaphysical adequacy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0403
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 521d-521e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 521d-521e
  start_marker: 521d
  end_marker: 521e
  start_char: 151141
  end_char: 152042
  text_sha256: fa4f48c31928b57edb5740ba371f7a8ac58f6f215c307dfe9b5cc42db687cfce
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates tells Callicles he expects to be brought to court by a wicked person (poneros), states it would not be strange if he died, and offers to explain why he expects this outcome.
textual_basis: At 521d Socrates says a wicked person will be his prosecutor (poneros tis m' estai ho eisagon), that no decent person would prosecute an innocent man, and that it would not be strange if he died (ouden ge atopon ei apothanoimi). He then asks Callicles if he wants to hear why he expects these things.
limits: This observation records the dramatic prediction of prosecution and possible death. It does not assess whether the prediction is accurate, ironic, or a literary construct inserted after the fact.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0404
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 521d-521e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 521d-521e
  start_marker: 521d
  end_marker: 521e
  start_char: 151141
  end_char: 152042
  text_sha256: fa4f48c31928b57edb5740ba371f7a8ac58f6f215c307dfe9b5cc42db687cfce
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates deploys an analogy in which a doctor (true craftsman) is judged before an audience of children with a pastry-chef as prosecutor, the doctor having harmed the children by cutting and burning them for their benefit.
textual_basis: At 521d-521e Socrates says he will be judged like a doctor before children with a pastry-chef as prosecutor (hos en paidiois iatros an krinoito kategorountos opsopoiou). He begins the analogy proper at 521e by asking what such a man would say in his defense if accused of corrupting the youngest by cutting and burning them (tous neotatous hymon diaphtheirei temnon te kai kaon).
limits: This record identifies the analogy structure (craftsman vs. pleasure-purveyor, ignorant audience). It does not interpret the analogy as a claim about Socrates' own trial outcome, nor does it assess whether the analogy is a fair representation of rhetoric's role.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0405
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 521d-521e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 521d-521e
  start_marker: 521d

  text_sha256: fa4f48c31928b57edb5740ba371f7a8ac58f6f215c307dfe9b5cc42db687cfce
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explicitly signals that the argument he is now making to Callicles is the same one he previously made to Polus, reintroducing a prior argumentative move into the current exchange.
textual_basis: At 521e Socrates states "the same argument comes to me which I was making to Polus" (ho autos de moi hekei logos honper pros Polon elegon) before launching into the doctor-pastry chef analogy. This creates an explicit argumentative link between the Callicles exchange and the earlier Polus exchange.
limits: This observation records the explicit cross-reference to an earlier argumentative stage. It does not evaluate whether the argument is more effective here than with Polus, nor does it track the full history of the doctor-pastry chef analogy across the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0406
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 518e-519a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 518e-519a
  start_marker: 518e
  end_marker: 519a
  start_char: 145593
  end_char: 146265
  text_sha256: bf53261ce173a11aebc92894ca1353df110dc83eb76827a97c4a414a18bb6e8f
greek_terms:
  - οἰδεῖ
  - ὕπουλός
english_gloss: The city is swollen and festering underneath.
observation: Socrates describes the city as swollen and festering underneath due to the older statesmen, using medical-diagnostic vocabulary. This extends the earlier cookery-vs-medicine craft analogy into a civic bodily pathology claim.
textual_basis: At 518e-519a Socrates says that people claim the old statesmen made the city great, but fail to perceive that it is swollen and festering because of those men of old. The terms are drawn from Hippocratic diagnostic vocabulary rather than from the cookery/bakery register used just before.
limits: The passage does not develop a systematic city-body isomorphism; the medical language is adjectival and diagnostic rather than an extended analogy with parts mapped. The observation is limited to the diagnostic vocabulary applied to the polis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0407
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 518e-519a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 518e-519a
  start_marker: 518e
  end_marker: 519a
  start_char: 145593
  end_char: 146265
  text_sha256: bf53261ce173a11aebc92894ca1353df110dc83eb76827a97c4a414a18bb6e8f
greek_terms:
  - Θεμιστοκλέα
  - Κίμωνα
  - Περικλέα
  - Ἀλκιβιάδου
english_gloss: Themistocles, Cimon, Pericles, Alcibiades.
observation: "Socrates names four Athenian figures together in a single critical frame: Themistocles, Cimon, and Pericles as past statesmen blamed for the city's underlying sickness, and Alcibiades (his own companion) as someone the people may also seize upon when the accumulated troubles come due."
textual_basis: At 519a Socrates says that when the crisis of the sickness arrives, the people will blame the present advisors but praise Themistocles, Cimon, and Pericles as the causes of the evils, and adds that they may also seize upon Callicles and Alcibiades when they lose even the old possessions on top of the new.
limits: The observation records the grouping and the blame framework; it does not assess the historical accuracy of the claim nor whether Socrates is consistent with other dialogues on these figures.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0408
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 518e-519a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 518e-519a
  start_marker: 518e
  end_marker: 519a
  start_char: 145593
  end_char: 146265
  text_sha256: bf53261ce173a11aebc92894ca1353df110dc83eb76827a97c4a414a18bb6e8f
greek_terms:
  - ἄνευ γὰρ σωφροσύνης καὶ δικαιοσύνης
  - λιμένων
  - νεωρίων
  - τειχῶν
  - φόρων
  - φλυαριῶν
english_gloss: Without moderation and justice, harbors, dockyards, walls, tribute, and such nonsense.
observation: Socrates lists the material-infrastructural achievements of the old statesmen (harbors, dockyards, walls, tribute revenues) and dismisses them as nonsense when accomplished without moderation and justice. The passage contrasts material civic greatness with the absence of the virtues that earlier in the dialogue have been the target of definition.
textual_basis: At 519a Socrates says the old statesmen have filled the city with harbors, dockyards, walls, tribute, and such trifles without moderation and justice.
limits: This does not constitute a definition of moderation or justice; it is a negative criterion applied to civic achievement. The observation does not address whether the earlier definitional attempts in the dialogue have converged on these terms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0409
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 527d-527e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 527d-527e
  start_marker: 527d
  end_marker: 527e
  start_char: 163517
  end_char: 164277
  text_sha256: 139f2c55d03a48da86fed15f6d8744ab75dabbcd17348875160782646e5df7e9
greek_terms:
  - δικαιοσύνην
  - ἀρετήν
  - ἀσκοῦντας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates closes the Gorgias with a final ethical exhortation: he calls on Callicles to practice justice and the rest of virtue both in living and in dying, and to follow the argument (logos) that has appeared as a guide indicating this way of life is best. He also urges calling on others to follow this path rather than the one Callicles has been urging."
textual_basis: At 527d-527e Socrates says 'let us use as a guide the argument that has now appeared, which indicates to us that this way of life is best, practicing justice and the rest of virtue both in living and dying. Let us follow this, and call on others also.' He then contrasts this path with the one Callicles trusts, declaring that latter path 'worth nothing, Callicles.'
limits: This observation records the exhortation form and content at the dialogue's close. It does not assess whether the exhortation is sincerely protreptic or functions as ironic commentary on Callicles' position.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0410
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 527d-527e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 527d-527e
  start_marker: 527d
  end_marker: 527e
  start_char: 163517
  end_char: 164277
  text_sha256: 139f2c55d03a48da86fed15f6d8744ab75dabbcd17348875160782646e5df7e9
greek_terms:
  - ἀπαιδευσίας
  - νεανιεύεσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates diagnoses both himself and Callicles as having reached such a degree of lack of education (apaideusia) that they hold no stable opinions about the greatest matters, and declares them not yet ready to engage in politics. The self-inclusive plural includes Socrates in the condition of ignorance and instability of opinion.
textual_basis: "At 527d-527e Socrates states: 'it is shameful, being in the state we now appear to be in, then to swagger as if we were something, when we never hold the same opinions about the same things, and these about the greatest matters — to such a degree of lack of education have we come.' He then proposes shared practice in virtue before any political engagement."
limits: This observation notes the self-inclusive diagnosis of apaideusia as a form of Socratic knowledge disavowal. It does not determine whether the self-diagnosis is genuine or a tactical move to bring Callicles into a shared project of self-improvement.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0411
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 526a-526b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 526a-526b
  start_marker: 526a
  end_marker: 526b
  start_char: 160160
  end_char: 161029
  text_sha256: 607678a2eb6430cd96a437b2226e1fd6305e9dd12faa02946be283c07e00cb9d
greek_terms:
  - ὀλίγοι δὲ γίγνονται οἱ τοιοῦτοι
  - καλοὶ κἀγαθοὶ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates claims that few men with great power to commit injustice live justly, calling them kaloi kagathoi with respect to the virtue of handling justly what is entrusted to them. He names Aristeides son of Lysimachos as a prominent example (526a-526b).
textual_basis: Socrates states at 526a that it is difficult and praiseworthy for someone with great license to do injustice to live justly, that such men are few, and that Aristeides son of Lysimachos is one very notable example among the Greeks (526b).
limits: This does not establish that Aristeides was in fact just; it reports Socrates' characterization within the eschatological myth context.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0412
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 526e-527a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 526e-527a
  start_marker: 526e
  end_marker: 527a
  start_char: 161801
  end_char: 162614
  text_sha256: dc3f9e2e685b42f3290030a6ebd8f632cf93b13288044dab006f7d414680d9c6
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates explicitly contrasts the likely status of his eschatological narrative with the possibility of finding a better and truer account. He acknowledges that Callicles may consider the story an old wife's tale (mythos...hosper graos) and treats the myth/logos boundary as a stated distinction.
textual_basis: |
  At 527a Socrates says: "Perhaps these things seem to you to be told as a myth like an old wife's tale (mythos...hosper graos) and you despise them, and it would be no wonder to despise these things, if by seeking we could find something better and truer (beltio kai alethestera) than them."
limits: |
  This observation does not assess whether the myth is presented as epistemically inferior to logos. It only records the explicit demarcation language.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0413
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 526e-527a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 526e-527a
  start_marker: 526e
  end_marker: 527a
  start_char: 161801
  end_char: 162614
  text_sha256: dc3f9e2e685b42f3290030a6ebd8f632cf93b13288044dab006f7d414680d9c6
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates frames the choice of life and the contest (ton bion kai ton agona touton) as against all contests in this place (anti panton ton enthade agonon), presenting the philosophical life as the only contest worth entering and reversing the conventional valuation of worldly competition.
textual_basis: |
  At 526e Socrates says: "I call upon all other men, as far as I can, and in particular I call upon you in return to this life and this contest (ton bion kai ton agona touton), which I say is worth all the contests here (anti panton ton enthade agonon)."
limits: |
  This observation notes the inversion rhetoric but does not evaluate whether Socrates proves the claim. It only records the structure of the ethical reversal.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0414
source_work: Gorgias

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates issues a direct exhortation (parakalo...antiparakalo) to Callicles and all others to adopt the philosophical life as the single contest worth entering, framing the closing of his speech as a protreptic appeal rather than a formal argumentative conclusion.
textual_basis: |
  At 526e Socrates says: "I call upon (parakalo) all other men, as far as I can, and in particular I call upon you in return (antiparakalo) to this life and this contest (ton bion kai ton agona touton)."
limits: |
  This observation records the hortatory speech-act at the close of Socrates' myth but does not assess whether the exhortation is persuasive or follows from the preceding argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0415
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 522e-523a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - μῦθον
  - λόγον
english_gloss: myth, account
observation: |
  At the beginning of the final myth, Socrates explicitly frames the ensuing narrative with a myth/logos distinction: Callicles will regard it as mythos, Socrates as logos (522e-523a).
textual_basis: |
  Socrates says "you will consider it a mythos, as I think, but I a logos; for I will tell you what I am about to say as being true" (522e-523a).
limits: |
  This observation records only the explicit boundary marker, not what the narrative proves or whether the myth/logos contrast resolves.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0416

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates signals the transition to myth with an appeal to the listener ("hear then, they say, a very fine logos") and then immediately cites Homeric divine partition as the narrative frame (523a).
textual_basis: |
  Socrates says "hear then … a very fine logos" and then quotes Homer on Zeus, Poseidon, and Plouton dividing rule (523a).
limits: |
  Only the transition marker is noted here; the observation does not assess the truth of the myth or its theological content.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0417
source_work: Gorgias

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

greek_terms:
  - Ἅιδου
english_gloss: Hades
observation: |
  Socrates invokes Hades as the destination of the soul burdened with many injustices and calls arriving there the ultimate evil, then introduces a narrative of divine judgement set in the time of Kronos (522e-523a).
textual_basis: |
  At 522e the soul "laden with many injustices" arrives in Hades, framed as the worst of evils; at 523a the narrative pivots to a law concerning humans "in the time of Kronos, and always and now still among the gods."
limits: |
  The record notes the afterlife setting and the temporal frame of the myth; it does not evaluate the myth's content or whether Plato endorses it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0418

greek_terms:
  - ἀσύμφωνον
  - ἐμαυτῷ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states he would rather have most people disagree with him and say the opposite than be one person who is dissonant with himself and says contradictory things.
textual_basis: At 482c Socrates says he prefers being opposed by the many to being out of tune with himself. The contrast between external disagreement and internal consistency is explicit in the syntax.
limits: This observation records Socrates' stated methodological preference. It does not establish that he actually achieves self-consistency in the dialogue, nor does it evaluate whether his preference is sound.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0419
source_work: Gorgias

  text_sha256: 884ef3f1a8792173d4e1ae2e825917a9a319e931d22307d5f77c5c03a9fd09ee
greek_terms:
  - νεανιεύεσθαι
  - δημηγόρος
  - δημηγορεῖς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles characterizes Socrates' speech as youthful swaggering and says Socrates is speaking like a true popular orator, then repeats the verb for delivering a set-piece address to assert Socrates is performing demagoguery rather than conducting dialectic.
textual_basis: At 482c, Callicles opens his response by accusing Socrates of swaggering in his speeches as if he were truly a popular speaker, and immediately repeats the charge that Socrates is delivering a set-piece. The accusation inverts the dialogue's framing by treating Socratic elenchus as a species of rhetoric rather than its opponent.
limits: This records Callicles' characterization, not an objective assessment of Socrates' speech. It does not establish that Socrates is actually a demagogue or that the characterization is accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - πάθος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Callicles observes that Polus has suffered the same experience that Polus himself had accused Gorgias of suffering at Socrates' hands — being led by shame to agree to something he did not believe.
textual_basis: At 482c, Callicles states that the same thing happened to Polus that Polus accused Gorgias of suffering. This is an in-text observation that the pattern of shame-induced concession has recurred across two interlocutors.
limits: This records Callicles' diagnosis of the elenctic pattern. It does not evaluate whether Gorgias and Polus were actually victims of shame rather than genuine refutation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates reports a prior elenctic moment: Gorgias was compelled through his own admission to contradict himself (482d)."
textual_basis: Socrates narrates that Gorgias agreed to teach something out of shame before the audience, and through this concession was forced to say things contrary to himself. The language of compulsion and self-contradiction marks an elenctic result.
limits: This is Socrates' retrospective narration of an earlier exchange, not a direct observation of Gorgias' self-contradiction happening in real time. Does not assess whether Socrates' account is accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0422
source_work: Gorgias

greek_terms:
  - καταγελᾶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states that he laughed at Callicles when Callicles previously enjoyed Polus suffering the same contradiction that Gorgias suffered, and adds that he thinks he laughed rightly (482d).
textual_basis: The verb of laughter and the parenthetical remark that Socrates thinks this was correct mark an explicit mention of laughter directed at an interlocutor. Socrates frames his own laughter as justified, which is a kind of ironic self-report.
limits: Does not determine whether the laughter was mocking, pedagogical, or ironic in the sense of complex dissimulation. Only records that Socrates narrates laughing at Callicles and claims it was correct.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0423

english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates attributes Gorgias' initial concession to shame driven by the custom of men: Gorgias would have been indignant if someone refused to claim he could teach (482d)."
textual_basis: The phrase meaning custom of men links Gorgias' agreement to teach to a social norm. Socrates explicitly says Gorgias was ashamed and that this shame triggered the chain of concessions leading to self-contradiction.
limits: Does not assess whether Socrates' analysis of Gorgias' motive is correct. Only records that audience-driven shame is offered as the cause of a dialectical concession.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0424

greek_terms:
  - παρρησιάζεσθαι
  - μὴ αἰσχύνεσθαι
english_gloss: to speak frankly and not to be ashamed
observation: At 487d, Socrates attributes to Callicles the quality of frank speech and lack of shame, stating that Callicles both claims this himself and that his preceding speech confirms it.
textual_basis: Socrates says that Callicles is the sort to speak frankly and not feel shame, and that his preceding speech agrees with this self-characterization. The explicit naming of frank speech marks a procedural condition for the ensuing exchange.
limits: This observation records the invocation of frank speech as a dialogue feature. It does not assess whether the interlocutor actually practices frank speech.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0425

greek_terms:
  - εὔνους
english_gloss: well-disposed, proof/evidence
observation: At 487d, Socrates infers Callicles' goodwill from the fact that Callicles advises him with the same counsel he gives to his dearest companions, calling this sufficient proof.
textual_basis: Socrates states that because he hears Callicles giving him the same advice he gives his closest companions, this is sufficient proof that Callicles is genuinely well-disposed toward him. The congruence of advice serves as evidential ground for asserting goodwill.
limits: This records an explicit inference from observed conduct to a dispositional claim about the interlocutor. It does not assess whether the inference is valid or whether the goodwill is genuine.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 491b Callicles describes the superior as not only wise (phronimoi) in civic affairs but also courageous (andreioi), capable of executing what they intend, and not flagging through softness of soul.
limits: Does not specify whether this is a revision of a prior definition or an initial enumeration; earlier passages would need to be checked.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 491b Socrates notes that Callicles accuses him of always saying the same things, but Socrates counters that Callicles never says the same things about the same subjects.
limits: The passage records the charge but not a demonstration; the excerpt of 491b ends before Socrates completes the contrast.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0428
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 491c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 491c
  start_marker: 491c
  end_marker: 491c
  start_char: 89393
  end_char: 89766
  text_sha256: fca9d64c43f923cf6f4f5620e7b87c94c76ff9b926d57ca0e7f61764aee6da60
greek_terms:
  - βελτίους
  - κρείττους
  - φρονιμωτέρους
  - ἀνδρειότεροί
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates recapitulates Callicles' shifting definition of the better and the stronger: first the stronger, then the wiser, and now yet another attempt is introduced — the more courageous. Socrates presses Callicles to state definitively who the better and stronger are and with respect to what.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates lists the succession: "you were defining the better and stronger as the stronger, then again as the wiser, and now you have come with yet another — the more courageous are said by you to be the stronger and better" (491c). He then demands: "speak and be done with it — who in the world are you calling better and stronger, and with respect to what?"
limits: |
  This observation notes the succession of definition candidates within Socrates' recapitulation at 491c. It does not assess whether Callicles actually endorsed each version or whether Socrates is distorting Callicles' position.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0429
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 487e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  end_char: 82413
  text_sha256: 2e4db0bc47bff6373cc58a6605cdef19078af39097f992924bcdfe654a83aaa9
greek_terms:
  - βεβασανισμένον
  - βάσανον
  - ὁμολογία
english_gloss: tested, touchstone/test, agreement
observation: Callicles presents dialectical agreement as a sufficient test of truth, using the vocabulary of touchstone assaying to describe the testing of arguments.
textual_basis: At 487e, Callicles claims that if Socrates agrees with him in argument, the point will have been sufficiently tested and will not need referral to another touchstone. He concludes their mutual agreement will possess the end of truth, mapping the craft of assaying precious metals onto dialectical consensus.
limits: This observation notes the presence of the craft analogy; it does not assess whether Callicles' confidence in agreement as truth-test is justified, nor does it claim that the analogy is sustained throughout the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0430

greek_terms:
  - φίλος
  - σοφίας ἐνδείᾳ
  - αἰσχύνης περιουσίᾳ
english_gloss: friend, lack of wisdom, excess of shame
observation: Callicles cites his friendship with Socrates as a guarantee that he will not concede from lack of wisdom or excess of shame, nor deceive Socrates in argument.
textual_basis: At 487e, Callicles asserts Socrates would never concede from lack of wisdom or excess of shame, and that Callicles would not deceive him in argument, because Socrates is his friend, as Socrates himself says. The personal relationship is offered as an epistemic warrant against insincere concession.
limits: This observation registers the appeal to friendship as an argumentative guarantee within the dialogue. It does not evaluate whether Callicles' self-characterization is accurate or whether Plato endorses this move.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates frames the upcoming discussion as an inquiry into what kind of man one should be, explicitly valorizing it as the most beautiful inquiry of all.
textual_basis: At 487e, Socrates responds by calling the inquiry the most beautiful of all and defines its topic as what kind of man one should be and what pursuits he should follow. This explicit framing marks the transition to a new topic and elevates its importance within the dialogue.
limits: This observation notes the framing language at this juncture. It does not claim this is the only or final framing of the dialogue's question, nor does it assess the outcome of the inquiry.

```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0432
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 491d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  end_char: 90204
  text_sha256: 386defbf6e2e52cbae45b6b43b4a9ff7d92e11674edaf12cd9093ed0635cb754
greek_terms:
  - σώφρονα
  - ἐγκρατῆ
  - αὐτὸν ἑαυτοῦ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates defines ruling oneself in terms the many would recognize: being sōphrōn and enkratēs over oneself with respect to pleasures and desires. He explicitly introduces this with 'not anything subtle but just as the many' (ouden poikilon all' hōsper hoi polloi)."
textual_basis: "Socrates says at 491d: ouden poikilon all' hōsper hoi polloi, sōphrona onta kai enkratē auton heautou, tōn hēdonōn kai epithymiōn. The appeal to popular understanding (hoi polloi) as the basis for a definition is explicit."
limits: This observation records only the appeal to popular usage as a definitional move. It does not evaluate whether the definition is Socratic or ironic, nor does it assess the adequacy of the definition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0433
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 491d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 491d
  start_marker: 491d
  end_marker: 491d
  start_char: 89766
  end_char: 90204
  text_sha256: 386defbf6e2e52cbae45b6b43b4a9ff7d92e11674edaf12cd9093ed0635cb754
greek_terms:
  - πῶς λέγεις;
  - πῶς ἑαυτοῦ ἄρχοντα λέγεις;
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Callicles demands clarification twice in quick succession at 491d: first with 'How do you mean?' (pōs legeis) when Socrates asks whether the stronger rule themselves, then with 'How do you mean ruling oneself?' (pōs heautou archonta legeis) when Socrates introduces self-rule."
textual_basis: At 491d, Callicles interrupts with pōs legeis after Socrates first mention of self-rule, then again with pōs heautou archonta legeis after Socrates elaborates. The double clarification request marks a moment where the argument's direction is contested at the level of basic terms.
limits: This records only the pattern of clarification demands. It does not assess whether Callicles genuinely misunderstands or is being obstructive, nor does it evaluate whether the clarifications are satisfied.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0434
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 491d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 491d
  start_marker: 491d
  end_marker: 491d
  start_char: 89766
  end_char: 90204
  text_sha256: 386defbf6e2e52cbae45b6b43b4a9ff7d92e11674edaf12cd9093ed0635cb754
greek_terms:
  - ἄρχοντας
  - ἀρχομένων
  - αὐτὸν ἑαυτοῦ ἄρχοντα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates takes Callicles' claim that rulers deserve more than the ruled (tous archontas tōn archomenōn) and reframes the concept of ruling toward self-rule (auton heautou archonta). The move shifts the domain from political rule over others to ethical rule over oneself.
textual_basis: "At 491d, Callicles states that the just thing is for rulers to have more than the ruled (tous archontas tōn archomenōn). Socrates immediately inserts a new distinction: what about ruling themselves? He then pivots to 'each individual ruling himself' (hena hekaston legō auton heautou archonta), redirecting the verb archein from political to ethical domain."
limits: This records the reframing move as a turn-structure phenomenon. It does not assess whether the reframing is legitimate or whether Callicles' original claim is thereby refuted.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0435
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 491e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 491e
  start_marker: 491e
  end_marker: 491e
  start_char: 90204
  end_char: 90632
  text_sha256: 005a716a2ef0e3452345df7e3f5606c607929bd4cb20ac056072e45889b01505
greek_terms:
  - ὡς ἡδὺς εἶ
  - τοὺς ἠλιθίους λέγεις τοὺς σώφρονας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 491e, after Socrates speaks of ruling the things within oneself, Callicles responds sarcastically: he tells Socrates he is pleasant and that Socrates is calling the temperate people imbeciles. Callicles does not believe Socrates is pleasant or that temperate people are imbeciles; this is ironic misrepresentation."
textual_basis: Callicles' next speech rejects the Socratic position outright, and his follow-up question at 491e — how can a man be happy while being a slave to anyone? — treats the Socratic self-rule as slavery, confirming he does not genuinely find Socrates' position pleasant or believe Socrates means what he claims about temperate people.
limits: This observation identifies a specific ironic address; it does not claim that Callicles' entire speech is ironic or that he is insincere about his positive doctrine.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0436
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 491e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt

  text_sha256: 005a716a2ef0e3452345df7e3f5606c607929bd4cb20ac056072e45889b01505
greek_terms:
  - παρρησιαζόμενος
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 491e, Callicles prefaces his doctrine of the natural good with an explicit parrhesia declaration: he says he speaks with frankness. This frames his claim about the right life as bold, unfiltered truth-telling."
textual_basis: "Callicles' own words at 491e: he states that what he now says is by nature noble and just, spoken with frankness — he explicitly marks his speech act as one of frank speech."
limits: This observation notes a speech-act framing device in the text. It does not evaluate whether Callicles' claim is in fact bold or truthful, nor does it assess the accuracy of the parrhesia self-description.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0437
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 491e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 491e
  start_marker: 491e
  end_marker: 491e
  start_char: 90204
  end_char: 90632
  text_sha256: 005a716a2ef0e3452345df7e3f5606c607929bd4cb20ac056072e45889b01505
greek_terms:
  - κατὰ φύσιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 491e, Callicles invokes nature as the normative standard for nobility and justice. He opposes the standard of nature to convention, since his doctrine of unrestrained appetite directly contradicts conventional Greek morality of temperance.
textual_basis: Callicles' own words at 491e present his doctrine — that the right life maximizes and does not restrain appetites — as what is by nature noble and just. He has just rejected Socrates' call for self-rule, which aligns with conventional temperance, positioning nature against convention.
limits: This observation records the appeal to nature as a normative standard. It does not analyze the substance of Callicles' nature-convention theory or evaluate whether his use of the distinction is consistent.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0438
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 447d-448c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 447d-448c
  start_marker: 447d
  end_marker: 448c
  start_char: 1163
  end_char: 2716
  text_sha256: f641bbb1bfada45e1c7cce5b27d5d69c0430e41441bbf6dc547ea4f944aeac3e
greek_terms:
  - σκυτοτόμος
  - ἐπαγγέλλῃ ἀποκρίνεσθαι
english_gloss: Opening scene establishes the dialogue's occasion through a question about Gorgias's identity and his claim to answer any question.
observation: At 447d-448a Socrates asks who Gorgias is, using a shoemaker analogy; Chaerephon then relays Callicles' report that Gorgias professes to answer any question, and Gorgias confirms at 448a that no one has asked him anything new in many years. This establishes the dialogue's premise as a test of Gorgias's open-question claim.
textual_basis: Socrates at 447d introduces the identification question with a craft analogy; Chaerephon at 447d-448a asks whether Callicles' report about Gorgias's profession is true; Gorgias at 448a confirms and adds that no one has asked him anything new for many years, setting up the expectation that Socrates will ask something new.
limits: The passage establishes the occasion but does not yet specify what Gorgias's art is or what Socrates will ask.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0439
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 447d-448c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 447d-448c
  start_marker: 447d
  end_marker: 448c
  start_char: 1163
  end_char: 2716
  text_sha256: f641bbb1bfada45e1c7cce5b27d5d69c0430e41441bbf6dc547ea4f944aeac3e
greek_terms:
  - τέχνη
  - ἐπιστήμων
  - ζωγράφον
  - ἰατρὸν
english_gloss: Socrates and Chaerephon use a series of craft analogies (shoemaker, physician, painter) to frame the question of what art Gorgias practices.
observation: At 447d Socrates introduces the pattern that a practitioner is identified by naming his craft (shoemaker), and at 448b-448c Chaerephon extends the same pattern with Herodicus (medicine) and Aristophon son of Aglaiophon (painting) to set up the unanswered question of what craft Gorgias is expert in. Polus at 448c responds with a general statement about arts and experience rather than naming a single craft, deflecting the analogy.
textual_basis: "The analogy chain runs from 447d through 448c: shoemaker at 447d, physician via Herodicus at 448b, painter via Aristophon at 448c, culminating in Chaerephon's direct question at 448c about what art Gorgias practices. Polus's reply at 448c shifts from naming a specific craft to praising Gorgias's art as the finest without identifying it."
limits: The analogy sets up the definitional question but does not yet produce an answer; Polus's response avoids the specific craft identification the analogy demands.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0440
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 447d-448c

greek_terms:
  - Γοργίας
  - Χαιρεφῶν
  - Καλλικλῆς
  - Ἡρόδικος
  - Ἀριστοφῶν
  - Ἀγλαοφῶντος
english_gloss: The opening segment names six historical persons plus Socrates, establishing the dialogue's cast and their relationships.
observation: The opening segment names Socrates, Chaerephon, Callicles, Gorgias, Polus, Herodicus, and Aristophon son of Aglaiophon, identifying speakers by name and role (Gorgias as the visiting sophist, Polus as his student or associate, Herodicus as Gorgias's brother, Aristophon as a painter). Callicles is referenced at 447d but does not yet speak in this segment.
textual_basis: Named speakers at 447d-448c include Socrates, Chaerephon, Gorgias, and Polus. Callicles is named at 447d as the source of the report about Gorgias's claim. Herodicus is named at 448b as Gorgias's brother and a medical practitioner. Aristophon son of Aglaiophon is named at 448b as associated with painting.
limits: The passage names the cast but does not provide biographical detail beyond familial and craft associations. Callicles's speaking role begins later.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0441
source_work: Gorgias
stephanus_span: 449d-451e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/gorgias.txt
  stephanus_span: 449d-451e
  start_marker: 449d
  end_marker: 451e
  start_char: 4716
  end_char: 9833
  text_sha256: 72132e2ac4370fb485adcd0430eea39fe122a5b78307cbab34fa432e3fe7df08
greek_terms:
  - ῥητορική
  - τέχνη
  - λόγοι
english_gloss: Socrates uses a battery of crafts as analogues to isolate rhetoric's subject matter.
observation: Socrates systematically compares rhetoric to multiple crafts — weaving, music, medicine, gymnastics, painting, sculpture, arithmetic, calculation, geometry, and astronomy — mapping each craft's relation to its subject matter onto the question of what rhetoric is about. The crafts serve as source domains whose structural relation to their objects is used to interrogate rhetoric's target domain.
textual_basis: At 449d Socrates asks what rhetoric is about, comparing it to weaving and music. At 450a-450b he extends the comparison to medicine and gymnastics, noting each craft is about its own logoi. At 450c-450e he distinguishes crafts that work mostly through manual action from those that work through speech, citing painting, sculpture, arithmetic, calculation, and geometry. At 451b-451c he models the desired answer by giving subject-specifications for arithmetic, calculation, and astronomy, then asks Gorgias to do the same for rhetoric.
limits: The passage shows a sustained craft-analogy procedure but does not establish whether the analogy ultimately succeeds or fails in defining rhetoric; the analogy is still in progress at 451e.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_gorgias_0442

  start_char: 4716
  end_char: 9833
  text_sha256: 72132e2ac4370fb485adcd0430eea39fe122a5b78307cbab34fa432e3fe7df08
greek_terms:
  - λόγοι
  - ῥητορική
  - πρᾶξις
  - κύρωσις
english_gloss: Gorgias defines rhetoric as the craft about words; Socrates refutes by showing other crafts are also about words; Gorgias revises; Socrates refutes again.
observation: "Gorgias first defines rhetoric as the craft concerned with words (449e). Socrates objects that medicine and gymnastics are also concerned with words about their respective subjects (450a-450b). Gorgias revises: rhetoric differs because its entire practice and authority is accomplished through words, not manual work (450b-450c). Socrates objects again that arithmetic, calculation, and geometry also operate entirely through words (450d-450e), then presses for rhetoric's specific subject matter. Gorgias answers that it concerns the greatest and best of human affairs (451d), which Socrates calls disputed and unclear (451e)."
textual_basis: The initial definition at 449e is "about words." The first objection at 450a-450b shows medicine and gymnastics are also about words. Gorgias revises at 450b-450c to distinguish rhetoric by its lack of manual work. The second objection at 450d-450e cites arithmetic and geometry as also word-based. Socrates renews the question at 451d, and Gorgias's answer at 451d-e is flagged as ambiguous.
limits: The observation tracks the definition-revision sequence but does not assess whether Socrates' objections are fair or whether Gorgias's final answer is defensible.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 449d Socrates says he admires Gorgias's answers because he answers as briefly as possible, and Gorgias confirms he thinks he does this reasonably well. Socrates then says "answer me thus" and proceeds with short questions and brief responses through 450e. At 451d Socrates again instructs Gorgias to complete the answer in the same manner.
limits: The observation notes the procedure control but does not assess whether Gorgias fully complies or whether the format shifts later in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - δίκαια
  - ἄδικα
english_gloss: Socrates presses whether a rhetoric student must already know justice and injustice; Gorgias concedes he will teach it.
observation: At 459e-460a Socrates forces Gorgias to concede that a student of rhetoric must know the just and the unjust, either before arriving or by learning it from Gorgias himself. Gorgias agrees that even if the student does not already know these things, he will learn them from him.
textual_basis: Socrates poses the dilemma at 459e whether the learner must already know justice and injustice or whether Gorgias will teach them. Gorgias replies at 460a that the student will learn these things from him. Socrates then confirms at 460a that anyone made into a rhetorician by Gorgias must know the just and unjust, either beforehand or later from him.
limits: This observation records Gorgias's concession that rhetoric instruction includes moral knowledge; it does not assess whether the concession is sincere or strategically motivated.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 460b Socrates draws a series of craft analogies -- carpentry, music, medicine -- to argue that whoever has learned a craft is the kind of person that craft produces, then applies the same reasoning to conclude that whoever has learned justice is just and does just things.
textual_basis: "Socrates asks whether the learner of carpentry is a carpenter, of music a musician, of medicine a doctor, and Gorgias assents to each. Socrates then extends the pattern: by the same reasoning, the learner of justice is just, and the just person does just things, with Gorgias assenting."
limits: The observation records the analogy structure and its target; it does not evaluate whether the analogy between technical crafts and justice is valid or whether Gorgias recognizes a problem with it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: From 481a to 481b Socrates restricts the usefulness of rhetoric to cases involving someone who is to do wrong, especially by helping a wrongdoer avoid trial, penalty, repayment, or death.
textual_basis: The passage lists actions to prevent an enemy from giving justice or appearing before a judge and then states at 481b that rhetoric seems useful for such things, while its need is not great for one not intending to do wrong.
limits: This records Socrates' stated delimitation of rhetoric's utility in this argument; it does not by itself describe all possible uses of rhetoric elsewhere in the dialogue.

```

```yaml
observation: At 483b-483d Callicles contrasts what is called unjust by law with what nature itself shows as just, using the same issue of seeking or having more as the point of opposition.
textual_basis: The passage first attributes the making of laws and praise or blame to the weak and the many at 483b-483c, then says that by law seeking more than the many is called unjust and shameful, while nature shows it to be just for the better or stronger to have more at 483c-483d.
limits: The record notes an explicit argumentative contrast in Callicles' speech; it does not determine whether Socrates or the dialogue endorses either side of the contrast.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The exact cited Greek interval explicitly supplies this attributed proposition.
```
```yaml
limits: This observation records only the exact source attribution; it does not infer endorsement, truth, absence, counterevidence, or a general doctrine.
```
